Q1: What is Git and how does it differ from other version control systems?

Direct answer: Git is a distributed version control system (DVCS) that tracks changes in source code. Unlike centralized systems, every developer has a full copy of the entire repository history locally.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to verify you understand the fundamental architecture of Git and can articulate why distributed version control is superior to older centralized systems. This demonstrates your foundational knowledge and ability to explain technical concepts clearly.

Breaking it down:
- Version Control System (VCS): A tool that manages changes to files over time, allowing you to track modifications, revert to previous states, and collaborate with others.
- Distributed vs Centralized: In centralized systems (like SVN), there's a single central server storing all versions. In distributed systems (like Git), every developer has a full copy of the repository, including its complete history.
- Key Git features: Branching and merging are lightweight and fast; every clone is a full backup; commits are identified by SHA-1 hashes; it uses a staging area (index) for crafting commits.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Git is a distributed version control system. Unlike centralized systems like SVN, where you have a single central server and everyone checks code in and out from that server, Git gives every developer a complete copy of the entire repository, including the full history. This means you can work offline, branch and merge very quickly and easily, and you're never dependent on a server being available. Git also uses a staging area where you can prepare your commits, and it identifies commits using cryptographic SHA-1 hashes, which ensures integrity."

Example:
git init
git add file.txt
git commit -m "Initial commit"

Real-world use case: In a typical software team, developers work on features in separate branches, commit changes locally, and then push to a shared remote repository. Git's distributed nature allows developers to work offline on trains or planes, commit as often as they want, and sync when back online.

Common mistakes:
- Confusing Git with GitHub (Git is the tool, GitHub is a hosting service)
- Thinking Git stores entire file copies (it stores snapshots)
- Not understanding the staging area concept

Interview tip: Mention that Git stores data as a series of snapshots, not file differences. This shows deeper understanding of Git's internal architecture and impresses interviewers.

---

Q2: What is the difference between Git and GitHub?

Direct answer: Git is a version control system (the tool), while GitHub is a cloud-based hosting service that provides a web interface and collaboration features for Git repositories.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to ensure you understand the distinction between the tool and the platform. Many candidates blur this line, and clarifying it demonstrates practical experience.

Breaking it down:
- Git: Command-line tool installed locally, handles version control operations (commit, branch, merge, rebase)
- GitHub: Web platform that hosts Git repositories and adds features like pull requests, issue tracking, project boards, CI/CD integration, code review tools
- Relationship: GitHub uses Git as its underlying technology. You can use Git without GitHub, and you can use GitHub without Git (though the web interface is limited)

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Git is the actual version control system - it's the command-line tool that tracks changes and manages your repository locally. GitHub, on the other hand, is a hosting service that provides a web interface for Git repositories. Think of Git as the engine and GitHub as the car - you can have the engine without the car, but the car needs the engine to work. GitHub adds collaboration features like pull requests, code reviews, issue tracking, and CI/CD pipelines that aren't part of Git itself."

Example:
# These are Git commands that work anywhere
git init
git add .
git commit -m "Message"
# This is GitHub-specific (requires account)
git remote add origin https://github.com/username/repo.git
git push -u origin main

Real-world use case: Companies use GitHub Enterprise or GitLab to host private repositories with access control, code review workflows, and automated CI/CD pipelines. The same Git commands work whether you're pushing to GitHub, GitLab, Bitbucket, or a self-hosted server.

Common mistakes:
- Saying "GitHub is Git" or using the terms interchangeably
- Not knowing that alternatives like GitLab, Bitbucket, and Azure DevOps exist
- Assuming GitHub is required to use Git

Interview tip: Mention that you've used both GitHub and other platforms (GitLab, Bitbucket). This shows breadth of experience.

---

Q3: What is a Git repository?

Direct answer: A Git repository is a directory that contains all project files and the `.git` subdirectory, which stores the complete version history, metadata, and configuration for your project.

Why this is asked: This tests your understanding of Git's fundamental structure. Knowing what a repository is and what it contains shows you understand how Git operates at a basic level.

Breaking it down:
- Working directory: The files you're currently editing
- .git folder: Hidden directory containing all Git metadata, including commits, branches, refs, and configuration
- Repository types: Local (on your machine) and remote (on a server)
- Contents: The repository stores objects (blobs, trees, commits, tags) compressed and indexed for efficient operations

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A Git repository is essentially a directory that Git is tracking. When I run `git init` in a folder, Git creates a hidden `.git` directory inside that folder. This `.git` directory contains everything Git needs to manage my project - all the commits, branches, tags, and configuration. The rest of the directory is my working tree, where I edit files. So when we say 'repository,' we're talking about the entire thing - both the `.git` folder and my working files."

Example:
# Create a new repository
mkdir my-project
cd my-project
git init
# Now a .git folder exists
ls -la   # shows .git directory

Real-world use case: When you start a new project, you initialize a repository locally. When you clone from GitHub, you get a complete repository with history. Each developer on a team has their own local repository.

Common mistakes:
- Thinking the repository is only the remote (like GitHub)
- Not knowing that `.git` is where all history lives
- Forgetting to initialize a repo before committing

Interview tip: Emphasize that the `.git` folder is the "brain" of the repository – without it, it's just a normal folder. If you delete it, you lose version history (unless you have a remote).

---

Q4: What is the difference between a local and a remote repository?

Direct answer: A local repository is the Git repository stored on your own computer, where you perform commits, branching, and merging. A remote repository is a copy hosted on a server or another machine, used for collaboration and backup.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to confirm you understand the distributed nature of Git and how teams synchronize work. This is essential for daily collaborative work.

Breaking it down:
- Local: Exists in your `.git` folder; you can commit, branch, merge, and view history entirely offline.
- Remote: Typically on GitHub, GitLab, or a server; you push your local commits to it and pull others' commits from it.
- Interaction: You use `git push` to send changes to a remote, `git pull` or `git fetch` to receive changes from it.
- Multiple remotes: You can have several remotes (e.g., `origin`, `upstream`).

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A local repository is the copy of the repository that lives on my own machine. I do all my work there – I commit changes, create branches, and merge. The remote repository is a copy that lives on a server, like GitHub. I use the remote to share my work with teammates and to get their updates. I push my commits to the remote and pull from it. The key point is that both are full Git repositories – the remote isn't a 'master' copy; it's just a shared point of sync."

Example:
# Add a remote
git remote add origin https://github.com/user/repo.git
# Push local commits to remote
git push origin main
# Pull updates from remote
git pull origin main

Real-world use case: In a team, each developer has their own local repository. They work on features, commit locally, and then push to a shared remote (e.g., `origin/main`). CI/CD systems often monitor the remote for new commits.

Common mistakes:
- Thinking the remote is the "source of truth" and local is just temporary – they are equal, but the remote is often the integration point.
- Not pulling before pushing, leading to merge conflicts.
- Forgetting to set up a remote or using the wrong remote name.

Interview tip: Clarify that you can have multiple remotes (e.g., `origin` for your fork and `upstream` for the original repo). This shows you're familiar with open-source contribution workflows.

---

Q5: What is a commit in Git?

Direct answer: A commit is a snapshot of your repository at a specific point in time, containing all changes you've staged, along with a commit message, author information, a timestamp, and a reference to its parent commit(s).

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to assess your understanding of Git's fundamental unit of work. Knowing what a commit contains and how it's created is core to using Git effectively.

Breaking it down:
- Snapshot: Not a diff; Git captures the entire state of all tracked files at that moment.
- Staged changes: Only changes added with `git add` become part of the commit.
- Metadata: Commit includes author, committer, date, message, and a unique SHA-1 hash.
- Parent: Each commit (except the first) points to the previous commit(s), forming a linked history.
- Commit message: Should be descriptive of the changes.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A commit is a record of changes in the repository. It's like a save point in a game – I capture the entire state of my project at that moment. When I make a commit, I tell Git to take all the changes I've staged with `git add` and package them into a permanent snapshot. Each commit gets a unique identifier, a message describing what I changed, and it links to the previous commit so we can trace history. Commits are immutable – once created, they can't be changed (though we can rewrite history with advanced commands)."

Example:
# Stage changes
git add file1.txt file2.txt
# Commit with message
git commit -m "Add validation logic to user form"
# View the commit
git show HEAD

Real-world use case: Developers commit frequently to save progress, often after completing a small logical unit of work. Commits are pushed to remote to share with the team and are used for code review via pull requests.

Common mistakes:
- Committing unrelated changes in one commit (should be atomic).
- Writing vague commit messages like "fix".
- Forgetting to stage changes before committing (then commit is empty).
- Committing large binary files (makes repo slow).

Interview tip: Mention that you follow conventional commit messages (e.g., feat:, fix:, docs:) and atomic commits. This demonstrates good engineering hygiene.

---

Q6: How do you create a new Git repository?

Direct answer: You create a new Git repository using either `git init` to initialize an empty repository in an existing directory, or `git clone` to copy an existing remote repository to your local machine.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to know you have the basic workflow down – how to start a project with Git, whether from scratch or from an existing source.

Breaking it down:
- `git init`: Creates a new `.git` subdirectory in the current folder, turning it into a repository. No commits yet.
- `git clone <url>`: Copies an existing remote repository to your local machine, including all history and branches, and sets up the remote automatically.
- `git clone --depth 1`: Shallow clone (saves bandwidth/time).
- After init, you need to add files and make the first commit.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To create a new repository, I use `git init` if I'm starting a brand new project from scratch. I navigate to the project folder, run `git init`, then add files and make the first commit. If I'm joining an existing project, I use `git clone` with the repository URL, which copies everything to my local machine. Cloning also automatically sets up the remote called 'origin' so I can push and pull later."

Example:
# From scratch
mkdir my-app
cd my-app
git init
echo "# My App" > README.md
git add README.md
git commit -m "Initial commit"
# Clone existing
git clone https://github.com/username/existing-repo.git
cd existing-repo

Real-world use case: When starting a new project, you init locally, then later add a remote on GitHub and push. When contributing to open source, you fork and clone.

Common mistakes:
- Running `git init` inside an already initialized repo (shows a warning).
- Forgetting to add a remote after init (then can't push).
- Not making an initial commit, then trying to push (push fails).

Interview tip: Emphasize that `git init` is only local; you need to create a remote on GitHub/GitLab separately and then add it with `git remote add`. Also mention `git clone` is the most common way for team projects.

---

Q7: What is a branch in Git?

Direct answer: A branch is a movable pointer to a specific commit, allowing you to isolate development work. The default branch is usually `main` (or `master`), and branches enable parallel development without interfering with the main codebase.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to check your understanding of branching, which is central to Git's collaboration model. They want to see if you know how to use branches to manage features, fixes, and releases.

Breaking it down:
- Pointer: A branch is just a reference (a file with a SHA-1 hash) that points to a commit.
- HEAD: Special pointer indicating which branch you're currently on.
- Creating a branch: `git branch <name>` or `git checkout -b <name>` / `git switch -c <name>`.
- Switching: `git checkout <name>` or `git switch <name>`.
- Merging: Combine work from different branches.
- Branch isolation: Changes in one branch don't affect others until you merge.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A branch in Git is essentially a lightweight movable pointer to a commit. When I create a new branch, I'm creating a new line of development. I use branches to work on new features, bug fixes, or experiments without disturbing the main codebase. For example, I might create a `feature/login` branch from `main`, work on it, and then merge it back. Branches are extremely cheap and fast in Git, so we create many of them."

Example:
# Create and switch to a new branch
git checkout -b feature/add-login
# Or using switch (newer)
git switch -c feature/add-login
# List branches
git branch
# Switch back to main
git checkout main

Real-world use case: Teams use feature branches (e.g., `feature/user-auth`), bugfix branches (`bugfix/issue-123`), and release branches. Pull requests are opened from these branches to main.

Common mistakes:
- Thinking branches are like folders (they're just pointers).
- Forgetting to switch branches before making changes (changes go to current branch).
- Not pulling latest changes before merging, causing conflicts.
- Not deleting merged branches (clutter).

Interview tip: Mention branch naming conventions (e.g., `feature/`, `hotfix/`, `chore/`) and that you follow Git Flow or GitHub Flow. This shows real-world practice.

---

Q8: How do you create a new branch?

Direct answer: You can create a new branch using `git branch <branch-name>` to create it without switching, or `git checkout -b <branch-name>` (or `git switch -c <branch-name>`) to create and switch to it immediately.

Why this is asked: This is a basic operation, but the interviewer wants to confirm you know the common commands and the difference between creating and switching. It's a fundamental skill.

Breaking it down:
- `git branch <name>`: Creates a new branch pointing to the current commit; you remain on the current branch.
- `git checkout -b <name>`: Creates and switches to the new branch in one step (older syntax).
- `git switch -c <name>`: Newer, more intuitive command (Git 2.23+).
- You can also create a branch from a specific commit: `git branch <name> <commit-hash>`.
- You can create from a remote branch: `git checkout -b <local-name> origin/<remote-branch>`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To create a new branch, I use `git branch feature-name` if I just want to create it but stay where I am. More often, I use `git checkout -b feature-name` because it creates the branch and switches to it immediately. In newer versions of Git, I use `git switch -c feature-name`, which is more explicit. I usually create branches from the main branch or from the branch I want to base my work on."

Example:
# Create but don't switch
git branch new-feature
# Create and switch
git checkout -b new-feature
# Or with switch
git switch -c new-feature
# Create from specific commit
git branch hotfix abc123

Real-world use case: A developer starts a new feature by creating a branch from `main`, does their work, commits, and opens a pull request.

Common mistakes:
- Not specifying the base (creates from current branch, which might be wrong).
- Using `git branch` without `-b` and forgetting to switch.
- Creating a branch with a name that conflicts with an existing one.
- Creating a branch without pulling latest changes first.

Interview tip: Mention that you always `git pull` (or `git fetch` and `git merge`) on the base branch before creating a new branch to avoid divergence. Also mention `git branch -d` to delete after merging.

---

Q9: What is the `HEAD` in Git?

Direct answer: `HEAD` is a special pointer in Git that references the current commit you are on. In most cases, it points to the branch you're currently on, which in turn points to the latest commit on that branch.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of Git's internal state. Knowing what HEAD is helps you understand checkout, reset, and detached HEAD states.

Breaking it down:
- `HEAD` is a file in `.git/HEAD` that usually contains a reference to a branch (e.g., `ref: refs/heads/main`).
- When you `git checkout <branch>`, HEAD updates to point to that branch.
- Detached HEAD: When HEAD points directly to a commit hash, not a branch – you're not on any branch.
- You can view where HEAD points with `git symbolic-ref HEAD` or `git rev-parse HEAD`.
- The `~` and `^` syntax uses HEAD: `HEAD~1` means the parent of the current commit.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"HEAD is a reference to the current commit that I'm working on. It's essentially where I am in the repository history. Normally, HEAD points to a branch name, and that branch points to the latest commit. So when I'm on the `main` branch, HEAD points to `main`, and `main` points to the latest commit. If I checkout a commit directly, HEAD points to that commit – that's a detached HEAD state, which means I'm not on any branch and any new commits I make won't belong to a branch unless I create one."

Example:
# See where HEAD points
cat .git/HEAD   # usually ref: refs/heads/main
# Move HEAD one commit back (relative)
git checkout HEAD~1
# Detached HEAD state
git checkout abc123   # detached

Real-world use case: When you use `git reset`, you move HEAD (and the branch) to a different commit. When you use `git checkout` on a branch, HEAD updates to point to that branch.

Common mistakes:
- Thinking HEAD always points to the latest commit globally (it points to your current position).
- Not realizing you're in detached HEAD and making commits that become orphaned.
- Confusing HEAD with origin/main.

Interview tip: Emphasize that in normal branch state, HEAD is a symbolic reference to a branch, not directly to a commit. This shows deeper understanding. Also mention that `git switch` and `git checkout` handle HEAD updates.

---

Q10: How do you check the status of your Git repository?

Direct answer: You check the status using `git status`, which shows the state of the working directory and staging area, including untracked files, changes to be committed, and changes not staged for commit.

Why this is asked: This is the most frequently used Git command. The interviewer wants to ensure you use it and understand its output to manage your workflow.

Breaking it down:
- `git status` gives a summary of:
  - Current branch and whether it's ahead/behind remote.
  - Files in staging area (changes to be committed).
  - Files modified but not staged (changes not staged for commit).
  - Untracked files.
  - Merge/rebase state if in conflict.
- Short status: `git status -s` for condensed output.
- It's safe and doesn't change anything.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"I use `git status` constantly to see what's going on in my repository. It tells me which branch I'm on, whether I have changes staged, changes that are modified but not staged, and any untracked files. It also shows if I'm ahead or behind the remote branch. This helps me decide my next step – whether to add files, commit, or pull. It's my primary sanity check."

Example:
$ git status
On branch main
Your branch is up to date with 'origin/main'.
Changes to be committed:
  (use "git restore --staged <file>..." to unstage)
    modified:   README.md
Changes not staged for commit:
  (use "git add <file>..." to update what will be committed)
  (use "git restore <file>..." to discard changes in working directory)
    modified:   src/app.js
Untracked files:
  (use "git add <file>..." to include in what will be committed)
    newfile.txt

Real-world use case: Before committing, you run `git status` to review what will be included. After adding, you run it again to confirm staging. When pulling, you check status to see if you have uncommitted changes.

Common mistakes:
- Not running status before committing, leading to accidental commits of unwanted files.
- Ignoring the "changes not staged" section and thinking everything is staged.
- Misinterpreting "untracked files" as tracked; need to add them.

Interview tip: Mention that `git status` is your friend; use it often. Also mention `git diff` to see actual changes, which complements status.

---

Q11: What does `git add` do?

Direct answer: `git add` moves changes from the working directory to the staging area (index), marking them to be included in the next commit. It doesn't affect the repository history.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to confirm you understand the staging area concept, which is unique to Git and crucial for crafting commits.

Breaking it down:
- Staging area (index): A file that holds the list of changes that will go into the next commit.
- `git add <file>`: Adds specific files or patterns to the staging area.
- `git add .` or `git add -A`: Adds all changes (including deletions) in the current directory.
- `git add -p`: Interactive patching, allows you to stage parts of a file.
- It's like "selecting" changes for the next snapshot.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`git add` stages changes. It takes the modifications I've made in my working directory and prepares them for the next commit. I can stage specific files, or use `git add .` to stage everything. The key is that staging lets me be selective – I can decide exactly what goes into each commit, which helps keep commits atomic and clean. It's like putting items in a shopping cart before checking out."

Example:
# Stage a single file
git add README.md
# Stage all changes in current directory
git add .
# Stage all changes including deletions (global)
git add -A
# Interactive staging
git add -p

Real-world use case: During development, you might have multiple files changed. You stage related ones together to form a logical commit. If you accidentally stage something, you can unstage with `git restore --staged`.

Common mistakes:
- Not using `git add .` correctly (it stages from current directory; might miss changes in subdirectories if not careful).
- Forgetting to add new files (they remain untracked and won't be committed).
- Using `git add *` vs `git add .` – wildcard may miss hidden files.

Interview tip: Emphasize that you use `git add -p` to review and split changes into clean commits. This shows high-level Git proficiency.

---

Q12: What does `git commit` do?

Direct answer: `git commit` takes all changes currently staged in the index, creates a new snapshot (commit) with those changes, and attaches a commit message. It also updates the current branch to point to this new commit.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to know you understand the commit workflow and the importance of commit messages. It's the core action of saving history.

Breaking it down:
- Commit creates a new commit object with:
  - Snapshot of the staged content
  - Author and committer info
  - Timestamp
  - Commit message
  - Parent commit(s)
- `git commit -m "message"`: Inline message.
- `git commit -a`: Automatically stages all tracked changes (skips staging area).
- `git commit --amend`: Modify the last commit.
- After commit, the branch pointer moves to the new commit.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`git commit` saves the staged changes as a permanent snapshot in the repository. I use it to record what I've done, and I always include a clear message explaining what the commit does. The commit becomes part of the history, and my current branch moves forward to this new commit. I can use `-m` to give a message directly, or omit it to open an editor for a more detailed message. Commits are immutable, so once made, they are part of history."

Example:
# Simple commit
git add file.txt
git commit -m "Add user authentication logic"
# Commit all tracked changes (skip staging)
git commit -a -m "Fix typo in config"
# Amend the last commit (change message or add forgotten files)
git add forgotten-file.txt
git commit --amend -m "Update authentication logic with error handling"

Real-world use case: After completing a small feature or fix, you stage related changes and commit with a descriptive message. You may commit multiple times a day, each representing a logical unit.

Common mistakes:
- Committing without a message (opens editor; might abort).
- Using `-m` with too short or unhelpful messages.
- Committing with `-a` accidentally including changes you didn't want.
- Not committing often enough, leading to large, messy commits.

Interview tip: Mention you write commit messages in the imperative mood ("Add feature" not "Added feature") and follow the 50/72 rule (50 chars for summary, 72 for body). This shows professionalism.

---

Q13: How do you view the commit history?

Direct answer: Use `git log` to view the commit history, displaying the commits in reverse chronological order with details like commit hash, author, date, and commit message.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to know if you can navigate the project history, which is essential for debugging, reviewing changes, and understanding project evolution.

Breaking it down:
- `git log`: Shows full history with details.
- `git log --oneline`: Compact view (one line per commit).
- `git log -p`: Shows patch (diff) for each commit.
- `git log --graph`: Shows ASCII graph of branching structure.
- `git log --author="name"`: Filter by author.
- `git log --since=2.days`: Filter by date.
- `git log <file>`: Show history of a specific file.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To view commit history, I use `git log`. This command shows me all the commits in the repository in reverse chronological order, with the latest commit at the top. I often use `git log --oneline` for a concise view, and `git log --graph` to see branching visually. I can also filter by author or time, and restrict to a specific file to see its history. It's essential for understanding what changes were made and by whom."

Example:
# Full history
git log
# Compact
git log --oneline
# Graph with branches
git log --oneline --graph --decorate --all
# History of a file
git log -- README.md
# Last 5 commits
git log -5

Real-world use case: When debugging, you use `git log` to find which commit introduced a bug. When reviewing a pull request, you might check the commit history to see how a feature was built.

Common mistakes:
- Using `git log` without any formatting and getting overwhelmed.
- Not knowing about `git log --oneline` and `git log --graph`.
- Forgetting to use `git log` for files to see rename history.

Interview tip: Mention `git log -p` to show diffs, and `git blame` is related but for file annotations. Also mention `git reflog` to recover lost commits.

---

Q14: What is the difference between `git log` and `git reflog`?

Direct answer: `git log` shows the public commit history (reachable commits), while `git reflog` shows the local history of where your branch pointers have moved, including commits that are no longer reachable (like after a reset or rebase).

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know how to recover lost commits and understand the difference between public history and local reference changes.

Breaking it down:
- `git log`: Displays commits that are reachable from the current HEAD (or specified branch). It's the "official" history.
- `git reflog`: Records all updates to branch pointers and HEAD in your local repository. It's a local-only log that tracks movements like switching branches, resetting, merging, rebasing.
- Reflog entries have a timestamp and a description (e.g., "commit", "reset", "checkout").
- Reflog is not shared with remotes; it's only for your local safety.
- You can use `git reflog` to find a commit hash and then `git reset` or `git checkout` to recover lost work.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The main difference is that `git log` shows the commit history of my current branch – the official, permanent record. `git reflog` shows the history of where my HEAD and branch pointers have been, including moves that might not be visible in `git log`. For example, if I do a hard reset, `git log` will no longer show the commits that were discarded, but `git reflog` will still show them until they expire. This is extremely useful for recovering lost commits, like when I accidentally reset and lose work."

Example:
# View reflog
git reflog
# Example output:
# abc123 HEAD@{0}: commit: Added new feature
# def456 HEAD@{1}: reset: moving to def456
# 7890ab HEAD@{2}: commit: Updated README
# Recover lost commit
git checkout abc123   # to go to that commit
# or create a branch there
git branch recover-branch abc123

Real-world use case: After a mistaken `git reset --hard`, you can use `git reflog` to find the hash of the commit before the reset and restore it. Reflog is your emergency recovery tool.

Common mistakes:
- Thinking `git reflog` shows the same as `git log`.
- Not using `git reflog` to recover lost commits; instead panicking.
- Forgetting that reflog entries expire after 90 days (default) or are garbage-collected.

Interview tip: Mention that reflog is local and not shared; if you push after a reset, recovering might be harder. Always use `git reflog` before rewriting history.

---

Q15: How do you undo changes in Git?

Direct answer: Undoing changes in Git depends on the state of the changes: you can use `git restore` for unstaged changes, `git reset` to unstage or reset to a previous commit, `git revert` to create a new commit that undoes a previous commit, or `git commit --amend` to modify the last commit.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of Git's various undo mechanisms and when to use each. This is a common interview question because developers frequently need to correct mistakes.

Breaking it down:
- Unstaged changes (working directory): `git restore <file>` (or `git checkout -- <file>` in older Git) discards changes.
- Staged changes (not committed): `git restore --staged <file>` to unstage, then `git restore` to discard.
- Last commit (not pushed): `git commit --amend` to change message or add changes.
- Pushed commit: `git revert <hash>` creates a new commit that reverses the changes (safe).
- Reset (local only): `git reset` moves the branch pointer to a previous commit; can be `--soft` (keep changes staged), `--mixed` (keep changes unstaged), `--hard` (discard changes).
- Interactive rebase to edit older commits.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"How I undo changes depends on where the changes are. If they're in my working directory and I haven't staged them, I use `git restore <file>` to discard them. If they're staged but not committed, I unstage them with `git restore --staged`. For the last commit, if I haven't pushed, I can use `git commit --amend` to modify it. For a commit that's already pushed, I use `git revert` because it's safe and keeps history linear. If I need to rewrite local history, I use `git reset`, but I'm cautious with `--hard` because it discards changes permanently."

Example:
# Discard unstaged changes
git restore file.txt
# Unstage a file
git restore --staged file.txt
# Amend last commit
git add forgotten-file.txt
git commit --amend
# Revert a pushed commit
git revert abc123
# Reset to a previous commit (keep changes)
git reset --soft HEAD~1   # changes become staged
git reset --mixed HEAD~1  # changes become unstaged
git reset --hard HEAD~1   # discard changes (dangerous)

Real-world use case: A developer accidentally includes a debug console.log in a commit, they amend it. Or they realize a commit broke the build and revert it. Or they want to discard local experimental changes before switching branches.

Common mistakes:
- Using `git reset --hard` on a shared branch (rewrites history and causes issues for others).
- Forgetting that `git revert` is the safe undo for pushed commits.
- Using `git restore` before checking if you need to commit changes first.
- Not using `git stash` to save work temporarily instead of discarding.

Interview tip: Emphasize that for collaborative work, use `git revert` or `git commit --amend` (if local) and avoid `git reset` on shared branches. Mention `git reflog` as a safety net for hard resets.

---

Q16: What is the difference between `git reset` and `git revert`?

Direct answer: `git reset` moves the branch pointer and changes the commit history locally, while `git revert` creates a new commit that undoes the changes of a previous commit, preserving history. Reset changes history, revert adds history.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to ensure you know the safe way to undo commits in a shared environment and the dangers of rewriting history.

Breaking it down:
- `git revert <commit>`: Creates a new commit that applies the inverse of the changes in the specified commit. It's a safe operation for public branches because it doesn't alter existing history.
- `git reset <commit>`: Moves the current branch pointer to the specified commit, optionally updating the index and working tree. It rewrites history locally and can cause problems when pushed to a shared branch.
- Reset modes: `--soft` (keep changes staged), `--mixed` (keep changes unstaged), `--hard` (discard changes).
- Revert can be used on any commit, even if it's not the latest.
- Use revert for public commits, reset for local, uncommitted, or private branches.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The key difference is that `git revert` is a safe undo for commits that have already been shared with others. It adds a new commit that reverses the changes, so the history is intact and everyone's history stays consistent. `git reset`, on the other hand, moves the branch pointer backward and effectively removes commits from the history. This is dangerous on a shared branch because it diverges the history and can cause conflicts for others. I use reset for local changes before they're pushed, or for private branches, but I never reset a branch that others have based work on."

Example:
# Revert a commit (safe)
git revert abc123   # creates a new commit that undoes abc123
# Reset to previous commit (dangerous if shared)
git reset --hard HEAD~1   # removes the last commit locally

Real-world use case: If a buggy commit is deployed, you revert it to fix the issue quickly. If you're working on a feature branch and want to discard the last commit because you realized it's wrong, you can reset.

Common mistakes:
- Using `git reset` on a branch that's already pushed, then forcing push, causing chaos.
- Using `git revert` when you wanted to completely remove a commit (but revert is better).
- Not understanding that revert may cause merge conflicts if files changed since then.

Interview tip: Emphasize that you always use revert for public history, and only reset for your local unshared branches. This demonstrates safe collaboration habits.

---

Q17: How do you delete a file from Git?

Direct answer: To delete a file from Git, you use `git rm <file>` which removes the file from both the working directory and the staging area, and then you commit the deletion. If you want to stop tracking a file but keep it on disk, use `git rm --cached <file>`.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know how to properly remove files from version control, including the distinction between deleting and untracking.

Breaking it down:
- `git rm <file>`: Removes the file from the working tree and stages the deletion. The file will be deleted from the filesystem and will not be tracked.
- `git rm --cached <file>`: Removes the file from Git's index (staging area) but leaves it in the working directory. This untracks the file without deleting it from disk.
- After `git rm`, you must commit to finalize the removal.
- For directories: `git rm -r <dir>`.
- To stop tracking files that were already committed but now should be ignored, use `git rm --cached` and add to `.gitignore`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To delete a file from Git, I use `git rm`. This stages the deletion and also removes the file from my working directory. If I want to keep the file locally but stop tracking it, I use `git rm --cached` instead. After that, I commit the change. This is common when I want to remove a sensitive file or when I move a file to `.gitignore`."

Example:
# Delete and remove from disk
git rm old-file.txt
git commit -m "Remove old-file.txt"
# Untrack but keep on disk
git rm --cached config.yml
# Add to .gitignore to prevent re-adding
echo "config.yml" >> .gitignore
git add .gitignore
git commit -m "Stop tracking config.yml"

Real-world use case: You accidentally committed a log file or a secret. You use `git rm --cached` and add to `.gitignore` to prevent future commits. For actual sensitive data, you'd also use `git filter-repo` to remove from history.

Common mistakes:
- Using `rm` (Unix command) then forgetting to stage deletion with `git rm`; Git still sees the file as deleted but not staged.
- Using `git rm` on a file you intended to keep but just untrack.
- Not updating `.gitignore` after untracking.
- Forgetting that the file remains in history; `git rm --cached` only stops future tracking.

Interview tip: Mention that `git rm --cached` is often used with `.gitignore` to remove files that were accidentally tracked. Also mention `git filter-repo` for removing sensitive data from history (though that's more advanced).

---

Q18: What is a `.gitignore` file?

Direct answer: A `.gitignore` file is a text file that tells Git which files or directories to ignore and not track in the repository. It's used to exclude build artifacts, logs, dependencies, secrets, and other non-versioned files.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to confirm you know how to keep repositories clean and prevent accidental commits of unnecessary or sensitive files.

Breaking it down:
- `.gitignore` is placed in the repository root (or any subdirectory) and contains patterns.
- Patterns can be file names, directories, wildcards (`*`), and negation (`!`).
- Already tracked files are not affected; you must untrack them separately.
- You can have multiple `.gitignore` files in subdirectories.
- Global `.gitignore` can be set with `git config --global core.excludesfile`.
- Common patterns: `*.log`, `node_modules/`, `.env`, `target/`, `*.class`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A `.gitignore` file is a list of patterns that Git should ignore. I put it in the root of my repository to prevent temporary files, build outputs, or environment-specific files from being committed. For example, I ignore `node_modules` for Node projects, `*.pyc` for Python, and `.env` for environment variables. This keeps the repository clean and avoids cluttering history with generated files. It's also important for security to avoid committing secrets."

Example:
# .gitignore file
# Ignore logs
*.log
# Ignore dependency directories
node_modules/
vendor/
# Ignore environment files
.env
# Ignore build output
dist/
build/
# But track a specific file in dist
!dist/important.json

Real-world use case: Every project has a `.gitignore` tailored to its tech stack. You can get templates for various languages (e.g., from gitignore.io). It's the first file created after `git init`.

Common mistakes:
- Forgetting to add `.gitignore` before committing large files.
- Adding `.gitignore` after files are already tracked (they won't be ignored; need to untrack).
- Using patterns that don't match correctly (e.g., trailing slashes).
- Not using a global gitignore for OS-specific files (e.g., .DS_Store).

Interview tip: Mention that you always set up `.gitignore` early, use templates, and check with `git status` to confirm ignored files are not shown. Also mention that you can test patterns with `git check-ignore`.

---

Q19: How do you clone a repository?

Direct answer: You clone a repository using `git clone <repository-url>`, which creates a local copy of a remote repository, including all files, branches, and history, and sets up the remote `origin` automatically.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to ensure you know the basic command to get a project started, which is a fundamental operation.

Breaking it down:
- `git clone <url>`: Downloads the entire repository and checks out the default branch (usually `main`) into a new directory.
- The directory name defaults to the repository name; you can specify a different name: `git clone <url> my-folder`.
- You can clone with specific options: `--depth 1` for shallow clone, `--branch <branch>` to clone a specific branch.
- Cloning sets up the remote as `origin`.
- It also creates a local branch tracking the remote branch.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To clone a repository, I use `git clone` followed by the URL. This creates a local copy of the entire repository, including all commits and branches. I can also provide a directory name if I don't want the default. Cloning sets up the remote 'origin' so I can push and pull. If I only need the latest snapshot and don't need full history, I use `--depth 1` to speed up the clone."

Example:
# Clone HTTPS
git clone https://github.com/user/repo.git
# Clone SSH
git clone git@github.com:user/repo.git
# Clone into a specific directory
git clone https://github.com/user/repo.git my-project
# Shallow clone
git clone --depth 1 https://github.com/user/repo.git
# Clone a specific branch
git clone --branch develop https://github.com/user/repo.git

Real-world use case: When joining a new project, you clone the repository to your local machine. You might also fork a repository on GitHub and then clone your fork.

Common mistakes:
- Using HTTP URL when you have SSH keys set up (can't push without credentials).
- Cloning a large repository with full history when you only need latest (slow).
- Forgetting to specify a branch and getting the default.
- Cloning into an existing directory (fails unless it's empty).

Interview tip: Mention that you often use `--depth 1` for CI pipelines to speed up builds. Also mention you can clone with `--recurse-submodules` if the repo has submodules.

---

Q20: What is the difference between `git clone` and `git fork`?

Direct answer: `git clone` is a Git command that creates a local copy of a remote repository on your machine. `Fork` is a GitHub (or Git hosting) operation that creates a copy of a repository on the remote server under your account.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to clarify the distinction between local copying and remote server-level copying, especially for open-source contribution workflows.

Breaking it down:
- `git clone`: Local operation; copies the repository to your computer, with remote tracking.
- `Fork`: Server-side operation; duplicates a repository on the hosting platform (e.g., GitHub) under your own namespace.
- After forking, you typically clone your fork locally to make changes.
- Forking is used to contribute to projects where you don't have write access – you fork, clone, make changes, and open a pull request from your fork to the original.
- Cloning is used for any repository you want to work on locally.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`git clone` is a command I run locally to download a repository to my computer. A fork is something I do on the GitHub website – it creates a copy of someone else's repository under my own GitHub account. After forking, I clone my fork to work on it locally. Forking is mainly used when I want to contribute to an open-source project where I don't have write access – I make changes in my fork and then open a pull request to the original repo."

Example:
# Clone a repository (local copy)
git clone https://github.com/upstream/project.git
# Fork on GitHub: click the Fork button
# Then clone your fork
git clone https://github.com/myusername/project.git
# Add upstream remote to sync with original
git remote add upstream https://github.com/upstream/project.git

Real-world use case: Open-source contribution: fork the repo, clone your fork, create a branch, make changes, push to your fork, and open a pull request to the original.

Common mistakes:
- Thinking `fork` is a Git command (it's a platform feature).
- Cloning the original repo directly and trying to push (permission denied).
- Forgetting to add the upstream remote after forking to keep in sync.
- Not understanding the difference between origin and upstream.

Interview tip: Emphasize that you use forks for contribution workflows and that you keep your fork in sync via `git fetch upstream` and `git merge upstream/main` or rebase.

---

Q21: What is a remote in Git?

Direct answer: A remote is a reference to a version of your repository that is stored on a server or another machine. It's essentially a bookmark for a remote repository, typically named `origin` by default.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to confirm you understand the collaborative aspect of Git and how repositories are linked.

Breaking it down:
- Remote is a named reference (e.g., `origin`, `upstream`) to a URL.
- You can have multiple remotes (e.g., `origin` for your fork, `upstream` for the original).
- Operations: `git push` sends commits to a remote, `git pull` fetches and merges from it, `git fetch` just downloads.
- You can see remotes with `git remote -v`.
- Remote branches are tracked locally as `remote-name/branch-name` (e.g., `origin/main`).

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A remote in Git is simply a pointer to another copy of the repository that lives somewhere else, typically on a server like GitHub. It's a named reference that I use to sync my local work with a shared repository. By default, the remote I clone from is called `origin`. I can also add other remotes, like `upstream`, to track the original repository of a project I've forked."

Example:
# List remotes
git remote -v
# Add a remote
git remote add upstream https://github.com/original/repo.git
# Push to a remote
git push origin main
# Fetch from a remote without merging
git fetch upstream

Real-world use case: In a team, `origin` points to the shared repository. Developers push and pull from it. In open-source, you have `origin` pointing to your fork and `upstream` pointing to the original repository to sync changes.

Common mistakes:
- Not knowing the difference between remote and local branches.
- Using `git push` without specifying remote and branch (uses default).
- Forgetting to add remotes or using wrong URLs.

Interview tip: Mention that remotes are just references; `git remote show <name>` gives detailed info about a remote.

---

Q22: How do you add a remote repository?

Direct answer: You add a remote repository using `git remote add <name> <url>`, where `<name>` is a short name (like `origin`) and `<url>` is the repository URL.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to know the command to link a local repository to a remote, which is a necessary step after `git init` for collaboration.

Breaking it down:
- `git remote add <name> <url>`: Creates a new remote connection.
- Common name: `origin` for the primary remote.
- URL can be HTTPS or SSH.
- After adding, you can push: `git push -u origin main` (sets upstream).
- You can rename or remove remotes with `git remote rename` and `git remote remove`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To add a remote repository, I use `git remote add` with a name and the URL. For example, if I've just initialized a local repo and created a blank repo on GitHub, I add `origin` pointing to that GitHub URL. Then I can push my code. I also add an `upstream` remote when I'm working with forks to keep sync with the original."

Example:
# Add origin remote
git remote add origin https://github.com/user/repo.git
# Verify
git remote -v
# Push and set upstream
git push -u origin main
# Add upstream for forked repos
git remote add upstream https://github.com/original/repo.git

Real-world use case: After `git init`, you create a repo on GitHub and add it as a remote. When cloning, the remote is automatically set up. For forks, you add `upstream` to track the original.

Common mistakes:
- Using the wrong URL (HTTP vs SSH).
- Forgetting to add a remote before trying to push.
- Adding a remote with a name that already exists (use `set-url` to update).

Interview tip: Mention that `git push -u` sets the upstream branch, so future pushes can just be `git push`.

---

Q23: What does `git push` do?

Direct answer: `git push` uploads your local commits to a remote repository, updating the remote branch to match your local branch.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to confirm you understand the fundamental command for sharing code and its common usage.

Breaking it down:
- `git push <remote> <branch>`: Uploads commits to the specified remote branch.
- `git push -u <remote> <branch>`: Sets the upstream branch (tracking relation).
- `git push --force`: Overwrites remote history (dangerous).
- `git push --delete <remote> <branch>`: Deletes a remote branch.
- The remote branch must exist, or you need permission.
- If the remote has commits you don't have, push is rejected; you must pull first.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`git push` takes my local commits and sends them to a remote repository. I typically use `git push origin main` to push my main branch to the remote. The first time, I use `-u` to set the tracking relationship so I can just type `git push` afterward. If the remote branch has changes I don't have, Git will reject the push, and I need to pull first. I avoid force push because it rewrites history and can cause problems for others."

Example:
# Push to origin main
git push origin main
# Set upstream and push
git push -u origin feature/new-login
# Delete remote branch
git push origin --delete feature/old-feature

Real-world use case: After finishing a feature and committing locally, you push to the remote to open a pull request or share with the team. CI/CD often triggers on push.

Common mistakes:
- Pushing before pulling, leading to rejection.
- Using `--force` without `--force-with-lease`, overriding others' work.
- Pushing to the wrong remote or branch.
- Forgetting to commit before pushing.

Interview tip: Mention `--force-with-lease` as a safer alternative to `--force` – it checks if your remote branch is up-to-date. Always pull before pushing when working on shared branches.

---

Q24: What does `git pull` do?

Direct answer: `git pull` fetches changes from a remote repository and integrates them into your current local branch by merging (or rebasing) them.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to verify you understand how to update your local repository with others' work.

Breaking it down:
- `git pull` is essentially `git fetch` + `git merge` (or `git rebase` with `--rebase`).
- It downloads commits from the remote branch and merges them into your current branch.
- If there are conflicts, you must resolve them.
- You can specify remote and branch: `git pull origin main`.
- `git pull --rebase` uses rebase instead of merge for a linear history.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`git pull` is how I bring in changes from the remote into my local branch. It does two things: it fetches the latest commits from the remote, and then it merges those commits into my current branch. I use it regularly to keep my branch up-to-date with what my teammates have pushed. If there are conflicts, Git will warn me and I have to resolve them. Sometimes I use `git pull --rebase` to avoid merge commits and keep history cleaner."

Example:
# Simple pull
git pull origin main
# Pull with rebase
git pull --rebase origin main
# Pull from default remote of current branch
git pull

Real-world use case: Before starting work each day, you `git pull` to get the latest changes. Before pushing, you `git pull` to integrate others' commits and avoid rejections.

Common mistakes:
- Using `git pull` when you have uncommitted changes (causes conflicts or abort).
- Forgetting to specify the remote/branch and pulling from the wrong one.
- Not understanding that pull is fetch + merge and can create merge commits.
- Using pull on a branch that has diverged significantly, leading to complex conflicts.

Interview tip: Mention that you prefer `git fetch` + `git merge` or `git pull --rebase` to have more control. Also mention `git pull --ff-only` for fast-forward only.

---

Q25: What is the difference between `git pull` and `git fetch`?

Direct answer: `git fetch` only downloads the commits and objects from the remote but does not integrate them into your current working branch. `git pull` downloads and then integrates (merges or rebases) them.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of the fetch/merge separation, which shows a deeper Git knowledge than just using pull blindly.

Breaking it down:
- `git fetch`: Updates remote-tracking branches (e.g., `origin/main`) but leaves your local branches and working directory untouched.
- `git pull`: Fetch + either merge or rebase.
- Use fetch when you want to see what's new before deciding to merge.
- Fetch is safe; it never changes your working directory or local branches.
- Pull can cause conflicts or unexpected merges.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The key difference is that `git fetch` just downloads the latest commits from the remote – it's like checking what's new without applying anything. My local branches remain exactly where they were. `git pull` does that and then immediately merges or rebases the downloaded commits into my current branch. I usually use `git fetch` when I want to review changes before integrating them, and `git pull` when I'm confident I want to bring them in right away."

Example:
# Just fetch (safe)
git fetch origin
# Check differences
git log HEAD..origin/main
# Then merge
git merge origin/main
# Or use pull (fetch + merge)
git pull origin main
# Pull with rebase
git pull --rebase origin main

Real-world use case: You fetch to see if a teammate has pushed changes. You inspect the commits, and then decide to merge or rebase. In CI scripts, you might fetch to compare branches.

Common mistakes:
- Thinking `git fetch` updates your local branch (it doesn't).
- Using pull and accidentally creating a merge commit when you wanted rebase.
- Not fetching before checking status with `git status` (shows behind/ ahead).

Interview tip: Mention that `git pull` without arguments uses the default remote and branch of the current branch, which is configured via tracking. Also mention that `git fetch --all` fetches all remotes.

---

Q26: How do you merge two branches?

Direct answer: You merge two branches by switching to the target branch (the one you want to merge into) and using `git merge <source-branch>`, which incorporates the changes from the source branch into the target.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to confirm you know the basic merge workflow, which is essential for collaboration.

Breaking it down:
- Switch to the target branch: `git checkout main` or `git switch main`.
- Ensure target is up-to-date: `git pull origin main`.
- Merge source: `git merge feature-branch`.
- If conflicts occur, resolve them, add, and commit.
- Fast-forward merge: If target hasn't diverged, Git simply moves the pointer.
- Three-way merge: Creates a merge commit if branches have diverged.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To merge two branches, I first switch to the branch I want to merge into, usually `main`. Then I run `git merge feature-branch`. This takes all the commits from the feature branch and integrates them into `main`. If there are no conflicts, it either fast-forwards or creates a merge commit. If there are conflicts, I resolve them, stage the resolved files, and commit. I always make sure my target branch is up-to-date before merging."

Example:
# Switch to main
git checkout main
git pull origin main
# Merge feature branch
git merge feature/user-auth
# If conflicts, resolve, then
git add .
git commit -m "Merge feature/user-auth"

Real-world use case: When a feature is complete and reviewed, you merge it into `main` via a pull request (which does a merge). Locally, you merge feature branches into your development branch.

Common mistakes:
- Merging without pulling the latest target branch.
- Resolving conflicts incorrectly.
- Not testing after merge.
- Using `git merge` when you meant `git rebase` for linear history.

Interview tip: Mention that you use pull requests for merging in teams; local merges are for syncing feature branches with `main` before pushing.

---

Q27: What is a fast-forward merge?

Direct answer: A fast-forward merge occurs when the target branch has not diverged from the source branch; Git simply moves the target branch pointer forward to the source branch's commit, without creating a merge commit.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand Git's merge behavior and when a merge commit is created.

Breaking it down:
- Condition: The target branch is an ancestor of the source branch (i.e., no new commits on target since branching).
- Git just updates the pointer of the target to point to the source's commit.
- No new commit is created.
- It keeps history linear but doesn't capture the "merge" event explicitly.
- You can prevent fast-forward with `--no-ff` to force a merge commit.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A fast-forward merge happens when the branch I'm merging into hasn't had any new commits since I branched off. In that case, Git just moves the target branch pointer forward to the latest commit of the source branch. It's called 'fast-forward' because it just slides the pointer forward. No merge commit is created, so history remains linear. I might use `--no-ff` if I want to record a merge commit for traceability, even if a fast-forward is possible."

Example:
# Feature branch has commits, main hasn't moved
git checkout main
git merge feature --ff-only
# The main pointer now points to the same commit as feature
# No new commit

Real-world use case: When you have a linear feature branch and main hasn't changed, fast-forward is common. If you want to preserve the merge commit for code review purposes, you use `--no-ff`.

Common mistakes:
- Thinking a fast-forward creates a merge commit (it doesn't).
- Not realizing that `git merge` defaults to fast-forward if possible.
- Using `--no-ff` unnecessarily causing extra commits.

Interview tip: Mention that `git merge --no-ff` is often used in release workflows to preserve feature grouping, and you can set it as default.

---

Q28: What is a three-way merge?

Direct answer: A three-way merge occurs when the target branch and the source branch have diverged since they split. Git creates a new merge commit that combines the changes from both branches, using the common ancestor as the base.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of the more complex merge scenario, which is common in team workflows.

Breaking it down:
- Divergence: Both branches have commits that the other does not have.
- Git finds the common ancestor commit (the point where they branched).
- It compares the changes from ancestor → target and ancestor → source.
- It then applies both sets of changes to the ancestor, creating a new merge commit with two parents (target and source).
- The merge commit captures the combination.
- Conflicts arise if both branches changed the same line.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A three-way merge happens when both branches have evolved separately from their common ancestor. Git looks at the ancestor commit, the latest commit on my current branch, and the latest commit on the branch I'm merging. It combines the changes from both branches, and if there are no conflicts, it creates a new merge commit with two parents – one from each branch. This is the default merge when a fast-forward isn't possible."

Example:
# main has commits A, B, C
# feature has commits A, B, D
# Merge: Git compares A (base) with C (main) and D (feature)
git checkout main
git merge feature   # creates merge commit E with parents C and D

Real-world use case: When multiple developers work on different features and merge them into a shared integration branch, three-way merges are inevitable.

Common mistakes:
- Not pulling before merging (causes unnecessary divergence).
- Not understanding why a merge commit appears.
- Panicking when conflicts arise; resolving incorrectly.

Interview tip: Emphasize that three-way merges are normal and that Git is smart enough to handle most cases automatically. Knowing how to resolve conflicts is the key.

---

Q29: What is a merge conflict and how do you resolve it?

Direct answer: A merge conflict occurs when Git cannot automatically reconcile changes from two branches because they modify the same parts of the same files differently. You resolve it by manually editing the files to choose which changes to keep, staging them, and completing the merge.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to know if you can handle one of the most common and challenging Git situations in daily development.

Breaking it down:
- Conflict markers: `<<<<<<< HEAD`, `=======`, `>>>>>>> branch-name` in the file.
- `HEAD` is your current branch's changes; the other part is the incoming branch's changes.
- Resolution: Edit the file to remove the markers and choose the correct final version.
- After editing: `git add <file>` to mark as resolved.
- Complete the merge: `git commit` (creates merge commit) or `git rebase --continue` if in rebase.
- You can use tools like `git mergetool` (e.g., VSCode, Meld).

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A merge conflict happens when two branches have changed the same lines of a file, and Git doesn't know which version to keep. Git marks the conflict in the file with special markers. To resolve it, I open the file, look at the changes, and decide which version to keep or combine them. Then I remove the markers, save the file, stage it with `git add`, and finally commit the merge. I also use my IDE's merge tool to help visualize and resolve conflicts."

Example:
# File content
<<<<<<< HEAD
print("Hello from main")
=======
print("Hello from feature")
>>>>>>> feature

# After resolution, keep one or combine
print("Hello from both")
# Then
git add conflicted-file.py
git commit -m "Resolve merge conflict"

Real-world use case: When two developers modify the same function signature in different branches. You resolve by deciding which parameters to keep.

Common mistakes:
- Forgetting to remove conflict markers.
- Resolving incorrectly and introducing bugs.
- Not testing after conflict resolution.
- Using `git commit` without `git add` after resolving.

Interview tip: Mention that you use `git status` to list conflicted files, and use `git diff` to see conflicts. Also mention that you often communicate with the other developer to decide the correct resolution.

---

Q30: How do you resolve a merge conflict manually?

Direct answer: You resolve a merge conflict manually by opening the conflicted files, editing them to keep the correct changes, removing conflict markers, staging the resolved files with `git add`, and committing to finalize.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to ensure you know the actual step-by-step process, not just a high-level description.

Breaking it down:
1. Run `git status` to find conflicted files.
2. Open each file in a text editor.
3. Look for conflict markers (`<<<<<<<`, `=======`, `>>>>>>>`).
4. Edit to remove the markers and keep the desired changes (either HEAD, incoming, or a combination).
5. Save the file.
6. Stage it: `git add <file>`.
7. Once all conflicts are resolved, commit: `git commit`.
8. If in a rebase, use `git rebase --continue` instead of commit.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"When I face a merge conflict, I first run `git status` to see which files need attention. Then I open those files and look for the conflict markers. I decide what the final code should be – sometimes I keep my version, sometimes the other, or I combine them. After editing, I remove the markers and save. Then I stage the file with `git add`. Once all conflicts are resolved, I run `git commit` to finish the merge. I always test my application after resolving conflicts."

Example:
# Step 1: Find conflicts
git status
# Step 2: Edit file (example)
# original conflict
<<<<<<< HEAD
const greeting = "Hello";
=======
const greeting = "Hola";
>>>>>>> feature
# Resolved
const greeting = "Hello"; # We choose HEAD
# Step 3: Stage
git add greeting.js
# Step 4: Commit
git commit -m "Merge branch 'feature' resolved conflict"

Real-world use case: After a pull request merge conflict on GitHub, you pull the changes locally and resolve manually, then push.

Common mistakes:
- Forgetting to stage the resolved files (Git still sees them as conflicted).
- Using `git commit -a` without adding resolved files.
- Not testing after resolving.
- Resolving conflicts differently from the team's intended behavior.

Interview tip: Mention that you use `git diff` to see the changes in conflict, and sometimes use `git mergetool` to launch a GUI. Also mention that rebase conflicts follow a similar process but use `git rebase --continue`.

---

Q31: What is `git rebase` and when would you use it?

Direct answer: `git rebase` is a command that moves or combines a sequence of commits to a new base commit. It rewrites history by applying your commits on top of another branch. It's used to keep a linear, clean commit history and to integrate upstream changes.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand advanced history rewriting and the trade-offs between merge and rebase.

Breaking it down:
- Rebase takes commits from your branch and replays them on top of another branch.
- It rewrites commit hashes and can cause conflicts during replay.
- Use cases: updating a feature branch with latest `main` before merging, squashing commits, editing history.
- Pros: Clean linear history, no merge commits.
- Cons: Rewrites history (dangerous on shared branches), requires force push.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`git rebase` takes my feature branch commits and replays them on top of the latest `main`. I use it to keep a linear history and avoid merge commits. For example, before I open a pull request, I rebase my feature branch onto `main` to ensure it's up-to-date and conflicts are resolved early. However, I never rebase a branch that others are working on because it rewrites history and can cause chaos. I prefer rebase for local cleanup and merge for shared branches."

Example:
# Rebase feature onto main
git checkout feature
git rebase main
# If conflicts, resolve and continue
git add .
git rebase --continue
# After rebase, push with force
git push --force-with-lease

Real-world use case: A developer rebases their feature branch daily to keep up with `main`, resolving conflicts early. Open-source maintainers often ask PR authors to rebase before merging.

Common mistakes:
- Rebasing a public branch that others have based work on.
- Using `git rebase` when you meant `git merge`, causing history divergence.
- Forgetting to force push after rebase (push fails).
- Not understanding that rebase changes commit hashes.

Interview tip: Emphasize that you use rebase for private branches and merge for public/historical branches. Mention `git pull --rebase` as a way to keep local branches updated.

---

Q32: What is an interactive rebase?

Direct answer: An interactive rebase (`git rebase -i`) allows you to modify, reorder, squash, edit, or drop commits in your branch's history before applying them to a new base. It's a powerful tool for cleaning up local commits.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to know if you can refine a series of commits into a clean, logical history, which is a sign of professional Git usage.

Breaking it down:
- `git rebase -i <base>` opens an editor with a list of commits.
- Actions: `pick` (use commit), `reword` (change message), `edit` (modify content), `squash` (combine with previous), `fixup` (squash but discard message), `drop` (remove commit).
- You reorder lines to reorder commits.
- You can edit commits by changing to `edit` and then `git commit --amend` and `git rebase --continue`.
- Interactive rebase is for local history only; never for pushed commits shared by others.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Interactive rebase is a way to rewrite history with full control. I use it to clean up my local commits before sharing. I run `git rebase -i HEAD~3` to edit the last three commits. In the editor, I can squash several WIP commits into one, reword messages, or drop unwanted commits. This makes my commit history cleaner and more logical for reviewers. I always do this before merging a feature branch."

Example:
# Interactive rebase last 3 commits
git rebase -i HEAD~3
# Editor opens:
pick a1b2c3 Add login feature
squash d4e5f6 Fix typo in login
squash g7h8i9 Add tests for login
# Save. Then Git combines them.

Real-world use case: A developer works on a feature and makes 10 commits with messages like "wip", "fix", "typo". Before opening a PR, they squash them into 2 logical commits.

Common mistakes:
- Rebasing commits that have already been pushed.
- Squashing commits that should remain separate.
- Forgetting to continue after resolving conflicts.
- Not knowing how to abort with `git rebase --abort` if things go wrong.

Interview tip: Mention that you use interactive rebase to prepare a clean PR history. Also mention `git rebase -i --root` to edit the initial commit if needed.

---

Q33: How do you squash commits using rebase?

Direct answer: To squash commits, you use `git rebase -i <base>` and change the action from `pick` to `squash` (or `s`) for the commits you want to combine into the previous commit. This combines them into a single commit with a consolidated message.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to verify you can clean up commit history, which is important for creating meaningful, review-friendly PRs.

Breaking it down:
- Run `git rebase -i HEAD~n` where n is the number of commits to review.
- In the editor, the commits are listed oldest to newest.
- For the commits you want to squash, change `pick` to `squash` or `s`.
- The first commit (with `pick`) remains; the squashed commits are merged into it.
- Git then opens a new editor for the combined commit message.
- You edit the message to reflect the combined changes.
- Save and Git rewrites history.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To squash commits, I use interactive rebase. I run `git rebase -i HEAD~4` to look at the last four commits. Then I change `pick` to `squash` for all the commits I want to combine except the first one. Git will combine them and let me edit the final message. I usually do this to turn a series of messy work-in-progress commits into a clean, single commit for review."

Example:
# Rebase last 4 commits
git rebase -i HEAD~4
# Editor shows:
pick 123a Add login logic
pick 456b Fix login bug
pick 789c Add login tests
pick abc1 Refactor login
# Change to:
pick 123a Add login logic
squash 456b Fix login bug
squash 789c Add login tests
squash abc1 Refactor login
# Save, edit final message, save again.

Real-world use case: Before opening a PR, you squash all fixup commits into the main feature commit so that the PR shows a clean history of the feature.

Common mistakes:
- Squashing commits that are already pushed (requires force push).
- Forgetting that the first commit must be `pick`; you cannot squash into nothing.
- Writing a poor combined message.
- Not using `fixup` if you want to discard the commit message.

Interview tip: Mention that you use `fixup` instead of `squash` when you just want to discard the messages of the squashed commits and keep the first one.

---

Q34: What is a pull request (PR) in GitHub?

Direct answer: A pull request is a GitHub feature that lets a developer notify team members that they have completed a feature or fix and want to merge their branch into the main codebase. It provides a platform for code review, discussion, and automated checks before merging.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to ensure you understand the collaborative workflow on GitHub, which is standard in modern development.

Breaking it down:
- PRs are opened from a branch (often a feature branch) to another (usually `main`).
- They show the diff, commits, and allow comments on specific lines.
- CI/CD can run on PRs (e.g., tests, linting).
- Reviews: team members approve or request changes.
- Options: merge commit, squash merge, rebase and merge.
- They can be closed without merging.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A pull request is a request to merge changes from one branch into another, usually from a feature branch into `main`. It's the primary way we collaborate on GitHub. I open a PR when my feature is ready, and my teammates review the code, leave comments, and suggest improvements. Automated tests run on the PR to ensure nothing breaks. Once approved and all checks pass, the PR is merged. It's also a great way to discuss the change asynchronously."

Example:
# Workflow
git checkout -b feature/new-api
# ... work, commit, push
git push origin feature/new-api
# Then on GitHub, create a PR from feature/new-api to main
# Add description, assign reviewers

Real-world use case: Every feature, bugfix, or hotfix in a team project goes through a PR to ensure code quality and knowledge sharing.

Common mistakes:
- Opening a PR with failing tests or merge conflicts.
- Not providing a clear description or context.
- Not addressing review comments before merging.
- Merging without approval.

Interview tip: Mention that you write PR descriptions with context, screenshots, and link to issues. Also mention that you review others' PRs constructively.

---

Q35: What is the difference between a pull request and a merge request?

Direct answer: A pull request (PR) is a term used by GitHub, while a merge request (MR) is the equivalent term used by GitLab. Both mean the same thing: a request to merge a branch into another. Functionally, they are nearly identical.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to check if you're familiar with different Git platforms and if you understand that the concepts are universal.

Breaking it down:
- GitHub: Pull Request (PR).
- GitLab: Merge Request (MR).
- Bitbucket: Pull Request (also PR).
- Core functionality: code review, discussion, CI, approval, merge options.
- Differences are minor UI/UX but the concepts (source branch, target branch, reviewers) are identical.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"There is no technical difference between a pull request and a merge request – they are just different names used by different platforms. GitHub calls it a pull request, GitLab calls it a merge request, and Bitbucket also calls it a pull request. The purpose is the same: to propose changes, review code, run automated checks, and merge the code into the target branch. I've used both terms interchangeably depending on the platform."

Example:
# GitHub: Pull Request
# GitLab: Merge Request
# Workflow is the same
# Create branch, push, open PR/MR, review, merge.

Real-world use case: If you switch companies or projects, you might use GitHub or GitLab. Knowing the equivalence helps you adapt quickly.

Common mistakes:
- Stating they are different in functionality (they are not).
- Not knowing the term for the platform you are using.
- Confusing them with `git merge` (the command).

Interview tip: Mention that in GitLab, merge requests have more built-in CI/CD features, but fundamentally they serve the same purpose.

---

Q36: What is the difference between a merge commit and a squash merge in GitHub?

Direct answer: A merge commit merges the entire history of the source branch into the target branch, preserving all individual commits. A squash merge combines all commits from the source branch into a single commit on the target branch, making the history cleaner but losing granularity.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand the different merge strategies on GitHub and their impact on commit history.

Breaking it down:
- Merge commit: All commits from the source branch are added to the target branch, plus a merge commit. History shows each commit and the merge.
- Squash merge: All commits are squashed into one new commit on the target branch. The source branch's individual commits are not preserved in the target's history.
- GitHub options: "Create a merge commit", "Squash and merge", "Rebase and merge".
- Squashing gives a cleaner linear history but loses the work-in-progress details.
- Merge commit preserves the context of the branch history.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A merge commit preserves the entire commit history of the source branch, so you can see all the individual commits and the merge point. A squash merge takes all the commits from the source branch and combines them into a single commit on the target branch. I use squash when the source branch has many small, messy commits (like 'wip', 'fix') and I want a cleaner history on `main`. I use merge commit when I want to preserve the detailed history, perhaps for a larger feature where each commit tells a story."

Example:
# Merge commit: history shows each commit
# Squash: history shows a single commit

Real-world use case: Teams often use squash for feature branches to keep `main` clean, especially when PRs have many temporary commits. Merge commits are used for long-running integration branches.

Common mistakes:
- Squashing when you need to preserve the granularity for troubleshooting (e.g., `git bisect`).
- Using merge commit and cluttering `main` with many small commits.
