- Not understanding that squash changes the commit hash.

Interview tip: Mention that you discuss with your team which strategy they prefer. Also mention that GitHub's "Rebase and merge" is another option that applies commits one by one without a merge commit.

---

Q37: What is a detached HEAD state?

Direct answer: A detached HEAD state occurs when `HEAD` points directly to a specific commit, rather than to a branch. You are no longer on any branch, and any new commits you make will not belong to a branch unless you create one.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of HEAD and branches, and to see if you know how to recover from this state.

Breaking it down:
- You enter detached HEAD by checking out a commit hash or a tag: `git checkout abc123`.
- Or checking out a remote branch without creating a local tracking branch: `git checkout origin/main`.
- In this state, commits you create are orphaned (unless you create a branch).
- To get back: `git switch -c new-branch` to create a branch from the current commit, or `git checkout main` to switch back to a named branch.
- You can view state with `git status` or `git branch`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A detached HEAD state is when HEAD points to a commit instead of a branch. This happens if I check out a specific commit or a tag. In this state, I'm not on any branch, so if I make new commits and then switch to another branch, those commits might be lost unless I create a branch to save them. I usually avoid making changes in detached HEAD unless I intend to create a new branch from that point. To fix it, I create a new branch or switch to an existing branch."

Example:
# Enter detached HEAD
git checkout abc123
# Make changes and commit
git add .
git commit -m "WIP in detached"
# Save to a new branch
git switch -c new-feature
# Or discard and switch back
git checkout main

Real-world use case: You check out an older commit to test, or you check out a tag (e.g., `v1.0`) to build a release. You might also enter detached HEAD when reviewing a PR locally.

Common mistakes:
- Making commits in detached HEAD and then losing them.
- Not realizing you're in detached HEAD (check with `git status`).
- Thinking you're on a branch when you're not.

Interview tip: Mention that `git checkout` can be confusing; newer `git switch` and `git restore` make it clearer. Also mention that you can always use `git reflog` to recover lost commits from detached HEAD.

---

Q38: How do you recover lost commits in Git?

Direct answer: You recover lost commits using `git reflog` to find the commit hash, and then you can `git checkout` that commit or create a new branch pointing to it.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to know if you can handle accidental history loss, which is a crucial troubleshooting skill.

Breaking it down:
- Lost commits are usually still in the reflog unless they have been garbage-collected.
- Run `git reflog` to see a list of all HEAD movements.
- Find the commit hash you want to recover (look for the state before the reset/rebase).
- Recover by: `git checkout <hash>` (detached HEAD) and then `git switch -c new-branch` to create a branch.
- Or directly: `git branch recover-branch <hash>`.
- The reflog expires after 90 days by default.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"When I lose commits, I don't panic – I use `git reflog`. This shows me a history of where HEAD has been, including commits that aren't shown in `git log`. I find the hash of the commit I want to recover, and then I either check it out or create a new branch from it. For example, after a hard reset, `git reflog` will show the commit before the reset. I create a branch from that hash and I'm back in business."

Example:
# View reflog
git reflog
# Output: abc123 HEAD@{0}: reset: moving to HEAD~1
#         def456 HEAD@{1}: commit: Add feature
# Recover
git checkout def456   # or
git branch recover-feature def456

Real-world use case: After an accidental `git reset --hard`, you recover the lost commit. After a failed rebase, you can use `git reflog` to find the state before the rebase.

Common mistakes:
- Not using reflog at all and giving up.
- Waiting too long (reflog expires).
- Not creating a branch, just checking out and losing it again.
- Forgetting that reflog is local; if you push after a reset, the commits might still exist but others won't see them.

Interview tip: Mention `git fsck --lost-found` as another way to find dangling commits if reflog is unavailable. Also mention `git reset --hard ORIG_HEAD` after a merge or rebase to undo the last operation.

---

Q39: What is a tag in Git and how is it different from a branch?

Direct answer: A tag is a fixed reference to a specific commit, typically used to mark release points (e.g., `v1.0`). Unlike a branch, which moves forward with new commits, a tag is immutable and stays pointing to the same commit forever.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to distinguish between moving pointers (branches) and fixed markers (tags), which is important for release management.

Breaking it down:
- Tag: Points to a specific commit and does not change.
- Branch: A movable pointer that advances with each new commit.
- Tags are often used for releases, versions, or important milestones.
- Tags can be lightweight (just a pointer) or annotated (with metadata like tagger, date, message).
- You can checkout a tag (detached HEAD) but you cannot commit to it.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A tag is a marker that points to a specific commit and never changes. It's like a bookmark for a release or a significant point in history. A branch, on the other hand, is a pointer that moves forward as I make new commits. I use tags to mark versions like `v1.0` or `v2.1` so I can always go back to that exact state. Branches are for ongoing development."

Example:
# Create a lightweight tag
git tag v1.0
# Create annotated tag
git tag -a v1.0 -m "Release version 1.0"
# List tags
git tag
# Push tags to remote
git push origin v1.0
git push --tags

Real-world use case: After a release, you tag the commit that was deployed. This allows you to quickly check out the exact version that was released.

Common mistakes:
- Moving a tag (they should be immutable; you can delete and recreate, but avoid).
- Confusing tags with branches (e.g., trying to commit on a tag).
- Forgetting to push tags to remote (`git push` doesn't push tags by default).
- Using tags for short-lived markers (use branches instead).

Interview tip: Mention that annotated tags are preferred because they store metadata. Also mention that tags are often used in CI/CD to trigger deployments.

---

Q40: What are lightweight and annotated tags?

Direct answer: A lightweight tag is simply a pointer to a commit (like a branch that doesn't move), while an annotated tag is an object in Git's database that stores metadata such as the tagger's name, email, date, and a message, and is signed with a GPG key.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know the difference and when to use each.

Breaking it down:
- Lightweight: `git tag v1.0` – just a file with a hash. No extra info.
- Annotated: `git tag -a v1.0 -m "message"` – creates a tag object with metadata.
- Annotated tags have a SHA-1 hash and can be verified for authenticity.
- Recommended for public releases.
- Lightweight is fine for temporary or personal tags.
- Use `git show <tagname>` to see details; for lightweight it shows the commit, for annotated it shows the tag object.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A lightweight tag is just a name that points to a commit, like a quick bookmark. An annotated tag is a full Git object with a message, date, and author. I use annotated tags for official releases because they provide context and can be signed with GPG to verify authenticity. Lightweight tags I might use for personal temporary markers, like if I want to remember a specific point for a quick experiment."

Example:
# Lightweight
git tag v1.0
# Annotated
git tag -a v1.0 -m "Release version 1.0 with new features"
# View tag details
git show v1.0

Real-world use case: Open-source projects use annotated tags for every release to provide release notes and verify signatures.

Common mistakes:
- Using lightweight tags for releases (lacks information).
- Using `-a` but forgetting `-m`, which opens an editor.
- Not pushing tags to remote.
- Not signing tags when security is important.

Interview tip: Mention that `git tag -s` is used for GPG-signed tags, and GitHub shows these as verified. This shows advanced knowledge.

Q41: How do you create a tag and push it to a remote?

Direct answer: Create a tag locally with `git tag <tagname>` (lightweight) or `git tag -a <tagname> -m "message"` (annotated), then push it to the remote with `git push origin <tagname>` or push all tags with `git push --tags`.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to know if you understand the release workflow and how to share version markers with the team.

Breaking it down:
- Local tag creation does not automatically push to remote.
- Push a single tag: `git push origin v1.0`.
- Push all tags: `git push --tags` (or `git push origin --tags`).
- You can also push tags with commits: `git push origin main --tags`.
- To delete a remote tag: `git push origin --delete <tagname>`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To create a tag, I use `git tag -a v1.0 -m "Release 1.0"` locally. That creates an annotated tag. Then I push it to the remote with `git push origin v1.0`. If I have multiple tags, I use `git push --tags` to send all of them. I always push tags separately because `git push` by default does not send them."

Example:
# Create annotated tag
git tag -a v1.0 -m "First production release"
# Push single tag
git push origin v1.0
# Push all tags
git push --tags
# Delete remote tag
git push origin --delete v1.0

Real-world use case: After a successful deployment, you tag the commit with the version number and push the tag. CI/CD systems often use tags to trigger production releases.

Common mistakes:
- Creating a tag but forgetting to push it (it stays local only).
- Using lightweight tags for official releases (lack metadata).
- Pushing tags without pushing the commit first (the tag will point to a commit that might not exist on remote).

Interview tip: Mention that you prefer annotated tags for releases because they store the author, date, and message. Also mention that GitHub releases can be created from tags.

---

Q42: What is `git stash` and when would you use it?

Direct answer: `git stash` temporarily saves uncommitted changes (both staged and unstaged) and reverts your working directory to the last commit, allowing you to switch branches or pull changes without committing incomplete work.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know how to handle work‑in‑progress when you need to context‑switch.

Breaking it down:
- Stash saves changes to a stack (LIFO) and resets your working tree.
- `git stash` (or `git stash push`) saves both staged and unstaged changes.
- `git stash -u` also saves untracked files.
- `git stash list` shows all stashes.
- `git stash apply` reapplies the latest stash without removing it.
- `git stash pop` applies and removes it.
- `git stash drop` deletes a stash.
- `git stash branch <name>` creates a branch from the stash.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"I use `git stash` when I have uncommitted changes and need to switch to another branch urgently, for example, to fix a critical bug. Instead of committing half‑done work, I stash the changes, switch branches, do the fix, and then pop the stash to continue. It saves my working directory and I can reapply it later. I also use `-u` to stash untracked files."

Example:
# Save changes
git stash -u
# Switch to other branch
git checkout hotfix
# ... do work ...
git checkout feature
# Reapply latest stash and remove it
git stash pop
# List stashes
git stash list

Real-world use case: You are in the middle of a feature and a production hotfix comes in. You stash, fix, then continue.

Common mistakes:
- Forgetting to stash untracked files (`-u`), so they remain and cause conflicts.
- Using `stash pop` and losing the stash if a conflict occurs (it doesn't drop on conflict).
- Not using a descriptive message: `git stash push -m "wip on login"`.
- Stashing too many times and not cleaning up.

Interview tip: Mention `git stash push -m "message"` to identify stashes. Also mention that stashes are local and not shared.

---

Q43: What is the difference between `git stash pop` and `git stash apply`?

Direct answer: `git stash apply` reapplies the stash to your working directory but keeps the stash in the stash list. `git stash pop` reapplies the stash and then drops it from the stash list.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to check your understanding of stash management, especially whether you know the safe vs. destructive options.

Breaking it down:
- `apply`: Reapplies changes, stash remains. Useful if you want to apply to multiple branches.
- `pop`: Applies and deletes the stash. It's a convenience for one‑time use.
- Both can specify a stash: `git stash apply stash@{1}`.
- If conflicts occur, `pop` does NOT drop the stash (safe behaviour).
- `git stash drop` deletes without applying.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The difference is that `pop` applies the stash and then removes it from the stack, while `apply` just applies it and leaves it there. I use `apply` when I want to apply the same stash to multiple branches or when I'm not sure if I want to keep it. I use `pop` when I'm confident and want to clean up. If there's a conflict, `pop` won't drop it, so my work is safe."

Example:
# Apply and keep
git stash apply stash@{0}
# Apply and remove
git stash pop
# Apply specific stash
git stash apply stash@{2}

Real-world use case: You stashed a fix, want to apply it to `main`, test it, and then apply it to `develop` – you use `apply` to reuse it.

Common mistakes:
- Using `pop` and losing the stash due to a conflict (it doesn't drop, but many think it does).
- Not specifying which stash and applying the wrong one.
- Forgetting that `pop` drops the stash, so you can't reapply if needed.

Interview tip: Mention that `git stash apply` is safer when you're unsure, and you can always `drop` later. Also mention `git stash show` to see what's in a stash.

---

Q44: What is `git cherry-pick`?

Direct answer: `git cherry-pick` takes a specific commit from one branch and applies its changes onto your current branch as a new commit, preserving the original commit's message and author.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know how to selectively apply changes, which is useful for hotfixes and backporting.

Breaking it down:
- `git cherry-pick <commit-hash>` applies that commit to your current branch.
- You can cherry-pick a range: `git cherry-pick A..B`.
- It creates a new commit with a different hash but same changes.
- Conflicts can occur; resolve and `git cherry-pick --continue`.
- Use `--no-commit` to apply changes without committing immediately.
- `-n` (no commit) allows you to stage and commit with a different message.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"I use `git cherry-pick` when I need to bring a specific commit from another branch into my current branch without merging the whole branch. For example, if a critical bug fix was made on `develop` and I need it on `main` immediately, I cherry-pick that commit. It applies the changes as a new commit, and I resolve any conflicts if they arise."

Example:
# Switch to main
git checkout main
# Cherry-pick a commit from develop
git cherry-pick abc123
# If conflict, resolve, then
git add .
git cherry-pick --continue
# Cherry-pick without committing
git cherry-pick -n def456

Real-world use case: Backporting a security patch to an older release branch. Or moving a single improvement without all the other commits.

Common mistakes:
- Cherry-picking merge commits (they have two parents – use `-m` to specify parent).
- Creating duplicate commits and confusion if the original branch is merged later.
- Not resolving conflicts properly.
- Forgetting to commit after `--no-commit`.

Interview tip: Mention that cherry-picking is a powerful tool but can cause duplicate commits; use it judiciously. Also mention `git revert` is for undoing, cherry-pick is for copying.

---

Q45: What is `git bisect` and how do you use it?

Direct answer: `git bisect` is a binary search tool that helps you find the commit that introduced a bug by repeatedly checking out commits between a known good and a known bad state, and asking you to mark each as good or bad.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know efficient debugging techniques in Git.

Breaking it down:
- Start bisect: `git bisect start`.
- Mark bad commit: `git bisect bad` (current HEAD) or `git bisect bad <hash>`.
- Mark good commit: `git bisect good <hash>` (e.g., a known working commit).
- Git checks out a midpoint commit.
- Test if the bug exists; mark `git bisect good` or `git bisect bad`.
- Repeat until Git identifies the first bad commit.
- End bisect: `git bisect reset` (returns to original branch).
- You can automate with `git bisect run <script>`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`git bisect` is a lifesaver for finding when a bug was introduced. I start by `git bisect start`, then mark the current commit as `bad` and an older commit I know was working as `good`. Git then checks out a commit halfway between them. I test if the bug is there, mark it good or bad, and Git keeps narrowing down until it tells me the exact commit that broke things. After that, I run `git bisect reset` to go back."

Example:
git bisect start
git bisect bad           # current commit has bug
git bisect good v1.0     # v1.0 is known good
# Git checks out a commit
# Test...
git bisect good          # if bug not present
# or git bisect bad      # if bug present
# Repeat...
git bisect reset         # back to original branch

Real-world use case: A regression is found in production. You use bisect on your commit history to pinpoint which commit caused it, even if it was days ago.

Common mistakes:
- Marking a commit as good when it actually has the bug (skews result).
- Forgetting to reset after bisect (leaves you in detached HEAD).
- Using bisect on a branch with many merge commits (can be complex).
- Not using `bisect run` to automate repetitive testing.

Interview tip: Mention that you often write a script that tests the bug and use `git bisect run ./test.sh` to fully automate the process – this impresses interviewers.

---

Q46: What is `git blame`?

Direct answer: `git blame` shows who last modified each line of a file, when, and in which commit. It's used to trace the origin of code changes, often for debugging or understanding why something was written.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to know if you can trace code authorship, which is important for code reviews and understanding context.

Breaking it down:
- `git blame <file>`: Annotates each line with commit hash, author, date.
- `git blame -L <start>,<end>`: Limits to specific line range.
- `git blame -C`: Detects code moved or copied within the same file.
- `git blame -C -C`: Also detects across files.
- Useful to find who to ask about a particular piece of logic.
- Also shows the commit message if you use `git show <hash>`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`git blame` is my go‑to when I need to know who changed a specific line and why. I run `git blame src/app.js` and it shows me every line with the commit hash and author. Then I can look at that commit with `git show` to see the full context. It's great for understanding the history of a piece of code, especially when I'm new to a project or when I find a bug and need to ask the original author."

Example:
# Blame a file
git blame src/index.js
# Blame lines 10-20
git blame -L 10,20 src/index.js
# Detect moved code
git blame -C src/index.js

Real-world use case: A developer finds a bug in a function, uses blame to see who wrote it, and asks them about the intent. Or to understand why a configuration was set a certain way.

Common mistakes:
- Using blame to assign blame (pun intended) – it's about understanding, not finger‑pointing.
- Not using `-C` to follow code that was moved.
- Forgetting that blame shows the last change – if a line was just reformatted, it may show the formatter commit.
- Not checking the commit message for the original reason.

Interview tip: Mention that `git blame` combined with `git log -p` gives a full picture. Also mention that GitHub has a "Blame" view in the UI.

---

Q47: What is a submodule? How do you add one?

Direct answer: A submodule is a reference to another Git repository embedded inside your repository, allowing you to include external dependencies while keeping them independent. You add one with `git submodule add <url> <path>`.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know how to manage dependencies and external repositories within a project.

Breaking it down:
- Submodule is a pointer to a specific commit in another repository.
- `git submodule add <url>` clones the external repo into a subdirectory.
- It creates a `.gitmodules` file tracking the URL and path.
- Submodules are not automatically updated when the external repo changes.
- To update: `git submodule update --remote`.
- Cloning a repo with submodules: `git clone --recurse-submodules`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A submodule is like a reference to another Git repository within my project. I use it to include external libraries or shared components without copying their code into my repo. To add one, I run `git submodule add https://github.com/org/library.git libs/library`. This clones the external repo into that folder and tracks the commit it's pinned to. When I need to update it, I run `git submodule update --remote`."

Example:
# Add a submodule
git submodule add https://github.com/jquery/jquery.git libs/jquery
# Clone with submodules
git clone --recurse-submodules https://github.com/user/project.git
# Update all submodules to latest
git submodule update --remote

Real-world use case: Projects that use common libraries across multiple repos – like a shared authentication library or a custom UI framework.

Common mistakes:
- Forgetting to pull submodule changes after pulling the main repo.
- Modifying files inside the submodule and not committing to the submodule's own repo.
- Not initializing submodules after cloning (`git submodule init` and `update`).
- Using submodules when a package manager would be simpler.

Interview tip: Mention that submodules are less common now with package managers, but still used for proprietary or version‑locked internal libraries. Also mention that `git clone --recurse-submodules` is essential.

---

Q48: What is the difference between a submodule and a subtree?

Direct answer: A submodule is a pointer to an external repository (a link), while a subtree is an actual copy of an external repository's files merged into your own repository. Subtree allows you to make changes and merge back more easily.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to distinguish between two common ways of including external code, to see if you understand the trade‑offs.

Breaking it down:
- Submodule: Just a reference; external repo is separate. Changes in submodule require separate commit in that repo.
- Subtree: The external repo's files are copied into your repo's history. You can edit them locally and push changes back to the external repo.
- Subtree simplifies the clone process – no extra `--recurse-submodules`.
- Subtree is easier for contributors who don't need the external repo separately.
- Submodule is cleaner for strict version pinning.
- Use `git subtree add --prefix=<dir> <url> <ref>`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A submodule is a link to another repository, so my repo only stores a commit hash. A subtree, on the other hand, copies the external repository's files directly into my repo's history. Submodules are lightweight and keep separate histories, but they require extra steps when cloning. Subtrees are more integrated – people can clone my repo without extra steps, and I can make changes to the external code directly. However, subtree can make my repo history larger and more complex."

Example:
# Add subtree
git subtree add --prefix=libs/jquery https://github.com/jquery/jquery.git main --squash
# Pull updates
git subtree pull --prefix=libs/jquery https://github.com/jquery/jquery.git main --squash

Real-world use case: A project that embeds a vendored dependency and needs to apply custom patches – subtree is often easier.

Common mistakes:
- Using subtree without `--squash` can bring in many unnecessary commits.
- Not understanding that subtree commits are regular commits in your history.
- Forgetting that submodule updates require separate commands.
- Choosing subtree for a large external repo and bloating your repo.

Interview tip: Mention that many teams now prefer package managers, but for internal monorepos, subtree is often used. Also mention `git subtree` is built‑in but not as well‑known.

---

Q49: What is a Git hook? Name a few examples.

Direct answer: A Git hook is a script that Git runs automatically before or after certain events (like commit, push, merge). They are used to enforce policies, run tests, format code, or prevent bad commits.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to know if you use automation and quality gates in your workflow.

Breaking it down:
- Hooks are stored in `.git/hooks/`; they are sample scripts by default.
- Client‑side hooks: `pre-commit` (run before commit), `commit-msg` (validate message), `pre-push` (run before push).
- Server‑side hooks: `pre-receive`, `update`, `post-receive` (used on GitHub/GitLab for CI).
- To enable, rename the sample file (remove `.sample`) and make it executable.
- Example: `pre-commit` runs linters and tests; if they fail, commit is aborted.
- Example: `commit-msg` ensures commit message follows a convention (e.g., conventional commits).

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Git hooks are scripts that run automatically at certain points in the Git workflow. For example, a `pre-commit` hook can run linters or unit tests before I commit, preventing broken code from entering the repo. A `commit-msg` hook can check that my commit messages follow the team's format. I often use them with tools like Husky to automate quality checks. They're stored in `.git/hooks` but I usually share them via tooling so the whole team uses them."

Example:
# .git/hooks/pre-commit (bash script)
#!/bin/sh
npm run lint
if [ $? -ne 0 ]; then
  echo "Linting failed. Commit aborted."
  exit 1
fi

Real-world use case: A team enforces that all code passes ESLint before commit. They use a pre-commit hook to enforce it automatically.

Common mistakes:
- Not sharing hooks with the team (they are not versioned by default).
- Making hooks too slow, causing developers to bypass them.
- Not handling exit codes correctly (non‑zero aborts the action).
- Using hooks that are too complex and hard to maintain.

Interview tip: Mention that you use Husky (for Node) or pre-commit (Python) to share hooks. Also mention server‑side hooks for CI enforcement.

---

Q50: What is the difference between `git merge --no-ff` and `git merge --ff-only`?

Direct answer: `--no-ff` forces a merge commit even if a fast‑forward is possible, preserving the branch history. `--ff-only` only allows a merge if it can be fast‑forwarded; otherwise, it aborts.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of merge strategies and when to use each.

Breaking it down:
- `--ff-only`: Only merges if the target branch hasn't diverged (i.e., fast‑forward possible). Prevents accidental merge commits.
- `--no-ff`: Always creates a merge commit, even if linear. Preserves the fact that a branch existed.
- `--ff` (default): Fast‑forward if possible, otherwise three‑way merge.
- Use `--ff-only` to ensure a clean, linear history.
- Use `--no-ff` for feature branches to keep them visible.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`--ff-only` tells Git to merge only if it can simply move the branch pointer forward – if not, it fails. I use this when I want to keep history linear without merge commits. `--no-ff` forces a merge commit, even if a fast‑forward is possible. I use this for feature branches to show that the work happened on a separate branch, which is helpful for traceability. By default, Git does a fast‑forward if possible."

Example:
# Force a merge commit
git merge --no-ff feature
# Only if fast-forward possible
git merge --ff-only feature
# Default (fast-forward if possible)
git merge feature

Real-world use case: Many teams use `--no-ff` in pull request merges to keep the branch history visible. Some use `--ff-only` for release branches to keep them clean.

Common mistakes:
- Using `--no-ff` unnecessarily and cluttering history.
- Using `--ff-only` on a branch that has diverged and getting an error.
- Not knowing the default behaviour.
- Not configuring the desired strategy in team settings.

Interview tip: Mention that GitHub's "merge commit" option uses `--no-ff`, while "rebase and merge" uses linear history. Choose based on team preference.

---

Q51: What is the purpose of `git rm --cached`?

Direct answer: `git rm --cached` removes a file from Git's staging area (index) without deleting it from the working directory. This untracks the file while keeping it on disk.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to check your knowledge of untracking files, which is common for `.gitignore` clean‑up.

Breaking it down:
- `git rm --cached <file>`: Removes the file from the index (staging area), so it will no longer be tracked in future commits.
- The file remains in your working directory.
- Often used after adding a file to `.gitignore` to stop tracking it.
- The file remains in history; to remove from history, use `git filter-repo`.
- You need to commit the change to finalize untracking.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"I use `git rm --cached` when I've accidentally committed a file that should be ignored, like a `.env` or a log file. It removes the file from the index so that future commits won't track it, but it keeps the file on my disk so I don't lose it. Then I add it to `.gitignore` and commit. The file stays in the history, but that's okay for non‑sensitive data; for secrets, I'd use a tool to rewrite history."

Example:
# Stop tracking config.yml
git rm --cached config.yml
# Add to .gitignore
echo "config.yml" >> .gitignore
git add .gitignore
git commit -m "Stop tracking config.yml"

Real-world use case: A developer accidentally committed `node_modules` and wants to stop tracking it without deleting the folder.

Common mistakes:
- Forgetting to add the file to `.gitignore` after `--cached`, so it becomes untracked again later.
- Using `git rm` (without `--cached`) and deleting the file by mistake.
- Thinking `--cached` removes the file from history (it doesn't).
- Not committing the change, so it remains staged.

Interview tip: Mention that for sensitive data, you should also rotate the secret and use `git filter-repo` to purge history, not just `--cached`.

---

Q52: How do you rename a branch in Git?

Direct answer: To rename a branch, use `git branch -m <old-name> <new-name>` if you're on a different branch, or `git branch -m <new-name>` if you're currently on the branch you want to rename.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to confirm you know how to maintain branch naming conventions and fix typos.

Breaking it down:
- Local rename: `git branch -m old-name new-name`.
- If you're on the branch: `git branch -m new-name`.
- To update the remote:
  - Delete the old remote branch: `git push origin --delete old-name`.
  - Push the new branch: `git push origin new-name`.
  - Reset the upstream: `git push -u origin new-name`.
- The `-m` stands for move (rename).

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To rename a branch locally, I use `git branch -m` – if I'm on the branch, just `git branch -m new-name`; if not, I include the old name. After renaming locally, I need to update the remote: delete the old remote branch and push the new one with the upstream set. I often do this when I realize a branch name is unclear or contains a typo."

Example:
# On the branch to rename
git branch -m feature/login feature/auth
# Push new branch
git push origin feature/auth
# Delete old remote branch
git push origin --delete feature/login
# Set upstream
git push -u origin feature/auth

Real-world use case: You start a branch as `fix-login` but later realize it's a new feature, so you rename it to `feature/login`.

Common mistakes:
- Forgetting to update the remote, leading to both branches existing.
- Renaming a branch that others are also working on (causes confusion).
- Not using `-u` to set the upstream, so `git push` and `git pull` don't track.
- Renaming the default branch (requires additional steps on GitHub).

Interview tip: Mention that renaming the default branch (`main` to something else) needs extra configuration on the remote hosting service. Also mention that you coordinate with the team before renaming shared branches.

---

Q53: What is the `git clean` command?

Direct answer: `git clean` removes untracked files from your working directory. It is used to delete build artifacts, temporary files, and other ignored or untracked clutter.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know how to keep your working tree clean and understand the risk of removing files.

Breaking it down:
- `git clean -n`: Dry run – shows what would be removed.
- `git clean -f`: Force remove untracked files (required for safety).
- `git clean -fd`: Removes untracked files and directories.
- `git clean -fx`: Also removes files listed in `.gitignore` (ignored files).
- It does NOT affect tracked files.
- Use with caution; deleted files cannot be recovered easily.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`git clean` is a command to delete untracked files from my working directory. I use it to remove build outputs like `dist/`, `node_modules/`, or temporary log files that aren't tracked. I always run `git clean -n` first to preview what will be deleted, then `git clean -f` to actually remove them. If I want to also delete ignored files (like `node_modules`), I use `-x`. It's powerful and dangerous, so I double‑check before running."

Example:
# Preview
git clean -n
# Remove untracked files
git clean -f
# Remove untracked directories
git clean -fd
# Also remove ignored files
git clean -fx

Real-world use case: After a build, your working directory is full of generated files. You run `git clean` to reset it to a clean state.

Common mistakes:
- Running `git clean -f` without preview and deleting important untracked files.
- Forgetting to commit tracked changes before clean (it doesn't affect them, but it's confusing).
- Using `-x` and deleting `.gitignore` files that are ignored on purpose.
- Not using `-d` and leaving empty directories.

Interview tip: Mention that `git clean` is often used in CI scripts to ensure a pristine working environment before building.

---

Q54: How do you change the last commit message?

Direct answer: Use `git commit --amend -m "New message"` to change the message of the most recent commit. If you haven't pushed, this is safe; if you have pushed, you'll need to force push (and should avoid this on shared branches).

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to check if you know the simplest way to fix a commit message typo.

Breaking it down:
- `git commit --amend -m "new message"` updates the message.
- If you also want to add more changes to the last commit: stage changes, then `git commit --amend --no-edit` (keeps message) or `--edit` to change it.
- It rewrites the commit hash (changes history).
- For older commits, use interactive rebase (`git rebase -i`) and `reword`.
- Only amend if the commit hasn't been pushed, or you're on a private branch.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To change the last commit message, I use `git commit --amend -m "New commit message"`. This updates the message and rewrites the commit. I do this if I just pushed to a private branch and notice a typo. If the commit is already pushed to a shared branch, I avoid amending because it changes history and would require a force push, which can disrupt my teammates. For older commits, I use interactive rebase."

Example:
# Change message
git commit --amend -m "Fix login bug"
# Add more changes and keep old message
git add .
git commit --amend --no-edit

Real-world use case: You realize your commit message is vague after committing. You amend it before pushing to make it clear.

Common mistakes:
- Amending after pushing to a shared branch (requires force push).
- Using `--amend` to add major changes that should be separate commits.
- Not using `--no-edit` when you only want to add changes.
- Forgetting to stage changes before amending (they won't be included).

Interview tip: Mention that you use `git commit --amend` frequently for small corrections, but for shared history, you use `git revert` instead.

---

Q55: How do you protect a branch in GitHub?

Direct answer: In GitHub, you protect a branch by going to Settings → Branches → Add rule, and enabling protections like requiring pull request reviews, status checks, and preventing force pushes or deletions.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to know if you understand code quality enforcement and collaboration safety in GitHub.

Breaking it down:
- Navigate to repository Settings > Branches > Branch protection rules.
- You can specify the branch name pattern (e.g., `main`).
- Options:
  - Require pull request reviews before merging.
  - Dismiss stale reviews.
  - Require status checks (CI passes) before merging.
  - Require linear history (no merge commits).
  - Restrict who can push (allow only specific users/teams).
  - Prevent force pushes and deletions.
- Rules are essential for maintaining a stable main branch.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To protect a branch on GitHub, I go to the repository Settings, then Branches, and add a protection rule for the branch I want to protect, usually `main`. I enable options like requiring pull request reviews, ensuring status checks pass, and blocking force pushes. This prevents developers from pushing directly to `main` and ensures all changes go through code review and CI. It's a standard practice for team projects."

Example:
# Steps:
# GitHub repo → Settings → Branches → Add rule
# Branch name pattern: main
# Check "Require pull request reviews before merging"
# Check "Require status checks to pass before merging"
# Check "Do not allow force pushes"
# Click Save

Real-world use case: Every production repository has branch protection on `main` to enforce code quality and prevent accidental breakage.

Common mistakes:
- Not protecting the branch, allowing anyone to push directly.
- Setting rules too strict (e.g., requiring too many approvals) that slow down development.
- Forgetting to enforce status checks, so broken code can be merged.
- Not applying the rules to all relevant branches (like `develop`).

Interview tip: Mention that branch protection is part of a healthy CI/CD pipeline. Also mention that you can require signed commits for security.

---

Q56: What is the difference between `origin` and `upstream` in Git?

Direct answer: `origin` is the default name for the remote repository you cloned from (usually your own fork or the main repo). `upstream` is the conventional name for the main repository from which you forked, used to sync changes.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand the fork‑based workflow and remote management.

Breaking it down:
- `origin`: Points to your remote (your fork if you forked, or the main repo if you cloned).
- `upstream`: A conventional name for the original repository when you have a fork.
- You add `upstream` manually: `git remote add upstream <url>`.
- Use `origin` to push your changes, `upstream` to pull updates from the original.
- `git pull upstream main` syncs your local main with the original repo.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"In a typical fork workflow, `origin` is my fork – the remote I push to. `upstream` is the original repository from which I forked. I use `origin` to push my feature branches and open pull requests. I use `upstream` to fetch new changes from the original repository and merge them into my local branches to keep everything up‑to‑date. They're just conventions, but everyone understands them."

Example:
# After cloning your fork
git remote -v  # origin points to your fork
# Add upstream
git remote add upstream https://github.com/original/repo.git
# Fetch upstream changes
git fetch upstream
# Merge them into your local main
git checkout main
git merge upstream/main
# Push to your origin
git push origin main

Real-world use case: Open‑source contributors fork a project, add `upstream`, and regularly sync their fork to stay current with the main project.

Common mistakes:
- Forgetting to add `upstream` after forking.
- Pushing to `upstream` (you don't have permission).
- Confusing the two and pulling from the wrong remote.
- Not updating `upstream` regularly, leading to large conflicts.

Interview tip: Mention that `git pull upstream main` is a common command, and you often rebase instead of merge to keep history clean.

---

Q57: How do you sync a forked repository with the original?

Direct answer: You sync a fork by adding the original repository as `upstream`, then fetching and merging (or rebasing) `upstream/main` into your local branch, and pushing to `origin`.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to ensure you can keep your fork up‑to‑date, which is essential for open‑source contributions.

Breaking it down:
1. Add upstream: `git remote add upstream <original-url>`.
2. Fetch upstream: `git fetch upstream`.
3. Checkout your local main: `git checkout main`.
4. Merge upstream: `git merge upstream/main` (or rebase: `git rebase upstream/main`).
5. Push to your fork: `git push origin main`.
- You can also sync feature branches by rebasing them onto the updated main.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To sync my fork, I first add the original repo as `upstream`. Then I fetch from upstream, checkout my local `main`, and merge the upstream changes. Finally, I push to my `origin` fork. This keeps my fork in sync with the original project. I do this regularly before starting new work so I'm always working on the latest code."

Example:
# Add upstream (once)
git remote add upstream https://github.com/original/repo.git
# Sync
git fetch upstream
git checkout main
git merge upstream/main
git push origin main
# For feature branch
git checkout feature-branch
git rebase main   # or merge main

Real-world use case: Before submitting a new pull request, a contributor syncs their fork to avoid conflicts.

Common mistakes:
- Forgetting to add upstream.
- Merging `upstream/main` into a dirty working tree (commit or stash first).
- Not pushing the updated main to `origin`.
- Rebasing `main` and then force‑pushing (should not force push shared main).

Interview tip: Mention that GitHub also provides a "Sync fork" button on the web, but the command‑line method is more powerful, especially for feature branches.

---

Q58: What is a fork vs. a branch in GitHub?

Direct answer: A fork is a server‑side copy of a repository under your own account, while a branch is a lightweight pointer within a repository that allows parallel development. Fork is a copy; branch is a line of development.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to clarify the distinction between the two, as they serve different purposes in collaboration.

Breaking it down:
- Fork: Entire repository copy on the server, under a different namespace. Used when you don't have write access to the original.
- Branch: A pointer within the same repository. Used to isolate features/fixes.
- Forking is a GitHub (or platform) operation, not a Git command.
- Branches are Git features.
- Forks can be merged via pull requests back to the original; branches are merged within the same repo (or across forks).

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A fork is a copy of an entire repository on the remote server, under my GitHub account. It's a full copy. A branch is just a pointer to a commit within a single repository – it's lightweight and part of the same codebase. I use forks when I want to contribute to a project I don't have write access to. I use branches when I'm working on multiple features within a single repository that I have permission to push to. They serve different levels of isolation."

Example:
# Branch: within repo
git checkout -b feature/new-ui
# Fork: click "Fork" on GitHub
# Then clone your fork
git clone https://github.com/myuser/repo.git

Real-world use case: Open‑source: fork the repo, clone your fork, create a branch in your fork, work, push, and open a PR to the original repo.

Common mistakes:
- Thinking a fork is a Git command (it's a platform feature).
- Using a fork when a branch would be simpler (within the same team).
- Not keeping the fork in sync with upstream.
- Confusing the two in conversation.

Interview tip: Mention that forks are often used for open‑source, while branches are used for internal team collaboration. Also mention that some companies use forks internally for security isolation.

---

Q59: How do you submit a contribution to an open‑source project on GitHub?

Direct answer: You fork the repository, clone your fork, create a new branch, make your changes, commit, push to your fork, and then open a pull request from your fork's branch to the original repository.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand the standard open‑source contribution workflow.

Breaking it down:
1. Fork the target repository on GitHub.
2. Clone your fork locally: `git clone https://github.com/youruser/repo.git`.
3. Add upstream (optional but recommended): `git remote add upstream <original-url>`.
4. Create a branch: `git checkout -b my-feature`.
5. Make changes, commit, and push to your fork: `git push origin my-feature`.
6. On GitHub, open a Pull Request from your fork's branch to the original repo's branch (usually `main`).
7. Fill in the PR template, describe changes, link issues.
8. Wait for reviews, address feedback, push additional commits.
9. Once approved and CI passes, the maintainer merges.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To contribute to an open‑source project, I start by forking the repository on GitHub. Then I clone my fork locally, create a feature branch, make my changes, and commit them. I push the branch to my fork and then open a pull request to the original repository. I make sure to write a clear description, link any related issues, and respond to reviewer feedback. I also sync my fork with the upstream repo to avoid conflicts."

Example:
# Fork on GitHub, then
git clone https://github.com/myuser/repo.git
cd repo
git remote add upstream https://github.com/original/repo.git
git checkout -b fix-issue-123
# ... make changes ...
git add .
git commit -m "Fix issue 123: ..."
git push origin fix-issue-123
# Then open PR on GitHub

Real-world use case: Every open‑source contribution follows this workflow.

Common mistakes:
- Opening a PR from the `main` branch of your fork (should use a feature branch).
- Not following the project's contribution guidelines (coding style, commit message format).
- Forgetting to sync with upstream, causing conflicts.
- Not responding to review comments.

Interview tip: Mention that you always read the CONTRIBUTING.md and test your changes locally before opening a PR.

---

Q60: What is the difference between a draft pull request and a regular pull request?

Direct answer: A draft pull request is a PR that is marked as a work in progress and cannot be merged until it is marked as ready for review. A regular PR is ready for review and merging.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know about the PR lifecycle and when to use draft PRs.

Breaking it down:
- Draft PRs are useful for early feedback, sharing progress, or collaborating before the feature is complete.
- They show a "Draft" label and are not eligible for merging.
- CI checks may still run.
- You can convert a draft to a regular PR when ready.
- Regular PRs are subject to reviews and merge rules.
- Draft PRs help avoid accidental merging of incomplete work.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A draft pull request is a PR that is not yet ready for final review or merging. I use it when I want to share work‑in‑progress with my team for early feedback, or when I'm collaborating and want to show what I'm doing. It cannot be merged until I mark it as ready. A regular PR means the work is complete and ready for review and potential merging. Draft PRs are a great way to get input without committing to a finished product."

Example:
# On GitHub, when opening a PR, click "Create draft pull request"
# Later, click "Ready for review" to convert

Real-world use case: A developer starts a large feature and wants to show the API design to the team early. They open a draft PR.

Common mistakes:
- Leaving PRs in draft indefinitely (they get stale).
- Opening a draft when you could just ask for feedback via chat.
- Not converting to ready when it's actually ready.
- Using draft for minor changes that are ready.

Interview tip: Mention that draft PRs are great for early CI testing and for pair programming. Also mention that GitHub Actions can be configured to skip certain jobs on draft PRs to save resources.

---

Q61: You accidentally committed sensitive information (e.g., password) to a public repository. How do you fix it?

Direct answer: Immediately rotate the compromised secret, then remove it from Git history using `git filter-repo` (or BFG), force push, and communicate with your team. Also consider using GitHub's secret scanning and revoking the commit.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to know if you understand the severity and the proper remediation process for a security incident.

Breaking it down:
- First, invalidate the exposed secret (change password, revoke API key).
- Remove the file from history: `git filter-repo --path <file> --invert-paths` or `git filter-branch`.
- Force push the rewritten history: `git push --force --all`.
- Notify the team and any affected parties.
- Add the file to `.gitignore` to prevent future commits.
- Use GitHub's secret scanning to check for other leaks.
- Consider using `git filter-repo` instead of `git filter-branch` (deprecated).

How to structure your spoken answer:
"If I accidentally commit a secret, the first thing I do is rotate the secret – change the password or API key – because the commit is already out there. Then I remove the secret from the entire Git history using `git filter-repo` to purge it from all commits. I force push the cleaned history. I also add the file to `.gitignore` to avoid re‑committing. Finally, I inform my team and check if GitHub's secret scanning has detected anything. Never just delete the file in a new commit because the secret is still in history."

Example:
# Install git-filter-repo if needed
# Remove the file from all history
git filter-repo --path path/to/secret.txt --invert-paths
# Force push all branches
git push --force --all
# Also check .gitignore

Real-world use case: A developer accidentally commits `.env` with AWS keys. They follow this process immediately.

Common mistakes:
- Only deleting the file in a new commit (it's still in history).
- Not rotating the secret first (the compromised key is still valid).
- Using `git reset` which doesn't remove from history.
- Forgetting to force push all branches and tags.

Interview tip: Mention that GitHub has "Remove sensitive data" guides and recommends `git filter-repo`. Also mention that you should consider using pre‑commit hooks to scan for secrets (e.g., `git-secrets`).

---

Q62: How do you revert a commit that is already pushed?

Direct answer: Use `git revert <commit-hash>` to create a new commit that undoes the changes of the specified commit, and then push that new commit. This is safe for shared branches.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to reinforce the safe way to undo changes in public history.

Breaking it down:
- `git revert` applies the inverse changes of a previous commit.
- It creates a new commit with a message like "Revert 'original message'".
- It does not rewrite history; it adds to it.
- You can revert multiple commits: `git revert -n HEAD~3..HEAD`.
- After revert, push normally: `git push origin main`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To undo a commit that's already pushed, I use `git revert <hash>`. This creates a new commit that does the opposite of the original commit, effectively undoing it. I then push that new commit. This is the correct way to undo public commits because it doesn't rewrite history and won't disrupt my teammates. I would not use `git reset` in this case because that would require a force push."

Example:
# Revert a specific commit
git revert abc123
# A new commit is created
git push origin main
# Revert the revert if needed
git revert <revert-commit-hash>

Real-world use case: A buggy commit was deployed; you revert it to fix production quickly.

Common mistakes:
- Using `git reset` instead of revert on a shared branch.
- Forgetting to push the revert commit.
- Reverting a merge commit without specifying the parent (`-m 1`).
- Reverting a commit that has been reverted before (creates a cycle).

Interview tip: Mention that `git revert` can handle merge commits with `-m` and that you can revert a range. Also mention that reverting is not the same as rolling back; it's a forward fix.

---

Q63: How do you undo a commit locally but keep the changes?

Direct answer: Use `git reset --soft HEAD~1` to undo the last commit while keeping the changes staged, or `git reset --mixed HEAD~1` (default) to keep changes unstaged.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to check your understanding of `git reset` modes.

Breaking it down:
- `--soft`: Moves HEAD back, leaves the index (staged) and working tree as they were. Changes remain staged.
- `--mixed` (default): Moves HEAD back and resets the index to match, but leaves working tree changes. Changes become unstaged.
- `--hard`: Discards changes (dangerous).
- Use `--soft` if you want to amend or recommit with different changes.
- Use `--mixed` if you want to unstage and review changes.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To undo the last commit locally but keep my changes, I use `git reset --soft HEAD~1`. This removes the commit but leaves my changes staged, so I can amend them or commit again with a different message. If I want the changes to be unstaged, I use `git reset HEAD~1` (which is `--mixed`). Either way, my work is safe."

Example:
# Keep changes staged
git reset --soft HEAD~1
# Keep changes unstaged
git reset HEAD~1   # or git reset --mixed HEAD~1
# Both allow you to modify and recommit

Real-world use case: You committed too early and want to add more changes to the same commit. You soft reset, add changes, and amend.

Common mistakes:
- Using `--hard` and losing changes.
- Not specifying `--soft` and using the default `--mixed`, then wondering why changes are unstaged.
- Resetting more commits than intended.
- Resetting after pushing (requires force push).

Interview tip: Mention that `git reset --soft` is great for combining commits or fixing the last commit message.

---

Q64: How do you change a commit that is not the latest?

Direct answer: Use interactive rebase (`git rebase -i`) and change the action to `edit` (or `reword` for message only) for the commit you want to change. Then amend that commit and continue the rebase.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your ability to rewrite history beyond the latest commit.

Breaking it down:
- `git rebase -i HEAD~n` where n is far enough back to include the commit.
- In the editor, change `pick` to `edit` for the target commit.
- Git stops at that commit; you can now:
  - Change files: `git add .` and `git commit --amend`.
  - Change message only: `git commit --amend -m "new message"`.
- Continue: `git rebase --continue`.
- For message only, use `reword` instead of `edit` to save steps.
- This rewrites history and requires force push if pushed.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To change a commit that isn't the latest, I use interactive rebase. I run `git rebase -i HEAD~5` to go back far enough. In the editor, I change `pick` to `edit` for the commit I want to change. Git stops at that commit, I make my changes or amend the message, and then `git rebase --continue`. If I only want to change the message, I use `reword` instead. This rewrites history, so I only do it for commits that haven't been shared."

Example:
# Rebase last 5 commits
git rebase -i HEAD~5
# Change pick to edit for commit abc123
# Git stops at abc123
git add .
git commit --amend -m "Updated commit message"
git rebase --continue
# For message only, change to reword

Real-world use case: You realize a commit from three commits ago has a typo or missing file. You edit it.

Common mistakes:
- Rebasing commits that have already been pushed to a shared branch.
- Not resolving conflicts during the rebase.
- Using `edit` when you only need `reword`.
- Forgetting to continue the rebase after amending.
- Not being aware that rebase changes commit hashes for all subsequent commits.

Interview tip: Mention that you use `git reflog` as a safety net before rewriting history. Also mention that you can split a commit with `edit` and `git reset HEAD^`.

---

Q65: What should you do if you encounter a 'merge conflict' during a rebase?

Direct answer: You resolve the conflict like a regular merge conflict, then stage the resolved files and run `git rebase --continue` to continue the rebase. You can also abort with `git rebase --abort`.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to confirm you can handle the most common rebase difficulty.

Breaking it down:
- During rebase, Git applies commits one by one; if a patch fails, it pauses.
- Resolve the conflict: edit files, remove conflict markers.
- Stage resolved files: `git add <file>`.
- Continue: `git rebase --continue`.
- If you want to skip the commit: `git rebase --skip`.
- To abort and return to original state: `git rebase --abort`.
- The process is similar to merge, but you're replaying commits.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"When I hit a conflict during a rebase, Git stops and tells me which file has the conflict. I open the file, resolve the conflict, remove the markers, and stage it with `git add`. Then I run `git rebase --continue` to replay the remaining commits. If I'm unsure, I can `git rebase --abort` to go back to the state before I started the rebase. Resolving conflicts in a rebase is similar to a merge, but each commit is reapplied."

Example:
git rebase main
# Conflict in file.txt
# Edit file.txt, remove markers
git add file.txt
git rebase --continue
# To abort
git rebase --abort

Real-world use case: A developer rebases a feature branch on `main` and encounters conflicts that were introduced since branching.

Common mistakes:
- Forgetting to stage resolved files, then continuing (rebase stops again).
- Using `git commit` instead of `git rebase --continue` (wrong commit).
- Aborting the rebase too early and losing progress.
- Not resolving conflicts correctly and introducing bugs.

Interview tip: Mention that `git status` shows you the rebase state, and you can `git diff` to see what's in conflict. Also mention that using `git mergetool` can help visualise conflicts during rebase.

---

Q66: How do you force push after a rebase?

Direct answer: After a rebase that rewrites history, you push with `git push --force-with-lease` (or `git push -f` as a less safe alternative) to update the remote branch to match your local branch.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to ensure you know how to update the remote after history changes and understand the risks.

Breaking it down:
- Rebasing changes commit hashes, so a normal push is rejected because the histories diverge.
- `git push --force` overwrites the remote branch with your local branch, discarding any commits that aren't in your local branch.
- `git push --force-with-lease` is safer; it checks that your remote branch hasn't been updated by someone else.
- Use force push only on private feature branches, never on shared branches like `main`.
- Inform your team before force pushing to avoid confusion.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"After I rebase, I need to force push because the commit history has changed. I use `git push --force-with-lease` rather than a plain `--force` because it checks if the remote branch has been updated since I last fetched. If someone else has pushed, it will fail and I'll need to sync first. I only force push to my own feature branches, never to shared branches like `main`."

Example:
# After rebase
git push --force-with-lease origin feature-branch
# If you're sure and want to override (not recommended)
git push -f origin feature-branch

Real-world use case: A developer rebases their feature branch on latest `main` and needs to update their remote branch to open a clean PR.

Common mistakes:
- Force pushing to `main` or other shared branches.
- Using `--force` without `--with-lease` and overriding someone else's work.
- Forgetting to pull before force pushing when others have pushed.
- Not informing teammates, causing confusion.

Interview tip: Emphasize that `--force-with-lease` is the safer option and shows you understand collaboration etiquette. Also mention that you can set `push.default` to `simple` to avoid accidental pushes.

---

Q67: What is the difference between `git push --force` and `git push --force-with-lease`?

Direct answer: `--force` overwrites the remote branch without checking if it has been updated. `--force-with-lease` checks that the remote branch is still at the commit you expect before pushing, preventing you from overwriting others' work.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know safe force‑push practices.

Breaking it down:
- `--force`: Blindly overrides the remote branch, discarding any commits that are not in your local branch.
- `--force-with-lease`: Only pushes if the remote branch's current commit matches the commit your local repo is tracking (i.e., you've fetched the latest). If someone else pushed, it aborts.
- Use `--force-with-lease` as the default force push.
- It prevents the "force push race" where you overwrite a teammate's work.
- You can specify a ref: `git push --force-with-lease=origin feature-branch`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The difference is safety. `git push --force` will overwrite the remote branch regardless of what's there – it could delete someone else's commits. `git push --force-with-lease` is more careful; it checks that the remote branch is still at the version I fetched. If someone else has pushed new commits, it aborts and tells me to fetch first. I always use `--force-with-lease` to avoid accidentally losing my teammates' work."

Example:
# Safe force push
git push --force-with-lease origin feature-branch
# Blind force push (risky)
git push --force origin feature-branch

Real-world use case: You rebase a feature branch; you use `--force-with-lease` to update your remote fork safely.

Common mistakes:
- Using `--force` habitually and overwriting others' changes.
- Not understanding that `--force-with-lease` can still fail if you haven't fetched.
- Using `--force-with-lease` on `main` (should avoid force push entirely).
- Forgetting that `--force-with-lease` doesn't protect you if you force push to the wrong branch.

Interview tip: Mention that you can configure Git to default to `--force-with-lease` by setting `alias.pushf = push --force-with-lease`. Also mention that force pushing is acceptable on private feature branches but never on main.

---

Q68: How do you find out who deleted a file or a line?

Direct answer: Use `git log --diff-filter=D -- <file>` to find the commit that deleted a file, and `git blame <file>` to see who last modified each line. For deleted files, use `git log --oneline -- <file>` to see its history.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you can trace history, especially when files are missing.

Breaking it down:
- For a deleted file: `git log --diff-filter=D --summary` shows all deleted files.
- To see commits that touched a file: `git log -- <file>`.
- To find who deleted a specific file: `git log --diff-filter=D -- <file>`.
- To see the last changes before deletion: `git log -p -- <file>`.
- For a line in an existing file: `git blame -L <start>,<end> <file>`.
- For a deleted file, you can check out an older commit to see its content.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To find who deleted a file, I use `git log --diff-filter=D -- <file>`. This shows the commit that deleted it. If I want to see who last modified a specific line in an existing file, I use `git blame` on that file. For deleted files, I can also `git log -p` to see the full diff before deletion."

Example:
# Find who deleted a file
git log --diff-filter=D -- path/to/file.txt
# Blame a file
git blame src/app.js
# Blame specific lines
git blame -L 10,20 src/app.js

Real-world use case: A file is missing from the repo; you need to know who removed it and why.

Common mistakes:
- Using `git blame` on a deleted file (it doesn't work).
- Not using `--diff-filter` to filter for deletion.
- Forgetting to specify the full path of the file.
- Not checking the commit message for context.

Interview tip: Mention that `git log --follow` can track renames. Also mention that you can use `git show <commit>` to see the deletion diff.

---

Q69: You have a large repository and want to clone it faster. What can you do?

Direct answer: You can use a shallow clone (`--depth 1`), a partial clone (`--filter=blob:none`), or clone only a specific branch (`--branch`). You can also use `--single-branch`.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know strategies for handling large repos, which is common in enterprise settings.

Breaking it down:
- Shallow clone: `git clone --depth 1 <url>` – downloads only the latest commit.
- Partial clone: `git clone --filter=blob:none <url>` – downloads commits and trees but not blobs until needed.
- Single branch: `--single-branch` – clones only the default branch.
- `--branch <branch>` – clones a specific branch.
- These reduce network and disk usage.
- Later you can `git fetch --unshallow` to get full history.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To clone a large repository faster, I use a shallow clone with `--depth 1` to only get the latest commit. If I need history but not all file blobs, I use a partial clone with `--filter=blob:none`. I also use `--single-branch` to avoid fetching all branches. These options significantly reduce the amount of data transferred, especially in CI/CD pipelines where I only need the latest state."

Example:
# Shallow clone
git clone --depth 1 https://github.com/large/repo.git
# Partial clone
git clone --filter=blob:none https://github.com/large/repo.git
# Single branch
git clone --single-branch --branch develop https://github.com/large/repo.git

Real-world use case: CI pipelines clone repos often; shallow clones reduce build time.

Common mistakes:
- Using shallow clones for development where you need full history for debugging.
- Not realizing that some operations (like `git blame`) need full history.
- Forgetting to `--unshallow` when you need more history later.
- Using partial clone without Git version 2.19+.

Interview tip: Mention that GitHub supports partial clone natively. Also mention that Git LFS can help with large binary files.

---

Q70: What is a shallow clone and when would you use it?

Direct answer: A shallow clone downloads only the latest commit(s) of a repository, truncating history at a specified depth. It's used to save bandwidth and time when you don't need full history.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand performance optimizations for cloning.

Breaking it down:
- `git clone --depth <n>` downloads only the last `n` commits.
- Example: `--depth 1` gives only the latest commit.
- Shallow clones have limited history; you cannot `git log` far back or `git blame` on older changes.
- You can fetch more history later with `git fetch --depth=<new>` or `git fetch --unshallow`.
- Useful in CI, automated builds, and when you only need the latest code.
- Not recommended for full‑time development where you need history.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A shallow clone downloads only a limited number of recent commits, specified by `--depth`. For example, `--depth 1` gives just the latest commit. I use it in CI pipelines where I need to test the latest code but don't need full history. It's much faster and uses less disk space. If I later need more history, I can `git fetch --unshallow`."

Example:
# Latest commit only
git clone --depth 1 https://github.com/user/repo.git
# Last 5 commits
git clone --depth 5 https://github.com/user/repo.git
# Unshallow later
git fetch --unshallow

Real-world use case: A build server clones the repo to run tests; it only needs the latest version.

Common mistakes:
- Using shallow clones for development, then struggling with `git log` or `git blame`.
- Forgetting that shallow clones cannot push to a remote that has a deeper history.
- Not using `--depth` correctly (default is full).
- Using shallow clone on a repository that needs full history for `git bisect`.

Interview tip: Mention that shallow clones are perfect for GitHub Actions and CI. Also mention that you can combine with `--single-branch`.

---

Q71: What is a sparse checkout and how does it help?

Direct answer: Sparse checkout allows you to check out only a subset of files or directories from a repository, rather than the entire working tree. It helps when you only need a portion of a large monorepo.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of managing large repositories and monorepos.

Breaking it down:
- Enable sparse checkout: `git config core.sparseCheckout true`.
- Define the paths you want in `.git/info/sparse-checkout`.
- Then `git checkout` (or `git read-tree`) will only populate those paths.
- You can also use `git sparse-checkout` commands in newer Git (2.25+).
- Sparse checkout reduces the size of your working directory.
- It doesn't reduce the size of the `.git` folder; use partial clone for that.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Sparse checkout lets me check out only specific files or folders from the repository. I enable it with `git config core.sparseCheckout true`, then list the directories I want in the sparse-checkout file. This is useful in monorepos where I only need a subproject. It reduces the clutter and saves disk space on my working tree, although the full history is still downloaded."

Example:
# Enable sparse checkout
git config core.sparseCheckout true
# Specify directories
echo "src/project-a/" >> .git/info/sparse-checkout
# Checkout
git checkout main
# Newer syntax
git sparse-checkout set src/project-a

Real-world use case: A huge monorepo with multiple services; you only work on one service's directory.

Common mistakes:
- Not enabling sparse checkout before checking out.
- Confusing sparse checkout with partial clone (they are complementary).
- Forgetting to update the sparse-checkout file when you need more files.
- Using sparse checkout without Git 2.25+ for the better syntax.

Interview tip: Mention that sparse checkout is often combined with partial clone (`--filter=blob:none`) to reduce both the working tree and the repository size.

---

Q72: What is a partial clone?

Direct answer: A partial clone is a clone that defers downloading large blobs (file contents) until they are needed. Git fetches only commit and tree objects initially, downloading blobs on demand.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know the modern Git feature for handling large repositories.

Breaking it down:
- `git clone --filter=blob:none` downloads all commits and trees, but no blob contents (file data).
- `git clone --filter=tree:0` downloads only commits, no trees or blobs (even lighter).
- When you checkout a file or `git blame`, Git fetches the needed blobs.
- Supported by GitHub, GitLab, and others.
- Reduces initial clone time and disk usage.
- You can later fetch all blobs with `git fetch --full`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A partial clone is a Git feature where I only download the commit and tree information initially, and file contents (blobs) are downloaded on demand when I need them. I use `git clone --filter=blob:none` for large repos. This makes the initial clone very fast. When I check out a file, Git fetches its content then. It's different from shallow clone because I still have full history, just not all file contents."

Example:
# Clone without blobs
git clone --filter=blob:none https://github.com/large/repo.git
# When you need a file, it's fetched automatically
git checkout main

Real-world use case: A developer works on a huge monorepo with thousands of files. They only need a few files for their task.

Common mistakes:
- Not having Git version 2.19+.
- Using partial clone without a server that supports the filter.
- Not understanding that `blob:none` still downloads tree objects.
- Forgetting that `git checkout` may trigger blob fetches that take time.

Interview tip: Mention that partial clone is the future for large repos and is used by GitHub. Also mention that you can combine with `--depth` for even faster clones.

---

Q73: What is `git filter-repo` and how is it used?

Direct answer: `git filter-repo` is a powerful, recommended tool for rewriting Git history. It can remove files, rename paths, change email addresses, and more across all commits. It is faster and safer than `git filter-branch`.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know the modern way to rewrite history, especially for purging secrets.

Breaking it down:
- It's a Python script, not bundled with Git by default (but widely available).
- Common use cases: removing a sensitive file from all commits, splitting a repo, changing author info.
- Example: `git filter-repo --path secret.txt --invert-paths` removes that file.
- It rewrites history and changes commit hashes.
- After using it, you must force push all branches.
- It does not leave a backup by default; use `--force` to overwrite.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`git filter-repo` is a tool for rewriting Git history. I use it when I need to purge a sensitive file from the entire history or when I need to split a monorepo. For example, to remove a file, I run `git filter-repo --path secret.txt --invert-paths`. It's much better than the older `git filter-branch` because it's faster and handles edge cases. After rewriting, I force push all branches. I always ensure I have a backup of the repo before running it."

Example:
# Install: pip install git-filter-repo
# Remove a file
git filter-repo --path secret.txt --invert-paths
# Rename author email
git filter-repo --email-callback 'return b"new@email.com"'
# Split a repo
git filter-repo --path subdir/ --subdirectory-filter

Real-world use case: A company needs to remove an accidentally committed API key from all history.

Common mistakes:
- Not backing up the repository before running.
- Forgetting that it rewrites all commit hashes, breaking pull requests.
- Using `filter-branch` instead, which is deprecated.
- Not updating remotes after rewrite (force push needed).

Interview tip: Mention that GitHub recommends `filter-repo` over `filter-branch`. Also mention that you can use `--dry-run` to preview changes.

---

Q74: What is Git LFS (Large File Storage) and when would you use it?

Direct answer: Git LFS is a Git extension that replaces large files (like binaries, images, videos) with text pointers, storing the actual file content on a separate server. It's used to keep Git repositories small and fast when dealing with large assets.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know how to handle large binary files in Git, which is a common problem.

Breaking it down:
- Git LFS tracks files based on patterns (e.g., `*.psd`, `*.mp4`).
- LFS stores the file content on a remote server (LFS server), and Git stores a pointer file (few bytes).
- When cloning, you get pointers; the actual files are downloaded on checkout.
- It saves storage and speeds up cloning/pushing.
- Supported by GitHub, GitLab, Bitbucket.
- You need to install the LFS client and track patterns: `git lfs track "*.png"`.
- Push normally; LFS handles the backend.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Git LFS is used for large files like images, videos, or compiled binaries. Instead of storing the file in Git, it stores a small pointer and the actual file goes to a separate LFS server. I use it when my repository has binary assets that would bloat the repo. I set up LFS by installing the client and tracking patterns with `git lfs track "*.psd"`. Then commit and push as usual. It keeps the repo fast and the clone time reasonable."

Example:
# Install LFS
git lfs install
# Track image files
git lfs track "*.png"
git lfs track "*.jpg"
# Add .gitattributes
git add .gitattributes
# Commit and push
git add large-file.png
git commit -m "Add asset"
git push origin main

Real-world use case: Game development with many assets, or a mobile app with large APK files.

Common mistakes:
- Forgetting to install LFS and pushing large files to Git directly.
- Not tracking the file types before adding them.
- Assuming LFS is automatic; you need the client and `.gitattributes`.
- Not understanding that LFS has bandwidth and storage quotas on some platforms.

Interview tip: Mention that LFS is essential for design and game dev projects. Also mention that you can migrate existing large files to LFS using `git lfs migrate`.

---

Q75: What is the Git object model? Name the four types of objects.

Direct answer: Git's object model stores data as objects in a content‑addressable filesystem. The four object types are: **blob** (file content), **tree** (directory structure), **commit** (snapshot metadata), and **tag** (annotated reference).

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your deep understanding of how Git works internally.

Breaking it down:
- **Blob**: Stores the content of a file (not the filename). Each version of a file is a separate blob.
- **Tree**: Stores a list of filenames, modes, and references to blobs or other trees (directories).
- **Commit**: Stores a tree, parent commit(s), author, committer, timestamp, and message.
- **Tag**: Stores a reference to a commit, with metadata (tagger, date, message). Lightweight tags are not objects but refs.
- Objects are addressed by SHA‑1 hash of their content.
- Objects are stored in `.git/objects/` compressed.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Git's object model is based on four types: blobs, trees, commits, and tags. A blob stores the actual content of a file. A tree stores a directory structure, mapping filenames to blobs or other trees. A commit stores a tree, parent commits, author, and message – it's a snapshot. A tag is an object that points to a commit with extra metadata. Git addresses everything by SHA‑1 hash, making it a content‑addressable store."

Example:
# Find a blob hash
git cat-file -p <blob-hash>
# Find a tree
git cat-file -p <tree-hash>
# View a commit
git cat-file -p <commit-hash>
