Real-world use case: Understanding the object model helps with recovering data and debugging Git internals.

Common mistakes:
- Thinking a blob stores the filename (it doesn't; the tree stores that).
- Confusing tree and commit.
- Not knowing that tags are optional objects.
- Thinking Git stores diffs between files (it stores snapshots).

Interview tip: Mention that Git's object model is what enables its speed and integrity. Also mention that you can use `git cat-file -t` to see an object's type.

---

Q76: What is a SHA-1 hash and how is it used in Git?

Direct answer: SHA‑1 is a cryptographic hash function that produces a 40‑character hexadecimal string. Git uses it as a checksum to uniquely identify objects (commits, trees, blobs, tags) and ensure data integrity.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to know if you understand how Git identifies and verifies data.

Breaking it down:
- Git computes a SHA‑1 hash of the content and metadata of each object.
- The hash becomes the object's name (e.g., `abc123...`).
- Changes in content produce a completely different hash.
- Git uses these hashes for references (branches, tags point to commit hashes).
- It ensures integrity: if the content changes, the hash changes, so corruption is detected.
- Git is moving towards SHA‑256 for better security.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"SHA‑1 is a hashing algorithm Git uses to generate a unique identifier for every object. When I commit, Git takes the content, metadata, and parent info, runs it through SHA‑1, and gets a 40‑character hash. This hash is the commit ID. It guarantees that if the content changes, the hash changes. It's used for object addressing, branching, and verifying that data hasn't been corrupted."

Example:
# View a commit hash
git log --oneline
# Compute SHA-1 of a file
git hash-object file.txt

Real-world use case: Every time you see a commit hash, it's a SHA‑1 (or SHA‑256 in newer versions) of that commit object.

Common mistakes:
- Thinking SHA‑1 is used for encryption (it's a hash, not encryption).
- Confusing SHA‑1 with other hash algorithms.
- Not knowing that Git is transitioning to SHA‑256.
- Believing that two different contents can have the same SHA‑1 (possible but extremely unlikely).

Interview tip: Mention that Git uses SHA‑1 for object identification and integrity, and that the transition to SHA‑256 is ongoing. Also mention that you can use `git rev-parse` to see hashes.

---

Q77: How does Git store commits internally?

Direct answer: Internally, a commit is an object that contains a reference to a tree object (the snapshot), parent commit(s), author, committer, timestamp, and a commit message. It is stored compressed in `.git/objects/` with a SHA‑1 hash as its name.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand the underlying structure beyond high‑level commands.

Breaking it down:
- A commit object points to a tree object (the root directory).
- The tree points to blobs (files) and other trees (subdirectories).
- The commit stores metadata: author, committer, time, message.
- Objects are stored in `.git/objects/<first-two-hash-chars>/<rest-hash>`.
- They are zlib‑compressed.
- Branches are just files containing a commit hash in `.git/refs/heads/`.
- `HEAD` points to a branch or a commit.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Internally, a commit is an object that points to a tree object, which represents the state of the repository at that time. The commit also stores the parent commit(s), author, committer, timestamp, and message. Git compresses these objects and stores them in `.git/objects/` using the SHA‑1 hash. Branches are simply text files that store a commit hash. This structure is what makes Git fast and reliable."

Example:
# View a commit object
git cat-file -p HEAD
# View the tree it points to
git cat-file -p <tree-hash>
# Explore object store
ls .git/objects/

Real-world use case: Understanding this helps when recovering lost objects or debugging corruption.

Common mistakes:
- Thinking commits store diffs (they store snapshots via tree).
- Not knowing that tree objects are needed for the snapshot.
- Believing that branches are complex objects (they're just files).
- Thinking that `.git/` is just a black box.

Interview tip: Mention that you can use `git cat-file` to explore objects, which is a great debugging tool. Also mention that packfiles compress multiple objects together for efficiency.

---

Q78: What is the index (staging area) in Git?

Direct answer: The index, also called the staging area, is a binary file (`.git/index`) that stores a list of files and their hashes that will be included in the next commit. It represents the proposed next commit snapshot.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to confirm you understand the three‑state architecture (working tree, index, repository).

Breaking it down:
- The index holds the state of tracked files.
- When you `git add`, you update the index with the current version of the file.
- When you `git commit`, Git converts the current index into a tree object.
- The index allows you to craft a commit piece by piece.
- You can view the index with `git ls-files`.
- `git status` compares working tree vs index vs HEAD.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The index is Git's staging area – a file that holds the snapshot of the changes I'm preparing to commit. It sits between my working directory and the repository. When I use `git add`, I update the index with the changes. When I commit, Git takes what's in the index and creates a new commit. This lets me selectively choose which changes go into a commit, which is very powerful."

Example:
# View index content
git ls-files
# Show staged changes
git diff --cached
# Show changes between index and HEAD
git diff --staged

Real-world use case: You use the index to stage only specific files or even specific lines (`git add -p`) to create clean atomic commits.

Common mistakes:
- Thinking the index is just the working directory.
- Not using the index to stage partially.
- Forgetting that `git commit -a` skips the index (adds all tracked changes directly).
- Not understanding that the index can be reset without losing working changes.

Interview tip: Mention that the index is the reason Git allows partial staging. Also mention that `git restore --staged` unstages without deleting changes.

---

Q79: Explain the difference between a blob and a tree object.

Direct answer: A blob stores the content of a single file. A tree stores a directory structure, listing filenames and referencing blobs (for files) or other trees (for subdirectories). Blobs have no name; trees provide the names.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of Git's internal file storage model.

Breaking it down:
- Blob: Content‑only, no filename, no metadata. Identified by content hash.
- Tree: Contains entries like `100644 blob <hash> file.txt` or `040000 tree <hash> dir/`.
- The tree gives context (where a blob lives).
- Multiple files with the same content share the same blob (deduplication).
- Commits point to a tree (the root directory).

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A blob is just the raw content of a file – it doesn't know its filename or permissions. A tree is like a directory listing; it maps filenames and modes to blobs or other trees. So a tree gives the structure, and blobs give the content. This separation allows Git to store each file content only once, even if it appears in multiple places."

Example:
# Content of a blob
echo "Hello" | git hash-object -w --stdin
# View a tree
git cat-file -p <tree-hash>

Real-world use case: This architecture enables Git's efficiency – identical files are stored once.

Common mistakes:
- Thinking a blob stores the filename (it doesn't).
- Confusing trees with commits.
- Not knowing that a tree can be empty.
- Believing that trees store file metadata beyond mode.

Interview tip: Mention that this model is why Git is a content‑addressable filesystem. Also mention that `git ls-tree` shows tree contents.

---

Q80: How do you create a GitHub Action that runs on push to the main branch?

Direct answer: You create a YAML workflow file in `.github/workflows/` with an `on` trigger for `push` to the `main` branch, and define jobs (e.g., build, test) that run on a runner.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know how to automate CI/CD with GitHub Actions.

Breaking it down:
- Create file: `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (or any name).
- Define `on: push: branches: [main]`.
- Define a `jobs` section with a `runs-on` (e.g., `ubuntu-latest`).
- Add `steps` using actions (e.g., `actions/checkout@v3`, `actions/setup-node@v3`).
- Run commands like `npm install`, `npm test`.
- Commit and push; the workflow triggers automatically.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To create a GitHub Action that runs on push to main, I create a YAML file in `.github/workflows/`, like `ci.yml`. I set the trigger to `push` with `branches: [main]`. Then I define a job, specify the runner (e.g., `ubuntu-latest`), and add steps: check out the code, set up the environment, install dependencies, and run tests. After I push that file to the main branch, the action runs automatically."

Example:
# .github/workflows/ci.yml
name: CI
on:
  push:
    branches: [ main ]
jobs:
  build:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v3
      - uses: actions/setup-node@v3
        with:
          node-version: '18'
      - run: npm install
      - run: npm test

Real-world use case: Every push to `main` runs tests and linting to ensure the branch is always healthy.

Common mistakes:
- Placing the workflow file in the wrong directory.
- Using incorrect YAML syntax.
- Forgetting to check out the code (`actions/checkout`).
- Not setting up the correct environment for the language.
- Not handling secrets properly.

Interview tip: Mention that you can add multiple triggers (`pull_request`, `schedule`) and use matrix strategies for multi‑version tests. Also mention that you can cache dependencies to speed up runs.

---

Q81: What are GitHub Workflow triggers (events)? Name a few.

Direct answer: Workflow triggers (events) are GitHub Actions events that automatically start a workflow. Common triggers include `push`, `pull_request`, `schedule`, `workflow_dispatch`, `release`, and `issue_comment`.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand CI/CD automation and can set up workflows for different scenarios.

Breaking it down:
- `push`: Runs when code is pushed to a branch.
- `pull_request`: Runs when a PR is opened, synchronized, or closed.
- `schedule`: Runs at scheduled times using cron syntax.
- `workflow_dispatch`: Allows manual triggering from the GitHub UI.
- `release`: Runs when a release is published or edited.
- `issue_comment`: Runs when a comment is added to an issue or PR.
- `fork`: Runs when a repository is forked.
- Events can be filtered by branch, tag, or path.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"GitHub Actions workflows are triggered by events. The most common one is `push`, which runs when code is pushed to a specific branch. I also use `pull_request` to run tests on PRs, `schedule` for nightly builds, and `workflow_dispatch` for manual runs. You can filter events by branch, tag, or file paths to control exactly when the workflow runs."

Example:
# .github/workflows/ci.yml
on:
  push:
    branches: [ main, develop ]
  pull_request:
    branches: [ main ]
  schedule:
    - cron: '0 0 * * *'   # daily
  workflow_dispatch:      # manual trigger

Real-world use case: A team runs linting on every push, runs integration tests on every PR, and runs a full security scan nightly.

Common mistakes:
- Not filtering branches, causing workflows to run on every branch.
- Using `pull_request` without specifying branches, running on every PR.
- Forgetting that `schedule` uses UTC time.
- Not using `workflow_dispatch` for testing workflows.

Interview tip: Mention that you can use `paths` and `paths-ignore` to run workflows only when specific files change, which saves time.

---

Q82: What is a GitHub Actions matrix strategy?

Direct answer: A matrix strategy allows you to run a job multiple times with different configurations (e.g., different operating systems, language versions, or environments) in parallel, using a single job definition.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know how to test across multiple environments efficiently.

Breaking it down:
- Define `strategy.matrix` with key-value pairs (e.g., `node-version: [14, 16, 18]`).
- The job runs for each combination of the matrix values.
- You can combine dimensions: `os: [ubuntu, windows]` and `node: [14, 16]` gives 4 runs.
- Use `matrix.key` in steps to reference the current value.
- `fail-fast` (default true) stops all runs if one fails.
- `max-parallel` limits concurrent runs.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A matrix strategy runs the same job multiple times with different parameters. For example, I can test my code on Node.js versions 14, 16, and 18, or on Ubuntu and Windows. I define the matrix in the YAML, and GitHub runs each combination in parallel. This is perfect for testing compatibility across environments. I can also use `exclude` to skip certain combinations."

Example:
jobs:
  test:
    runs-on: ${{ matrix.os }}
    strategy:
      matrix:
        os: [ubuntu-latest, windows-latest]
        node-version: [14, 16, 18]
        exclude:
          - os: windows-latest
            node-version: 14
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v3
      - uses: actions/setup-node@v3
        with:
          node-version: ${{ matrix.node-version }}

Real-world use case: A library author tests their package on multiple Node versions and operating systems.

Common mistakes:
- Creating a matrix that is too large, causing long waits.
- Not using `exclude` to skip unsupported combinations.
- Forgetting that `fail-fast` stops all runs early, which might hide failures.
- Not using `matrix` values in step commands correctly.

Interview tip: Mention that you can use `include` to add specific combinations, and `${{ matrix }}` to reference the current values.

---

Q83: How do you create a release in GitHub from a tag?

Direct answer: You create a release by pushing a tag, then going to the GitHub "Releases" page and creating a new release from that tag. You can also automate it with GitHub Actions using `softprops/action-gh-release`.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to know if you understand the release workflow and automation.

Breaking it down:
- Push a tag: `git tag v1.0 && git push origin v1.0`.
- On GitHub: Click "Releases" → "Draft a new release".
- Choose the tag, add a title, description, and attach assets.
- Publish the release.
- Automation: Use a workflow triggered by `on: push: tags: - 'v*'` and use the action to create the release.
- Releases are linked to tags and show up on the repository main page.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"To create a release, I first push a tag to the repository. Then I go to the Releases page on GitHub, draft a new release, select that tag, write release notes, and publish. For automation, I set up a GitHub Action that triggers on tag pushes and uses an action like `softprops/action-gh-release` to automatically create the release, attach build artifacts, and publish."

Example:
# Push a tag
git tag -a v1.0 -m "Release 1.0"
git push origin v1.0

# Automated workflow .github/workflows/release.yml
name: Release
on:
  push:
    tags: [ 'v*' ]
jobs:
  release:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v3
      - name: Build
        run: npm run build
      - name: Create Release
        uses: softprops/action-gh-release@v1
        with:
          files: dist/*

Real-world use case: Every time a new version is tagged, a release is automatically created with the built artifacts.

Common mistakes:
- Forgetting to push the tag to remote.
- Not using a workflow and manually creating releases each time.
- Not attaching build artifacts to the release.
- Using a tag that doesn't exist yet.

Interview tip: Mention that you can use `release` event triggers to run additional jobs (like deploying) when a release is published.

---

Q84: How do you manage environment-specific secrets in GitHub Actions?

Direct answer: You use GitHub Environments to define environment‑specific secrets (e.g., DEV, STAGING, PRODUCTION). In the workflow, you reference the environment in the job, and GitHub loads the corresponding secrets and variables.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand secure deployment practices and environment separation.

Breaking it down:
- Create environments in GitHub: Settings → Environments → New environment (e.g., `production`).
- Add secrets to that environment (e.g., `DEPLOY_KEY`, `DB_PASSWORD`).
- In the workflow, add `environment: production` to the job.
- Use `${{ secrets.DEPLOY_KEY }}` in steps; it resolves to the environment-specific secret.
- You can also add protection rules (required reviewers, wait timer).
- Variables can also be environment‑specific.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"I use GitHub Environments to manage environment‑specific secrets. I create environments like `dev`, `staging`, and `production` in the repo settings, and add secrets to each. In my workflow, I specify the environment for the job. When the job runs, it gets the secrets from that environment. This way, I never expose production secrets in development, and I can enforce approval rules for production deployments."

Example:
# In workflow
jobs:
  deploy:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    environment: production
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v3
      - run: echo "Deploying to ${{ github.ref }}"
      - run: ./deploy.sh
        env:
          DEPLOY_KEY: ${{ secrets.DEPLOY_KEY }}

Real-world use case: Deploying to production requires a different API key and needs manual approval; staging uses lower-privilege keys.

Common mistakes:
- Not creating the environment first (secrets won't be available).
- Referencing `secrets.KEY` without setting the environment in the job.
- Forgetting that environment secrets override organization secrets if both exist.
- Not using protection rules for production.

Interview tip: Mention that you can use `environment` with `github.event.deployment.environment` for dynamic environments. Also mention that secrets are masked in logs.

---

Q85: How would you handle a situation where two developers have diverged branches and you need to get them back in sync?

Direct answer: You can use `git pull` (merge) or `git pull --rebase` to integrate the changes. If they are diverged significantly, you may need to manually resolve conflicts or communicate with the team to decide the integration strategy.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to assess your conflict resolution and collaboration skills.

Breaking it down:
- Diverged branches: Both have commits the other doesn't.
- Option 1: Merge – `git checkout <your-branch>` and `git merge <other-branch>`.
- Option 2: Rebase – `git rebase <other-branch>` to apply your commits on top.
- Both will require conflict resolution.
- Communicate: discuss which approach (merge vs rebase) the team prefers.
- For a team branch, use merge (preserves history). For a feature branch, rebase might be preferred.
- After resolution, test and push.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"When two branches diverge, I first communicate with the other developer to understand the changes. Then I choose between merging and rebasing. If we're working on a shared branch, I use `git merge` to preserve the exact history. If it's a feature branch being updated with `main`, I might rebase to keep a linear history. Either way, I pull the latest changes, resolve conflicts carefully, test, and push. Clear communication is key."

Example:
# Merge approach
git checkout feature
git merge main   # resolve conflicts
# Rebase approach
git checkout feature
git rebase main   # resolve conflicts

Real-world use case: Two developers worked on different parts of the same file; they need to integrate their changes.

Common mistakes:
- Not pulling the other branch before starting.
- Rebasing a shared branch (causes divergence for others).
- Not testing after resolving conflicts.
- Not communicating, leading to duplicate work.

Interview tip: Mention that you document the merge strategy in the team's Git workflow guide. Also mention that you use `git diff` to see what's changed before merging.
