Q217: What are the main principles of Object-Oriented Programming (OOP)?

Direct answer: The main principles are Encapsulation, Inheritance, Polymorphism, and Abstraction (often called the four pillars of OOP).

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to verify your foundational understanding of OOP, which is essential for any Java developer.

Breaking it down:
- Encapsulation: Bundling data (fields) and methods that operate on that data within a single unit (class), and hiding internal details. Achieved via access modifiers (private, protected).
- Inheritance: Creating a new class (subclass) from an existing class (superclass), inheriting fields and methods.
- Polymorphism: The ability to present the same interface for different underlying forms (e.g., method overloading and overriding).
- Abstraction: Hiding complex implementation details and showing only the essential features. Achieved via abstract classes and interfaces.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"OOP has four main principles. Encapsulation is about bundling data and methods together and hiding internal state. Inheritance allows a class to inherit properties from a parent class, promoting code reuse. Polymorphism allows objects to be treated as instances of their parent class, with the correct method being called at runtime. Abstraction is about hiding complexity and exposing only what's necessary. These principles make code more modular, reusable, and maintainable."

Example:
// Encapsulation
private String name;
public String getName() { return name; }

// Inheritance
class Animal { void sound() {} }
class Dog extends Animal { void sound() { System.out.println("Bark"); } }

// Polymorphism
Animal a = new Dog();
a.sound(); // Prints "Bark"

// Abstraction
abstract class Vehicle { abstract void start(); }

Real-world use case: All Java applications are built on these principles. Frameworks like Spring heavily use them.

Common mistakes:
- Not understanding the difference between abstraction and encapsulation.
- Forgetting that polymorphism includes both compile-time (overloading) and runtime (overriding).
- Using inheritance when composition would be better.
- Exposing internal fields directly without getters/setters.

Interview tip: Mention that these principles help achieve SOLID design and make code easier to test and maintain.

---

Q218: What is the difference between an abstract class and an interface?

Direct answer: An abstract class can have both abstract and concrete methods and can hold state (fields). An interface (pre-Java 8) only had abstract methods; since Java 8, it can have default and static methods, but it still cannot hold state (instance fields). A class can extend only one abstract class but can implement multiple interfaces.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand when to use each, which is a common design decision.

Breaking it down:
- Abstract class: Can have constructors, instance variables, and concrete methods. Used for a common base with shared state.
- Interface: Defines a contract. Can have default methods (Java 8+), static methods, and constants (public static final). Used for capabilities or behaviors.
- Multiple inheritance: A class can implement multiple interfaces but extend only one abstract class.
- Access: Abstract class methods can be protected; interface methods are public by default.
- Use case: Abstract class for "is-a" relationship; interface for "can-do" relationship.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"An abstract class is a class that cannot be instantiated and can have both abstract and concrete methods, as well as fields. An interface defines a contract—it only has method signatures (and default methods since Java 8) and constants. The key difference is that a class can extend only one abstract class but can implement multiple interfaces. I use an abstract class when I have a common base with shared state, and an interface when I want to define a capability."

Example:
// Abstract class
abstract class Vehicle {
    private int speed;
    abstract void start();
    void stop() { speed = 0; }
}

// Interface
interface Electric {
    void charge();
}

// Concrete class
class Tesla extends Vehicle implements Electric {
    void start() { /* ... */ }
    void charge() { /* ... */ }
}

Real-world use case: In Spring, `@Repository` is an interface, while `AbstractController` is an abstract class.

Common mistakes:
- Not knowing that interfaces can have default methods in Java 8.
- Using abstract classes when an interface would suffice.
- Forgetting that abstract classes can have constructors.
- Not knowing that a class can implement multiple interfaces.

Interview tip: Mention that you prefer interfaces for defining contracts and abstract classes for code reuse with shared state.

---

Q219: Explain the difference between `String`, `StringBuilder`, and `StringBuffer`.

Direct answer: `String` is immutable, `StringBuffer` is mutable and thread-safe (synchronized), and `StringBuilder` is mutable and not thread-safe, making it faster than `StringBuffer`.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of performance and thread safety in string manipulation.

Breaking it down:
- `String`: Immutable – every modification creates a new object. Good for constant strings.
- `StringBuilder`: Mutable – can append/insert without creating new objects. Not synchronized, so faster but not thread-safe.
- `StringBuffer`: Mutable and synchronized – thread-safe but slower.
- Use `StringBuilder` in single-threaded scenarios (most common).
- Use `StringBuffer` when sharing across threads.
- Use `String` when the value won't change.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`String` is immutable, so every time I modify it, a new object is created, which is inefficient for frequent changes. `StringBuilder` is mutable and not thread-safe, making it the fastest option for single-threaded manipulation. `StringBuffer` is mutable and thread-safe, but slower due to synchronization. I almost always use `StringBuilder` unless I am in a multi-threaded environment and need to share the buffer."

Example:
// String: creates new object
String s = "Hello";
s += " World"; // new object

// StringBuilder: efficient
StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder("Hello");
sb.append(" World");

// StringBuffer: thread-safe
StringBuffer sbuf = new StringBuffer("Hello");
sbuf.append(" World");

Real-world use case: Building complex SQL queries or JSON strings in loops.

Common mistakes:
- Using `String` in loops for concatenation (creates many objects).
- Using `StringBuffer` when `StringBuilder` is sufficient.
- Not knowing that `StringBuilder` is not thread-safe.
- Using `StringBuilder` inside a loop without clearing it.

Interview tip: Mention that the compiler sometimes optimizes `String` concatenation with `StringBuilder` under the hood, but explicit usage is better for complex cases.

---

Q220: What is the difference between `==` and `.equals()` in Java?

Direct answer: `==` compares memory references (for objects) or primitive values. `.equals()` compares the content or state of the objects, and can be overridden to define custom equality.

Why this is asked: This is a fundamental Java question that tests your understanding of object comparison.

Breaking it down:
- For primitives: `==` compares values (e.g., `5 == 5`).
- For objects: `==` checks if two references point to the same memory location.
- `.equals()` is a method in `Object`; by default, it does `==`, but classes like `String`, `Integer` override it to compare content.
- Always override `.equals()` (and `hashCode()`) for custom classes.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`==` is a reference comparison for objects – it checks if two references point to the same object. `.equals()` is a method that by default does the same, but it's often overridden to compare the state of the objects. For example, `String` overrides `.equals()` to compare the actual characters. I always use `.equals()` for content comparison and `==` only for primitives or when I explicitly want to check if two references are the same."

Example:
String s1 = new String("Hello");
String s2 = new String("Hello");
System.out.println(s1 == s2); // false (different objects)
System.out.println(s1.equals(s2)); // true (same content)

String s3 = "Hello";
String s4 = "Hello";
System.out.println(s3 == s4); // true (string pool)

Real-world use case: Comparing user input, validating database objects, and equality checks in collections.

Common mistakes:
- Using `==` for string content comparison.
- Forgetting to override `.equals()` for custom classes.
- Not overriding `hashCode()` when overriding `.equals()`.
- Using `.equals()` on null (throws NPE).

Interview tip: Mention the contract of `equals()` and `hashCode()`: if two objects are equal, they must have the same hash code.

---

Q221: What is method overloading and method overriding?

Direct answer: Method overloading is having multiple methods with the same name but different parameters within the same class (compile-time polymorphism). Method overriding is providing a specific implementation of a method in a subclass that is already defined in its superclass (runtime polymorphism).

Why this is asked: This tests your understanding of polymorphism and the difference between compile-time and runtime behavior.

Breaking it down:
- Overloading: Same name, different parameter list (type, number, or order). Return type can differ but is not part of the signature.
- Overriding: Same name, same parameters, same return type (or covariant) in a subclass. Must not reduce access (e.g., private to public is okay, but not public to private).
- Overloading happens within the same class or between parent/child; overriding happens between parent/child.
- Overloading is resolved at compile-time; overriding is resolved at runtime.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Overloading is when I have multiple methods with the same name but different parameters – it's compile-time polymorphism. For example, a `print(int)` and `print(String)`. Overriding is when a subclass provides a specific implementation of a method already defined in the parent class – it's runtime polymorphism. I use overloading for convenience (same operation, different inputs) and overriding to customize behavior."

Example:
// Overloading
class MathUtils {
    int add(int a, int b) { return a + b; }
    double add(double a, double b) { return a + b; }
}

// Overriding
class Animal {
    void sound() { System.out.println("Animal sound"); }
}
class Dog extends Animal {
    @Override
    void sound() { System.out.println("Bark"); }
}

Real-world use case: Overloading in Spring MVC (`@RequestMapping` variants), overriding in template methods.

Common mistakes:
- Confusing overloading with overriding.
- Not using `@Override` annotation when overriding.
- Changing method signature in overriding.
- Thinking return type is part of the overload signature.

Interview tip: Mention that `@Override` helps catch errors at compile-time if the method doesn't actually override anything.

---

Q222: What is the `static` keyword in Java?

Direct answer: `static` means that a member (field or method) belongs to the class itself, not to any instance. It can be accessed without creating an object of the class.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of class-level vs instance-level members.

Breaking it down:
- Static fields: Shared across all instances. Useful for constants (`public static final`).
- Static methods: Can be called without an instance. Cannot access non-static members directly.
- Static block: Executed once when the class is loaded. Used for static initialization.
- Static nested class: Inner class that doesn't have a reference to the outer class.
- Static methods cannot be overridden, but they can be hidden.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The `static` keyword means the member belongs to the class, not to any particular object. For example, a static variable is shared across all instances. A static method can be called without creating an object. I use static for utility methods (like `Math.sqrt`) or constants. A static block runs once when the class is first loaded. I'm careful not to overuse static because it makes the code less flexible and harder to test."

Example:
public class Counter {
    public static int count = 0; // shared
    public static void increment() { count++; }
}

// Usage
Counter.increment(); // no instance needed

Real-world use case: Singleton pattern, utility classes (`StringUtils`), constants.

Common mistakes:
- Using static methods to access instance fields (compilation error).
- Overusing static, leading to tight coupling.
- Thinking static methods are overridden (they are hidden, not overridden).
- Not making static fields `final` when they are constants.

Interview tip: Mention that static methods can be mocked with PowerMock, but it's harder, so use dependency injection for testability.

---

Q223: Explain the `final` keyword in Java.

Direct answer: `final` can be applied to variables, methods, and classes. A final variable cannot be changed, a final method cannot be overridden, and a final class cannot be extended.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand immutability and design constraints.

Breaking it down:
- final variable: Once assigned, it cannot be reassigned. For objects, the reference is immutable, but the object's state can change (unless it's immutable).
- final method: Cannot be overridden by subclasses.
- final class: Cannot be subclassed (e.g., `String` is final).
- final parameters: Cannot be reassigned within the method.
- final blank variable: Must be initialized in the constructor or static block.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`final` is used to impose restrictions. A final variable is immutable – it can be assigned only once. A final method cannot be overridden. A final class cannot be extended – that's why `String` is final, for security and performance. I use `final` for constants, to prevent method overriding, and to design immutable classes. It also helps with thread safety."

Example:
final int MAX = 10; // constant
final class String { ... } // cannot extend
class Parent {
    final void display() { ... }
}

Real-world use case: Immutable classes like `String`, `Integer`; constants in interfaces.

Common mistakes:
- Thinking final means the object is immutable (it only means the reference is final).
- Declaring a final variable without initializing it.
- Trying to extend a final class.
- Not using `final` on method parameters for clarity.

Interview tip: Mention that `final` can improve performance by allowing compiler optimizations.

---

Q224: What is the `super` keyword used for?

Direct answer: `super` is used to refer to the immediate parent class's fields, methods, or constructors. It's used to access overridden methods or parent class constructors.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of inheritance and constructor chaining.

Breaking it down:
- `super()`: Calls the parent class constructor. Must be the first statement in a constructor.
- `super.method()`: Calls the parent class version of an overridden method.
- `super.field`: Accesses a parent class field (when hidden by a subclass field).
- Super is used to avoid ambiguity and to invoke parent behavior.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`super` is a reference to the parent class. I use it to call the parent's constructor with `super()`, which must be the first line. I also use it to call overridden methods with `super.methodName()`, or to access parent fields. For example, if I override a method but still want to use the parent's logic, I call `super.method()` inside the overridden method."

Example:
class Parent {
    Parent(String name) { /* ... */ }
    void display() { System.out.println("Parent"); }
}
class Child extends Parent {
    Child(String name) {
        super(name); // parent constructor
    }
    void display() {
        super.display(); // call parent version
        System.out.println("Child");
    }
}

Real-world use case: Extending framework classes and adding custom behavior while preserving parent logic.

Common mistakes:
- Forgetting to call `super()` in a constructor (compiler adds default `super()` if no explicit call).
- Using `super()` after other statements (must be first).
- Using `super` in a static context (compilation error).
- Confusing `super` with `this`.

Interview tip: Mention that `super` is also used in method overriding to call the parent's implementation before or after adding custom logic.

---

Q225: What is a constructor in Java?

Direct answer: A constructor is a special method that is invoked when an object is instantiated, used to initialize the object's state. It has the same name as the class and no return type.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to confirm your understanding of object initialization.

Breaking it down:
- Same name as class, no return type.
- Can be overloaded (multiple constructors with different parameters).
- Default constructor: provided by the compiler if none are defined.
- Constructor chaining: calling one constructor from another using `this()`.
- Parent constructor is called first (via `super()`).
- Can have access modifiers (public, private, protected, package-private).

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A constructor is a method that initializes an object when it's created. It has the same name as the class and no return type. If I don't define one, Java provides a default no-argument constructor. I can overload constructors to create objects with different initial states. The constructor is the first thing called when I use `new`, and the parent's constructor is called first."

Example:
public class Person {
    private String name;
    public Person(String name) {
        this.name = name;
    }
    public Person() {
        this("Default"); // calling overloaded constructor
    }
}

Real-world use case: Setting up initial state in Spring beans, creating DTOs.

Common mistakes:
- Defining a constructor with a return type (it becomes a method).
- Forgetting to call `super()` when needed.
- Not overloading constructors for flexibility.
- Not understanding that a private constructor prevents instantiation.

Interview tip: Mention that constructors can also be used for dependency injection in Spring.

---

Q226: Can a constructor be `private`? What is the use of a private constructor?

Direct answer: Yes, a constructor can be private. It's used to prevent instantiation from outside the class, commonly in the Singleton pattern, utility classes, or factory methods.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of design patterns and object creation control.

Breaking it down:
- Private constructor cannot be called from outside the class.
- Singleton: prevent direct instantiation; provide a `getInstance()` method.
- Utility classes: prevent instantiation (e.g., `java.lang.Math`).
- Factory pattern: use a static method to create instances, enforcing controlled construction.
- Builder pattern: can use private constructors with public static builder.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Yes, a constructor can be private. I use it to control how objects are created. For example, in the Singleton pattern, I make the constructor private and provide a static `getInstance()` method. For utility classes, I make the constructor private so the class cannot be instantiated. It gives me full control over object creation."

Example:
// Singleton
public class Singleton {
    private static Singleton instance;
    private Singleton() {}
    public static Singleton getInstance() {
        if (instance == null) instance = new Singleton();
        return instance;
    }
}

Real-world use case: `Math` class, `Collections` utility, Singletons in Spring.

Common mistakes:
- Forgetting to provide a static method to create objects.
- Using reflection to bypass private constructor (can be done with `setAccessible`).
- Not making the class final to prevent subclassing (which could bypass).
- Private constructor without any way to create an object makes the class useless.

Interview tip: Mention that private constructors are also used in the Builder pattern to enforce construction via the builder.

---

Q227: What is a package in Java and why is it used?

Direct answer: A package is a namespace for organizing classes and interfaces into groups. It is used to avoid naming conflicts, control access, and make code more manageable.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand code organization.

Breaking it down:
- Packages are directories in the file system.
- Naming convention: reverse domain (e.g., `com.company.project`).
- Purpose: prevent naming conflicts, provide access protection (package-private), help with modularity.
- `import` statement: brings classes from other packages.
- `package` statement: must be the first line (except comments).

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A package is a way to organize classes and interfaces into logical groups, like `java.util` for utilities. It prevents naming conflicts – I can have a `User` class in `com.myapp.model` and another `User` in `com.myapp.dto`. It also helps with access control: package-private members are only accessible within the same package. I always follow the reverse-domain naming convention."

Example:
package com.example.model;
public class User { /* ... */ }

// In another class
import com.example.model.User;

Real-world use case: All Java projects use packages to structure code.

Common mistakes:
- Using flat directories without packages.
- Forgetting to declare package name (default package, which is discouraged).
- Importing with wildcards (`.*`) in large codebases (reduces readability).
- Not following the reverse-domain convention.

Interview tip: Mention that packages help with modularization in Java 9+.

---

Q228: What are primitive data types in Java? List them.

Direct answer: Java has 8 primitive data types: `byte`, `short`, `int`, `long`, `float`, `double`, `char`, and `boolean`.

Why this is asked: This is a basic question to ensure you know the fundamental types.

Breaking it down:
- Integer types: `byte` (8-bit), `short` (16-bit), `int` (32-bit), `long` (64-bit).
- Floating-point: `float` (32-bit), `double` (64-bit).
- Character: `char` (16-bit, Unicode).
- Boolean: `boolean` (true/false).
- Primitives are stored directly in memory, not as objects.
- Wrapper classes: `Byte`, `Short`, `Integer`, `Long`, `Float`, `Double`, `Character`, `Boolean`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"There are 8 primitive types. They are `byte`, `short`, `int`, `long` for integers; `float` and `double` for decimals; `char` for single characters; and `boolean` for true/false. They are not objects, so they are stored in stack memory and are faster than their wrapper counterparts. I use `int` for most numbers, `double` for decimals, and `boolean` for flags."

Example:
int age = 25;
double price = 19.99;
char grade = 'A';
boolean isActive = true;

Real-world use case: Every Java program uses primitives.

Common mistakes:
- Using `Integer` when `int` is sufficient (unnecessary boxing).
- Using `float` without `f` suffix (double is default).
- Using `char` for strings (use `String`).
- Not knowing the size of each primitive.

Interview tip: Mention that autoboxing/unboxing converts between primitives and their wrappers automatically.

---

Q229: What is type casting in Java? Explain upcasting and downcasting.

Direct answer: Type casting is converting one data type to another. Upcasting is casting a subclass to a superclass (implicit, safe). Downcasting is casting a superclass to a subclass (explicit, potentially unsafe).

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of inheritance and polymorphism.

Breaking it down:
- Upcasting: `Animal a = new Dog();` – implicit, safe because it's narrowing.
- Downcasting: `Dog d = (Dog) a;` – explicit, requires a check (`instanceof`) to avoid `ClassCastException`.
- Primitives: implicit widening (int to long), explicit narrowing (long to int with loss).
- Downcasting is needed to access subclass-specific methods.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Type casting is converting a variable from one type to another. Upcasting is moving a subclass to a superclass – it's always safe and implicit. For example, `Object obj = new String();`. Downcasting is moving a superclass to a subclass – it's explicit and can fail, so I always check with `instanceof` first. I use downcasting when I need to call subclass-specific methods."

Example:
// Upcasting (implicit)
Animal animal = new Dog();

// Downcasting (explicit)
if (animal instanceof Dog) {
    Dog dog = (Dog) animal;
    dog.bark(); // subclass method
}

Real-world use case: Collections (list of `Object`), frameworks, and generic code.

Common mistakes:
- Downcasting without `instanceof`, causing `ClassCastException`.
- Trying to downcast to an unrelated type.
- Not handling checked exceptions in primitive casting.
- Overusing downcasting (suggests poor design).

Interview tip: Mention that generics reduce the need for downcasting.

---

Q230: What is an array in Java and how is it different from an `ArrayList`?

Direct answer: An array is a fixed-size, contiguous block of memory that holds elements of the same type. `ArrayList` is a resizable, dynamic array implementation of the `List` interface, backed by an array.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know when to use each data structure.

Breaking it down:
- Array: fixed length, can hold primitives, no methods (only `length`), faster.
- `ArrayList`: dynamic size, holds only objects (autoboxing for primitives), has rich methods (`add`, `remove`, `get`), slower due to resizing.
- `ArrayList` is part of the Collections Framework, offering more flexibility.
- Arrays are best for fixed-size, performance-critical code.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"An array is fixed-size and can hold both primitives and objects. It has a `length` property. `ArrayList` is dynamic, resizes automatically, and holds only objects. `ArrayList` provides many convenience methods like `add`, `remove`, and `contains`. I use arrays when I know the size in advance and need performance; otherwise, I use `ArrayList` for flexibility."

Example:
// Array
int[] arr = new int[10];
arr[0] = 5;

// ArrayList
ArrayList<String> list = new ArrayList<>();
list.add("Hello");
list.get(0);

Real-world use case: ArrayList is used heavily in everyday coding; arrays are used in low-level or performance-critical code.

Common mistakes:
- Using arrays when dynamic sizing is needed.
- Not knowing that arrays don't have `remove` or `contains` methods.
- Using `ArrayList` with primitives (autoboxing overhead).
- Not initializing arrays properly.

Interview tip: Mention that `ArrayList` uses `System.arraycopy()` internally for resizing, which is efficient.

---

Q231: Explain the Single Responsibility Principle (SRP).

Direct answer: SRP states that a class should have only one reason to change, meaning it should have only one job or responsibility.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand the first SOLID principle and software design.

Breaking it down:
- A class should do one thing and do it well.
- If a class has multiple responsibilities, changes in one responsibility affect the other, making the class fragile.
- SRP leads to more maintainable, testable, and cohesive code.
- Example: A `Report` class that generates and prints a report violates SRP; split into `ReportGenerator` and `ReportPrinter`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"SRP means a class should have one job. If a class handles both data storage and business logic, a change in one area could break the other. I keep classes focused. For example, I separate a `User` class from a `UserRepository` and `UserService`. This makes the code easier to test and maintain because each class has a clear purpose."

Example:
// Bad: does both
class Report {
    void generate() {}
    void print() {}
}

// Good: separated
class ReportGenerator { void generate() {} }
class ReportPrinter { void print() {} }

Real-world use case: Layered architecture (Controller, Service, Repository).

Common mistakes:
- Putting too much logic in one class (God object).
- Not identifying responsibilities clearly.
- Over-engineering (splitting too much).
- Confusing SRP with "one method per class" (not true).

Interview tip: Mention that SRP is the foundation of clean architecture.

---

Q232: Explain the Open/Closed Principle.

Direct answer: The Open/Closed Principle states that software entities should be open for extension but closed for modification. You should be able to add new behavior without changing existing code.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand how to design for change.

Breaking it down:
- Open for extension: allow adding new features or behaviors.
- Closed for modification: existing code should not be changed when adding new features.
- Achieved through inheritance, interfaces, and polymorphism.
- Example: Instead of modifying a `Shape` class to add a new shape, extend via subclasses.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Open/Closed means I can add new functionality without altering existing code. I achieve this by using interfaces and abstract classes. For example, if I have a `Shape` interface with a `draw()` method, I can add `Circle` and `Square` without changing the `Shape` interface. This prevents bugs in existing code and promotes stability."

Example:
// Open/Closed
interface Shape { void draw(); }
class Circle implements Shape { public void draw() { ... } }
class Square implements Shape { public void draw() { ... } }

// New shape added without modifying existing code

Real-world use case: Strategy pattern, plugin architectures.

Common mistakes:
- Not using interfaces early.
- Adding `if-else` chains for new types instead of extending.
- Forgetting that modification is sometimes necessary (e.g., bug fixes).
- Over-engineering with too many abstractions.

Interview tip: Mention that this principle is key to robust frameworks.

---

Q233: Explain the Liskov Substitution Principle (LSP).

Direct answer: LSP states that objects of a superclass should be replaceable with objects of a subclass without affecting the correctness of the program. Subclasses should honor the behavior of the parent class.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand proper inheritance.

Breaking it down:
- Derived classes must extend the base class without changing its intended behavior.
- A subclass should not violate the parent's invariants.
- Example: `Square` extending `Rectangle` violates LSP because `setWidth` on a `Square` also changes height, breaking the `Rectangle` contract.
- LSP ensures substitutability.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"LSP means a child class can stand in for its parent class without breaking anything. If I have a `Bird` and a `Penguin` that cannot fly, substituting `Penguin` for `Bird` in a method that expects flying would break, so `Penguin` shouldn't inherit `Bird` if it overrides `fly` incorrectly. I design subclasses to honor the parent's contract."

Example:
// Violation
class Rectangle { void setWidth(int w) { ... } }
class Square extends Rectangle { void setWidth(int w) { ... } } // changes height too

// If code expects Rectangle, Square breaks.

Real-world use case: Frameworks like Spring rely on LSP for inheritance.

Common mistakes:
- Overriding methods that don't make sense in the subclass.
- Narrowing preconditions or strengthening postconditions.
- Using inheritance when composition would be better.

Interview tip: Mention that if a subclass doesn't fully satisfy LSP, consider composition instead of inheritance.

---

Q234: Explain the Interface Segregation Principle (ISP).

Direct answer: ISP states that no client should be forced to depend on methods it does not use. It's better to have multiple small interfaces than one large fat interface.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand designing lean interfaces.

Breaking it down:
- Split large interfaces into smaller, more specific ones.
- This prevents implementing classes from having empty methods.
- Improves maintainability and reduces coupling.
- Example: Instead of `Worker` with `work()` and `eat()`, split into `Workable` and `Eatable`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"ISP is about not forcing a class to implement methods it doesn't need. If I have a `Machine` interface with `print()`, `scan()`, `fax()`, a basic printer would have to implement `scan()` and `fax()` as no-ops. I split it into `Printable`, `Scannable`, `Faxable`. This keeps interfaces clean and classes focused."

Example:
// Bad
interface Worker { void work(); void eat(); }
class Robot implements Worker { void work() { ... } void eat() {} } // empty

// Good
interface Workable { void work(); }
interface Eatable { void eat(); }

Real-world use case: Java's `Readable`, `Writable`, `Closeable` are segregated.

Common mistakes:
- Having huge interfaces with many methods.
- Creating interfaces that are too granular (over-engineering).
- Not recognizing when to split.
- Using ISP when a single interface would suffice.

Interview tip: Mention that ISP helps with testing and mocking.

---

Q235: Explain the Dependency Inversion Principle (DIP).

Direct answer: DIP states that high-level modules should not depend on low-level modules. Both should depend on abstractions (interfaces). Also, abstractions should not depend on details; details should depend on abstractions.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand dependency management and decoupling.

Breaking it down:
- High-level (business logic) should not depend on low-level (database, network).
- Use interfaces to invert the dependency.
- This leads to loose coupling and easier testing.
- Example: A `UserService` depends on `UserRepository` interface, not on `MySQLUserRepository` implementation.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"DIP means I depend on abstractions, not concretions. My high-level service doesn't know about the database; it knows about a `UserRepository` interface. The concrete implementation depends on that interface. This allows me to swap out database implementations easily and mock dependencies in tests. It's a key principle in Spring, where we inject dependencies."

Example:
// High-level depends on abstraction
interface UserRepository { void save(User u); }
class UserService {
    private UserRepository repo;
    // injection
}

// Low-level depends on same abstraction
class MySQLUserRepository implements UserRepository { ... }

Real-world use case: Dependency Injection in Spring.

Common mistakes:
- Not using interfaces (tight coupling).
- Inverting dependencies incorrectly (creating unnecessary abstractions).
- Using DIP when not needed (over-engineering).
- Not using dependency injection frameworks.

Interview tip: Mention that DIP is the "D" in SOLID and is the foundation of the Spring framework.

---

Q236: What is the difference between composition and inheritance?

Direct answer: Inheritance is an "is-a" relationship where a subclass extends a superclass. Composition is a "has-a" relationship where a class contains an instance of another class. Composition is preferred over inheritance for greater flexibility and to avoid tight coupling.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand design trade-offs.

Breaking it down:
- Inheritance: child class inherits all methods and fields; can override. Strong coupling.
- Composition: class has a reference to another object; delegates behavior. Loose coupling.
- Composition is more flexible (can change behavior at runtime).
- Inheritance violates encapsulation (child knows parent internals).
- "Favor composition over inheritance" is a design maxim.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Inheritance is an "is-a" relationship. For example, a `Dog` is an `Animal`. Composition is a "has-a" relationship. For example, a `Car` has an `Engine`. I prefer composition because it's more flexible – I can swap the engine at runtime, and it doesn't couple me to the parent's implementation. Inheritance can break encapsulation and create fragile hierarchies. I use inheritance only when there's a clear "is-a" and the hierarchy is stable."

Example:
// Inheritance
class Animal { void eat() {} }
class Dog extends Animal {}

// Composition
class Car {
    private Engine engine;
    void start() { engine.start(); }
}

Real-world use case: Strategy pattern uses composition; framework base classes use inheritance.

Common mistakes:
- Using inheritance for code reuse instead of composition.
- Creating deep inheritance hierarchies.
- Not using delegation in composition.
- Thinking composition is always better (inheritance has its place).

Interview tip: Mention that inheritance is best for polymorphic behavior, composition for code reuse.

---

Q237: What is the `volatile` keyword?

Direct answer: `volatile` is a keyword in Java that ensures that a variable's value is always read from main memory, not from the CPU cache. It guarantees visibility of changes across threads, but does not provide atomicity.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of concurrency and memory visibility.

Breaking it down:
- In multi-threaded environments, each thread can cache variables locally.
- `volatile` forces reads/writes to main memory, ensuring all threads see the latest value.
- It prevents instruction reordering (happens-before guarantee).
- It does NOT replace synchronization for compound actions (e.g., `++`).
- Use for flags (e.g., `boolean running`) or simple state.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`volatile` is used for variables that are accessed by multiple threads. It ensures that when one thread updates the variable, other threads see the updated value immediately, because it's written to main memory instead of the CPU cache. I use it for simple flags, like `isRunning`. However, it's not atomic, so I don't use it for read-modify-write operations like `count++` – for those I use `AtomicInteger` or synchronization."

Example:
private volatile boolean running = true;

// Thread 1
while (running) { /* do work */ }

// Thread 2
running = false; // Thread 1 will see this immediately

Real-world use case: Thread control flags, double-checked locking (with caution).

Common mistakes:
- Using `volatile` for compound operations (e.g., `volatile int count; count++` is not atomic).
- Thinking `volatile` provides atomicity (it doesn't).
- Using `volatile` on 64-bit primitives (not needed in Java).
- Overusing `volatile` when synchronization is needed.

Interview tip: Mention that `volatile` provides a "happens-before" guarantee, which is part of the Java Memory Model.

---

Q238: What is polymorphism? Provide a real-world example.

Direct answer: Polymorphism is the ability of an object to take many forms. It allows a reference of a supertype to point to an object of a subtype, and the correct method is called at runtime (or compile-time for overloading).

Why this is asked: This tests your understanding of a core OOP concept.

Breaking it down:
- Compile-time (overloading): same name, different parameters.
- Runtime (overriding): subclass overrides parent method; correct one is called based on object type.
- Enables flexibility and code reusability.
- Example: A `Shape` reference can point to `Circle` or `Square`, and `draw()` calls the correct method.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Polymorphism means "many forms". In Java, I can have a variable of a superclass type that refers to a subclass object. When I call a method, the actual object's version is executed. For example, I have a `List` interface, and I can assign `ArrayList` or `LinkedList` to it. The `add` method behaves differently depending on the actual list, but my code doesn't care. This is runtime polymorphism."

Example:
// Runtime polymorphism
Animal animal = new Dog();
animal.sound(); // prints "Bark"

// Compile-time polymorphism (overloading)
void print(String s) { ... }
void print(int i) { ... }

Real-world use case: Collection framework (List, Set, Map), event handling.

Common mistakes:
- Confusing overloading with overriding.
- Not using polymorphism to simplify code (e.g., using `instanceof` checks).
- Not understanding that the actual method called is determined at runtime.
- Forgetting that static methods are not polymorphic.

Interview tip: Mention that polymorphism is key to designing extensible systems.

---

Q239: What is the difference between checked and unchecked exceptions?

Direct answer: Checked exceptions are checked at compile-time and must be handled (try-catch or throws). Unchecked exceptions (runtime exceptions) are not checked at compile-time and can be left unhandled.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of Java's exception handling mechanism.

Breaking it down:
- Checked: `IOException`, `SQLException`. Must be caught or declared in method signature.
- Unchecked: `NullPointerException`, `ArithmeticException`. Subclasses of `RuntimeException`. Can be handled but not required.
- Checked exceptions are for recoverable conditions.
- Unchecked are for programming errors.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Checked exceptions are checked at compile time, so the compiler forces me to handle them with try-catch or declare them with `throws`. For example, `IOException`. Unchecked exceptions are runtime exceptions like `NullPointerException` – I'm not forced to handle them. I use checked exceptions for recoverable conditions, like a file not found, and unchecked for bugs, like null references."

Example:
// Checked - must handle
try { new FileReader("file.txt"); }
catch (IOException e) { /* handle */ }

// Unchecked - not required
if (str == null) str.length(); // NullPointerException

Real-world use case: File I/O, network calls (checked); null checks (unchecked).

Common mistakes:
- Catching checked exceptions and doing nothing (swallowing).
- Throwing checked exceptions when the caller can't recover.
- Not using appropriate exception types.
- Overusing checked exceptions (Java's checked exceptions are often criticized).

Interview tip: Mention that in Spring, most exceptions are unchecked, which simplifies code.

---

Q240: What is the `finally` block and is it always executed?

Direct answer: The `finally` block is used in try-catch to execute code regardless of whether an exception is thrown or not. It is not always executed if the JVM exits (e.g., `System.exit()`) or there's an infinite loop.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of resource cleanup and guarantees.

Breaking it down:
- `finally` is used for cleanup (closing files, releasing resources).
- It runs after `try` and `catch` blocks, even if a return statement is present.
- It does NOT run if the JVM shuts down (`System.exit()`, `Runtime.halt()`).
- It does NOT run if there's an infinite loop or a fatal error (e.g., `OutOfMemoryError`).
- In Java 7+, try-with-resources is a better alternative for resource cleanup.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The `finally` block is guaranteed to run after a try block, regardless of whether an exception occurs or not. I use it to close resources like file streams and database connections. However, if I call `System.exit()` inside the try block, the JVM exits and finally does not run. Also, if there's an infinite loop or a severe JVM error, it may not run."

Example:
try {
    // some code
} catch (Exception e) {
    // handle
} finally {
    // always runs (unless System.exit)
    resource.close();
}

Real-world use case: Closing database connections, file handles, and cleaning up.

Common mistakes:
- Forgetting that `finally` can also throw exceptions (masking original).
- Not using try-with-resources for AutoCloseable.
- Assuming `finally` always runs in all circumstances.
- Returning values from `finally` (overrides previous returns).

Interview tip: Mention that try-with-resources is preferred over `finally` for resource cleanup.

---

Q241: What is the difference between `throw` and `throws`?

Direct answer: `throw` is used to explicitly throw an exception from a method. `throws` is used in a method signature to declare that the method might throw an exception.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to check if you understand exception propagation.

Breaking it down:
- `throw`: an action – used inside a method to throw an exception object.
- `throws`: a declaration – used in method signature to indicate possible exceptions.
- You can `throw` any throwable object.
- `throws` is mandatory for checked exceptions if not handled.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`throw` is a statement I use to actually throw an exception, like `throw new IOException()`. `throws` is a keyword in the method signature that tells the caller that this method might throw a specific exception. I use `throws` for checked exceptions, so the caller knows to handle them. `throw` is the action; `throws` is the declaration."

Example:
// throws declaration
public void readFile() throws IOException {
    if (file == null) {
        throw new IOException("File not found"); // throw action
    }
}

Real-world use case: Any method that can fail.

Common mistakes:
- Confusing `throw` and `throws`.
- Using `throws` with unchecked exceptions (not necessary but allowed).
- Throwing a checked exception without declaring it.
- Throwing an exception in `finally` (can mask original).

Interview tip: Mention that `throws` is part of the method contract.

---

Q242: What is a custom exception and why would you create one?

Direct answer: A custom exception is a user-defined exception class that extends `Exception` (checked) or `RuntimeException` (unchecked). You create one to provide more meaningful error details for your specific application domain.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you can design meaningful error handling.

Breaking it down:
- Extend `Exception` for checked custom exceptions.
- Extend `RuntimeException` for unchecked.
- Can include custom fields (e.g., error codes).
- Provides better error context than generic exceptions.
- Improves readability and maintainability.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"I create custom exceptions to make errors more specific to my domain. For example, instead of throwing a generic `Exception` when a user isn't found, I throw `UserNotFoundException`. This makes the error handling more precise and helps the caller understand what went wrong. I extend `Exception` for checked or `RuntimeException` for unchecked, depending on whether I want the caller to handle it."

Example:
public class UserNotFoundException extends RuntimeException {
    public UserNotFoundException(String userId) {
        super("User not found: " + userId);
    }
}

// Usage
if (user == null) throw new UserNotFoundException(id);

Real-world use case: Business rule validation, API error handling, service layer exceptions.

Common mistakes:
- Creating too many custom exceptions (over-engineering).
- Extending `Exception` when `RuntimeException` would be better.
- Not including meaningful messages.
- Not using exception chaining (cause).

Interview tip: Mention that in Spring, I often use `@ResponseStatus` with custom exceptions for REST APIs.

---

Q243: How does exception handling work in method overriding?

Direct answer: When overriding a method, the overriding method can throw fewer or narrower checked exceptions than the parent method, but it cannot throw broader or new checked exceptions.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of inheritance and exception rules.

Breaking it down:
- Overriding method can throw no exceptions if parent throws none.
- It can throw a subclass of the parent's declared exception.
- It cannot throw a superclass or a different checked exception.
- Unchecked exceptions can be thrown freely.
- This ensures the Liskov Substitution Principle.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The rule is that an overridden method cannot throw broader checked exceptions than the parent. For example, if a parent method throws `IOException`, the child method can throw `FileNotFoundException` (subclass) or no exception, but not `Exception` (superclass) or a different checked exception. Unchecked exceptions are not restricted."

Example:
class Parent { void read() throws IOException {} }
class Child extends Parent {
    // allowed: narrower or none
    void read() throws FileNotFoundException {} // OK
    // void read() throws Exception {} // NOT allowed
}

Real-world use case: Extending framework classes with specific implementations.

Common mistakes:
- Throwing a broader exception in the child class.
- Throwing a different checked exception.
- Not knowing that unchecked exceptions are always allowed.
- Forgetting the rule when implementing an interface.

Interview tip: Mention that this rule preserves polymorphic behavior.

---

Q244: What is the try-with-resources statement?

Direct answer: Try-with-resources is a feature introduced in Java 7 that automatically closes resources that implement `AutoCloseable` when the try block exits, ensuring proper resource cleanup.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know modern Java idioms.

Breaking it down:
- Resources declared in parentheses after `try` are automatically closed.
- Resources must implement `AutoCloseable` (or `Closeable`).
- It's cleaner than `try-catch-finally`.
- Resources are closed in reverse order.
- It supports multiple resources.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Try-with-resources automatically closes resources when the try block finishes. I declare the resource inside the parentheses, and Java takes care of calling `close()` for me. It's much cleaner than using `finally` and avoids boilerplate. I use it for file streams, database connections, and any `AutoCloseable` resource."

Example:
try (FileReader fr = new FileReader("file.txt");
     BufferedReader br = new BufferedReader(fr)) {
    String line = br.readLine();
} catch (IOException e) {
    // handle
}
// br and fr are auto-closed

Real-world use case: File I/O, network connections, JDBC.

Common mistakes:
- Forgetting that the resource must be `AutoCloseable`.
- Not handling exceptions from `close()`.
- Using try-with-resources with resources that need to be closed later.
- Not using it in older Java versions (pre-7).

Interview tip: Mention that try-with-resources is the best practice for resource management.

---

Q245: What is the Java Collections Framework? Name its core interfaces.

Direct answer: The Java Collections Framework is a unified architecture for storing and manipulating groups of objects. Its core interfaces are `Collection`, `List`, `Set`, `Queue`, `Deque`, and `Map`.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to ensure you know the backbone of Java's data structures.

Breaking it down:
- `Collection`: the root interface (not `Map`).
- `List`: ordered, allows duplicates (ArrayList, LinkedList).
- `Set`: no duplicates (HashSet, TreeSet).
- `Queue`: FIFO (PriorityQueue, ArrayDeque).
- `Deque`: double-ended queue.
- `Map`: key-value pairs (HashMap, TreeMap).

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The Collections Framework is a set of classes and interfaces for storing and manipulating groups of objects. The core interfaces are `Collection`, `List`, `Set`, `Queue`, and `Map`. `List` is ordered and allows duplicates; `Set` has no duplicates; `Queue` is for FIFO operations; `Map` stores key-value pairs. I use these daily in almost every project."

Example:
List<String> list = new ArrayList<>();
Set<Integer> set = new HashSet<>();
Map<String, Integer> map = new HashMap<>();

Real-world use case: Storing any collection of data.

Common mistakes:
- Using `Collection` where a specific interface is needed.
- Not knowing the difference between `List` and `Set`.
- Using `Map` when a `Set` would suffice.
- Not using generics.

Interview tip: Mention that the framework also includes utility classes like `Collections` and `Arrays`.

---

Q246: What is the difference between `ArrayList` and `LinkedList`?

Direct answer: `ArrayList` is backed by a dynamic array, providing fast random access but slow insertions/deletions in the middle. `LinkedList` is backed by a doubly linked list, providing fast insertions/deletions but slow random access.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of data structures and their trade-offs.

Breaking it down:
- `ArrayList`: O(1) get, O(n) add/remove in middle, O(1) amortized add at end.
- `LinkedList`: O(n) get, O(1) add/remove at ends, O(n) for middle (requires traversal).
- `ArrayList` uses less memory per element.
- `LinkedList` can be used as a stack or queue.
- Use `ArrayList` unless you frequently add/remove at the beginning or middle.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`ArrayList` is faster for random access because it uses an array under the hood, but inserting or removing in the middle is slow because it shifts elements. `LinkedList` is a doubly-linked list, so it's fast for adding and removing at the ends, but random access is slow because it has to traverse the list. I use `ArrayList` 90% of the time because random access is more common. I use `LinkedList` when I need a queue or stack, or many insertions at the beginning."

Example:
// ArrayList: fast get
List<String> list = new ArrayList<>();
list.get(5); // O(1)

// LinkedList: fast add to front
LinkedList<String> list2 = new LinkedList<>();
list2.addFirst("Hello"); // O(1)

Real-world use case: `ArrayList` is the most common list implementation.

Common mistakes:
- Using `LinkedList` unnecessarily (slower than ArrayList).
- Not knowing that `ArrayList` has O(n) for removing from the middle.
- Using `LinkedList` for random access.
- Not considering `ArrayDeque` for queue/stack operations.

Interview tip: Mention that `ArrayList` is almost always the better choice unless you have a specific need.

---

Q247: How does `HashMap` work internally?

Direct answer: `HashMap` works by storing key-value pairs in an array of buckets (nodes). It uses the key's `hashCode()` to determine the bucket index, and `equals()` to resolve collisions. When the load factor is reached, the array is resized (doubled).

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your deep understanding of one of the most used data structures.

Breaking it down:
- Bucket array: initial capacity 16, load factor 0.75.
- Hash: computes `hash = key.hashCode()` then `index = hash & (n-1)`.
- Collision: if multiple keys map to the same bucket, they are stored as a linked list (or tree after a threshold – Java 8+).
- Insert: calculates index, checks if key exists (via `equals`), updates or adds new node.
- Get: computes hash, finds bucket, iterates list/tree, returns value.
- Resize: when size > capacity * load factor, capacity doubles, and all elements are rehashed.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`HashMap` uses an array of buckets. When I put a key-value pair, Java calls `hashCode()` on the key to get an integer, then computes an index in the array. If there's already an entry at that index (collision), it's added to a linked list or tree. When retrieving, it finds the bucket, then uses `equals()` to find the right key. When the map gets 75% full, it resizes (doubles the array) and rehashes all entries."

Example:
// Simplified
int hash = key.hashCode();
int index = hash & (table.length - 1);

Real-world use case: Caching, counting occurrences, storing configuration.

Common mistakes:
- Not overriding `hashCode()` and `equals()` properly.
- Using mutable objects as keys.
- Not tuning initial capacity for large maps.
- Relying on order (HashMap doesn't guarantee order).

Interview tip: Mention that `HashMap` is not thread-safe; use `ConcurrentHashMap` for concurrency.

---

Q248: What is the difference between `HashMap` and `HashTable`?

Direct answer: `HashMap` is not thread-safe and allows one `null` key and multiple `null` values. `HashTable` is thread-safe (synchronized) and does not allow `null` keys or values. `HashMap` is faster and preferred.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of legacy and modern collections.

Breaking it down:
- `HashMap`: not synchronized, faster, allows nulls.
- `HashTable`: synchronized, slower, no nulls.
- `HashTable` is legacy (since Java 1.0); `HashMap` is part of the Collections Framework (Java 1.2).
- `ConcurrentHashMap` is preferred for thread-safe maps.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The main difference is that `HashTable` is thread-safe because its methods are synchronized, and it doesn't allow null keys or values. `HashMap` is not thread-safe, allows one null key and multiple null values, and is faster. I always use `HashMap` unless I'm in a multi-threaded environment, and then I use `ConcurrentHashMap`."

Example:
HashMap<String, String> map = new HashMap<>();
map.put(null, "value"); // allowed

HashTable<String, String> table = new HashTable<>();
table.put(null, "value"); // NullPointerException

Real-world use case: Modern applications use `HashMap` and `ConcurrentHashMap`.

Common mistakes:
- Using `HashTable` when `HashMap` is sufficient.
- Not knowing that `ConcurrentHashMap` is better for concurrency.
- Assuming `HashTable` is the only thread-safe map.
- Using `HashMap` in a multi-threaded context without external synchronization.

Interview tip: Mention that `HashTable` is obsolete; use `ConcurrentHashMap` instead.

---

Q249: What is the difference between `HashSet` and `TreeSet`?

Direct answer: `HashSet` is a Set implementation backed by a `HashMap`, providing O(1) performance and no ordering. `TreeSet` is backed by a `TreeMap`, providing sorted order and O(log n) performance.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand ordering and performance trade-offs.

Breaking it down:
- `HashSet`: unordered, faster (O(1)), allows null.
- `TreeSet`: sorted (natural order or comparator), slower (O(log n)), no nulls (due to compare).
- `HashSet` uses `hashCode()` and `equals()`.
- `TreeSet` uses `compareTo()` or `Comparator`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`HashSet` is fast but doesn't guarantee any order. It uses a `HashMap` internally. `TreeSet` keeps elements sorted according to their natural order or a comparator. It's slower but useful when I need sorted unique elements. I use `HashSet` for most cases, and `TreeSet` when I need order, like displaying a sorted list."

Example:
Set<String> hashSet = new HashSet<>();
hashSet.add("B"); hashSet.add("A"); // no order

Set<String> treeSet = new TreeSet<>();
treeSet.add("B"); treeSet.add("A"); // [A, B]

Real-world use case: `HashSet` for deduplication; `TreeSet` for sorted unique lists.

Common mistakes:
- Not knowing that `TreeSet` doesn't allow nulls.
- Using `TreeSet` when you don't need ordering.
- Not overriding `compareTo` for custom objects.
- Assuming `HashSet` iteration order is consistent (it's not).

Interview tip: Mention that `LinkedHashSet` preserves insertion order.

---

Q250: What is a `ConcurrentHashMap` and how does it differ from `HashMap`?

Direct answer: `ConcurrentHashMap` is a thread-safe implementation of `Map` that allows concurrent reads and writes without locking the entire map. It uses a segmentation mechanism (locking a portion, not the whole map) for better performance.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of concurrency utilities.

Breaking it down:
- `ConcurrentHashMap` is thread-safe, `HashMap` is not.
- It uses lock striping (multiple locks) or CAS operations to allow concurrent updates.
- It doesn't allow null keys or values.
- It provides better performance than `Hashtable` in multi-threaded environments.
- It supports aggregate operations like `computeIfAbsent()`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`ConcurrentHashMap` is a thread-safe version of `HashMap`. Unlike `Hashtable`, which locks the entire map, `ConcurrentHashMap` locks only a portion, allowing multiple threads to read and write concurrently. It's faster in a multi-threaded environment. I use it when I need a shared map in a concurrent application. It doesn't allow null keys or values."

Example:
// Thread-safe
ConcurrentHashMap<String, Integer> map = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
map.put("key", 1);
map.computeIfAbsent("key", k -> 2); // atomic

Real-world use case: Caching, shared state in web applications.

Common mistakes:
- Using `HashMap` in a multi-threaded context.
- Assuming `ConcurrentHashMap` is completely lock-free (some methods are).
- Using `ConcurrentHashMap` when thread-safety is not needed (overhead).
- Not knowing that it doesn't allow nulls.

Interview tip: Mention that `ConcurrentHashMap` is preferred over `Hashtable` for concurrency.

---

Q251: What is the `Comparable` and `Comparator` interface?

Direct answer: `Comparable` defines a natural ordering for a class (implemented by the class itself via `compareTo()`). `Comparator` is an external interface used to define custom ordering (implemented by a separate class) without modifying the original class.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of sorting and ordering.

Breaking it down:
- `Comparable`: single `compareTo()` method. Defines the natural order (e.g., `String` alphabetically).
- `Comparator`: multiple `compare()` methods. Defines custom order (e.g., sort by length, then alphabetically).
- `Comparable` is implemented by the class; `Comparator` is external.
- Use `Comparator` for multiple sorting strategies.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`Comparable` is implemented by the class itself, providing a natural order. For example, `String` implements `Comparable`, so I can sort strings alphabetically. `Comparator` is an external class that defines a custom ordering – I use it when I want to sort by a different field or multiple fields. I prefer `Comparator` when I need flexibility."

Example:
// Comparable (natural)
class Person implements Comparable<Person> {
    public int compareTo(Person other) { return this.age - other.age; }
}

// Comparator (custom)
Comparator<Person> byName = (p1, p2) -> p1.name.compareTo(p2.name);
List<Person> list = new ArrayList<>();
Collections.sort(list, byName);

Real-world use case: Sorting collections in Java.

Common mistakes:
- Not ensuring `compareTo` is consistent with `equals` (contract).
- Using `Comparable` when multiple orderings are needed.
- Forgetting that `Comparator` can be used with `TreeSet` and `TreeMap`.
- Not handling nulls in comparators.

Interview tip: Mention that you can chain comparators with `thenComparing()`.

---

Q252: What is `Collection` vs `Collections` in Java?

Direct answer: `Collection` is an interface that is the root of the collection hierarchy. `Collections` is a utility class with static methods for operating on collections (sorting, searching, synchronizing, etc.).

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of the framework's structure.

Breaking it down:
- `Collection`: interface, part of the hierarchy.
- `Collections`: final utility class, cannot be instantiated.
- `Collections` provides methods like `sort()`, `reverse()`, `synchronizedList()`, `unmodifiableList()`.
- Do not confuse the two; common interview trap.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`Collection` is the root interface of the collection hierarchy – it's implemented by `List`, `Set`, and `Queue`. `Collections` is a utility class that provides static helper methods to work with collections, like `sort()`, `reverse()`, and `shuffle()`. It also has methods for creating synchronized or unmodifiable collections."

Example:
// Collection interface
Collection<String> c = new ArrayList<>();

// Collections utility
Collections.sort(list);
Collections.synchronizedList(list);

Real-world use case: Using utility methods to manipulate collections.

Common mistakes:
- Confusing `Collection` and `Collections`.
- Trying to instantiate `Collections` (it's final with private constructor).
- Not knowing the utility methods available.
- Using `Collections.sort()` instead of `List.sort()`.

Interview tip: Mention that Java 8 added `List.sort()` which is often preferred.

---

Q253: What is a `TreeMap` and how does it work?

Direct answer: `TreeMap` is a Map implementation that stores key-value pairs in a Red-Black tree (a self-balancing binary search tree), maintaining keys in sorted order.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you understand sorted map implementations.

Breaking it down:
- Keys are sorted according to natural order or a comparator.
- Operations: `put`, `get`, `remove` are O(log n).
- It is not thread-safe.
- It does not allow null keys (if using natural ordering).
- Supports methods like `firstKey()`, `lastKey()`, `subMap()`.
- Backed by a Red-Black tree.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`TreeMap` is a sorted map implementation. It uses a Red-Black tree internally, so keys are stored in sorted order. Operations are O(log n). I use it when I need keys in a specific order, like a sorted list of students by ID. It doesn't allow null keys (unless a comparator allows). It also provides methods to get the first and last keys and sub-maps."

Example:
TreeMap<Integer, String> map = new TreeMap<>();
map.put(3, "C");
map.put(1, "A");
map.put(2, "B");
System.out.println(map); // {1=A, 2=B, 3=C}

Real-world use case: Sorted key-value storage, range queries.

Common mistakes:
- Using `TreeMap` when order is not needed (overhead).
- Using it with inconsistent comparator (breaks the tree).
- Not knowing that it has O(log n) performance.
- Using null keys without a custom comparator.

Interview tip: Mention that `LinkedHashMap` preserves insertion order, while `TreeMap` sorts.

---

Q254: What are the differences between `List`, `Set`, and `Map`?

Direct answer: `List` is an ordered collection with duplicates allowed. `Set` is an unordered collection with no duplicates. `Map` is a collection of key-value pairs, with no duplicate keys.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your fundamental knowledge of collection types.

Breaking it down:
- `List`: order preserved, indexed, allows duplicates (ArrayList, LinkedList).
- `Set`: no duplicates, order may be natural or insertion (HashSet, TreeSet).
- `Map`: key-value pairs, no duplicate keys (HashMap, TreeMap).
- `List` and `Set` extend `Collection`; `Map` does not.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`List` is an ordered collection that allows duplicates, and I can access elements by index. `Set` does not allow duplicates and has no specific order (unless it's a TreeSet). `Map` is not a Collection; it stores key-value pairs where keys are unique. I use `List` for sequences, `Set` for uniqueness, and `Map` for lookups."

Example:
List<String> list = new ArrayList<>(); // [A, B, B]
Set<String> set = new HashSet<>();    // [A, B]
Map<String, Integer> map = new HashMap<>(); // {A=1, B=2}

Real-world use case: Choosing the right collection is fundamental to programming.

Common mistakes:
- Using `List` when duplicates are not allowed.
- Using `Map` when you just need a key set.
- Not knowing that `Map` doesn't extend `Collection`.
- Forgetting about `Queue` and `Deque`.

Interview tip: Mention that you consider the data access patterns to choose the right collection.

---

Q255: What are the main features introduced in Java 8?

Direct answer: The main features are Lambda expressions, Stream API, Optional class, Date-Time API, default methods in interfaces, Functional Interfaces, and the new `java.util.function` package.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you are up-to-date with modern Java.

Breaking it down:
- Lambda expressions: functional programming.
- Stream API: pipeline operations on collections.
- Optional: to represent nullable values.
- Date-Time API (java.time): new date/time handling.
- Default and static methods in interfaces.
- Functional interfaces: `Predicate`, `Function`, `Consumer`.
- Method references.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Java 8 was a major release. It introduced lambda expressions, which allow me to pass behavior as data. The Stream API provides functional operations on collections. `Optional` helps handle nulls safely. The `java.time` API replaced the flawed Date/Calendar. Interfaces can now have default methods. These features make Java more expressive and functional."

Example:
// Lambda
list.forEach(item -> System.out.println(item));

// Stream
list.stream().filter(s -> s.length() > 5).collect(Collectors.toList());

Real-world use case: All modern Java applications use these features.

Common mistakes:
- Not using lambda expressions where they improve readability.
- Using `Optional` incorrectly (e.g., as a field).
- Not knowing about `java.time`.
- Using streams inefficiently (e.g., parallel streams inappropriately).

Interview tip: Mention that Java 8 is the baseline for most projects.
