Q256: What is a lambda expression in Java?

Direct answer: A lambda expression is an anonymous function that can be passed as a parameter to a method. It enables functional programming by providing a concise way to implement functional interfaces.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of functional programming in Java.

Breaking it down:
- Syntax: `(parameters) -> expression` or `(parameters) -> { statements; }`.
- Used with functional interfaces (single abstract method).
- Replaces anonymous inner classes for brevity.
- Examples: `(x, y) -> x + y`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A lambda expression is a shorthand for an anonymous function. I use it to implement functional interfaces like `Predicate`, `Consumer`, and `Function`. For example, instead of writing an anonymous class for `Runnable`, I write `() -> System.out.println("Hello")`. It makes the code more concise and readable, especially with the Stream API."

Example:
// Using lambda
List<String> list = Arrays.asList("a", "b");
list.forEach(s -> System.out.println(s));

// Instead of anonymous class
list.forEach(new Consumer<String>() {
    public void accept(String s) { System.out.println(s); }
});

Real-world use case: Streams, event handlers, and callbacks.

Common mistakes:
- Not knowing the target type (must be a functional interface).
- Using lambda when method reference is cleaner.
- Capturing variables incorrectly (must be effectively final).
- Overusing lambda in complex logic (readability suffers).

Interview tip: Mention that method references (`::`) are often preferred when the lambda is just calling an existing method.

---

Q257: What is a functional interface? Name a few examples.

Direct answer: A functional interface is an interface with exactly one abstract method. It can have multiple default or static methods. Examples: `Runnable`, `Callable`, `Comparable`, `Predicate`, `Function`, `Consumer`, `Supplier`.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of the foundation of lambdas.

Breaking it down:
- Single Abstract Method (SAM).
- Can be annotated with `@FunctionalInterface` (optional).
- Used as the target type for lambda expressions and method references.
- Examples from `java.util.function`: `Predicate<T>` (boolean test), `Function<T,R>` (apply), `Consumer<T>` (accept), `Supplier<T>` (get).

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A functional interface has only one abstract method. It's the target for lambda expressions. For example, `Runnable` has `run()`, `Callable` has `call()`. The `java.util.function` package provides common functional interfaces like `Predicate` for conditions, `Function` for mapping, `Consumer` for consuming, and `Supplier` for producing values."

Example:
@FunctionalInterface
interface MyInterface {
    void doSomething();
}

// Usage
MyInterface obj = () -> System.out.println("Hello");

Real-world use case: Stream operations, event handlers, callbacks.

Common mistakes:
- Adding more than one abstract method (compiler error if annotated).
- Not knowing the built-in functional interfaces.
- Using a functional interface where a regular interface would do.
- Forgetting that `Runnable` and `Callable` are functional.

Interview tip: Mention that `@FunctionalInterface` is optional but recommended.

---

Q258: What is the `Optional` class in Java 8?

Direct answer: `Optional` is a container that may or may not contain a value. It is used to avoid `NullPointerException` and make code more explicit about the possibility of null.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know how to handle nulls safely.

Breaking it down:
- `Optional.of(value)`: for non-null values.
- `Optional.ofNullable(value)`: for values that may be null.
- Methods: `isPresent()`, `ifPresent()`, `orElse()`, `orElseGet()`, `orElseThrow()`.
- Avoid using `Optional` as a field, method parameter, or in serialization.
- Useful for return types of methods that may not have a value.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`Optional` is a wrapper that explicitly communicates that a value might be absent. I use it as a return type to force the caller to handle the absence. For example, `Optional.ofNullable(user).orElseThrow(() -> new UserNotFoundException())`. It helps avoid `NullPointerException` and makes the code more expressive. However, I don't use it for fields or parameters."

Example:
Optional<String> opt = Optional.ofNullable(getName());
opt.ifPresent(System.out::println);
String name = opt.orElse("Default");

Real-world use case: Repository find methods, API response handling.

Common mistakes:
- Using `Optional.get()` without checking `isPresent()`.
- Using `Optional` as a field or constructor parameter.
- Using `Optional` for collections (use empty collection instead).
- Not using `ifPresent` or `orElse` correctly.

Interview tip: Mention that `Optional` is not meant to replace all null checks, just for return types where absence is meaningful.

---

Q259: Explain the Stream API in Java 8.

Direct answer: The Stream API is a functional approach to processing sequences of data, allowing declarative operations like filtering, mapping, and reducing, which can be performed sequentially or in parallel.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of Java 8's most powerful feature.

Breaking it down:
- Streams are not data structures; they are pipelines of operations.
- Operations: intermediate (lazy, filter, map, flatMap) and terminal (eager, collect, forEach, reduce).
- Streams can be created from collections, arrays, or generators.
- Parallel streams: `parallelStream()` for multi-threading.
- Streams are immutable; they don't modify the source.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The Stream API lets me process collections in a declarative way. I can filter, map, and collect data in a single chain. For example, `list.stream().filter(s -> s.length() > 3).map(String::toUpperCase).collect(Collectors.toList())`. Streams are lazy, so intermediate operations don't execute until a terminal operation is called. I also use parallel streams for performance on large datasets."

Example:
List<String> result = users.stream()
    .filter(u -> u.getAge() > 18)
    .map(User::getName)
    .collect(Collectors.toList());

Real-world use case: Data transformation, reporting, and aggregation.

Common mistakes:
- Using streams for simple operations (overhead).
- Modifying the source during stream operations (causes errors).
- Using parallel streams on small datasets or non-thread-safe sources.
- Not using `collect` with the right collector.

Interview tip: Mention that streams are a functional programming paradigm and improve readability.

---

Q260: What is the difference between `map` and `flatMap` in Streams?

Direct answer: `map` applies a function to each element and returns a stream of the resulting values (one-to-one). `flatMap` applies a function that returns a stream for each element and then flattens all those streams into a single stream (one-to-many).

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of stream operations.

Breaking it down:
- `map`: returns one output per input.
- `flatMap`: returns zero or more outputs per input, by flattening nested streams.
- Example: `map` turns `Stream<List<T>>` into `Stream<Stream<T>>`? Actually `map` turns `Stream<T>` into `Stream<R>`. `flatMap` turns `Stream<List<T>>` into `Stream<T>`.
- Used to flatten nested collections.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`map` transforms each element into another value – it's one-to-one. `flatMap` transforms each element into a stream, and then flattens all those streams into a single stream – it's one-to-many. I use `flatMap` when I have nested collections or optional values. For example, `listOfLists.stream().flatMap(List::stream)` flattens a list of lists into a single list."

Example:
// map: one-to-one
List<String> upper = list.stream().map(String::toUpperCase).collect(Collectors.toList());

// flatMap: one-to-many
List<List<String>> listOfLists = ...;
List<String> flat = listOfLists.stream()
    .flatMap(List::stream)
    .collect(Collectors.toList());

Real-world use case: Flattening nested lists, handling optional values, processing file lines.

Common mistakes:
- Using `map` when you need `flatMap` (result is a stream of streams).
- Not understanding that `flatMap` returns a stream of the inner elements.
- Using `flatMap` for simple transformations.
- Forgetting that `flatMap` can be used with `Optional.flatMap`.

Interview tip: Mention that `flatMap` is also used with `Optional` to chain optional values.

---
Q261: What is method reference in Java 8? Explain different types.

Direct answer: A method reference is a shorthand syntax for a lambda expression that calls an existing method. It uses `::` and is more concise when the lambda simply passes parameters to a method.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know when to use method references for cleaner code.

Breaking it down:
- Types:
  1. Static method reference: `ClassName::staticMethod`
  2. Instance method of a particular object: `object::instanceMethod`
  3. Instance method of an arbitrary object of a particular type: `ClassName::instanceMethod`
  4. Constructor reference: `ClassName::new`
- Method references are used where lambdas are expected (functional interfaces).
- They are more readable when the lambda body is just a single method call.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A method reference is a compact way to write a lambda when it just calls an existing method. For example, `list.forEach(System.out::println)` is a method reference to `println`. There are four types: static method reference (`Math::abs`), instance method of a specific object (`obj::method`), instance method of any object of a type (`String::length`), and constructor reference (`ArrayList::new`). I use them to make code more concise and readable."

Example:
// Static method reference
Function<String, Integer> parseInt = Integer::parseInt;

// Instance method reference on a specific object
List<String> list = Arrays.asList("a", "b");
list.forEach(System.out::println);

// Instance method reference on any object of the type
Function<String, Integer> length = String::length;

// Constructor reference
Supplier<ArrayList<String>> supplier = ArrayList::new;

Real-world use case: Replacing lambdas in Stream APIs, event listeners.

Common mistakes:
- Not knowing which type to use when.
- Using method reference when the lambda does more than one call.
- Confusing static and instance method references.
- Not handling overloaded methods (need to be specific).

Interview tip: Mention that method references can improve readability, and the IDE often suggests them.

---

Q262: What is the `default` keyword in interfaces?

Direct answer: The `default` keyword allows interfaces to provide concrete method implementations. This was introduced in Java 8 to support backward compatibility when adding new methods to interfaces.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of interface evolution.

Breaking it down:
- Default methods are fully implemented in the interface.
- Classes can override them if needed.
- They solve the "binary compatibility" problem.
- Multiple inheritance of behavior: a class can inherit default methods from multiple interfaces.
- If two interfaces have the same default method, the class must override it.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`default` methods let interfaces have method implementations. This was added in Java 8 so that I can add new methods to interfaces without breaking existing implementations. For example, `List` now has a default method `sort()`. A class can override the default if it needs different behavior. I use this for evolution and for providing common utility methods in interfaces."

Example:
interface Vehicle {
    default void start() {
        System.out.println("Vehicle starting");
    }
}
class Car implements Vehicle { }

Real-world use case: Adding methods to collection interfaces (e.g., `stream()`).

Common mistakes:
- Using default methods when an abstract class would be better.
- Not resolving diamond problems (multiple inheritance of same default method).
- Overusing default methods (makes interface messy).
- Forgetting that default methods are not `static`.

Interview tip: Mention that default methods are also used in functional interfaces for utility.

---

Q263: What is the `java.time` API?

Direct answer: The `java.time` API (introduced in Java 8) provides a modern, immutable, thread-safe date and time handling framework, replacing the flawed `java.util.Date` and `Calendar` classes.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know the modern way to handle date/time.

Breaking it down:
- Core classes: `LocalDate`, `LocalTime`, `LocalDateTime`, `ZonedDateTime`, `Instant`, `Duration`, `Period`.
- Immutable and thread-safe.
- Clear separation: date, time, date-time, and timezone.
- Provides extensive methods for formatting, parsing, and manipulation.
- Uses the ISO-8601 calendar system by default.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The `java.time` API is the modern date and time library. I use `LocalDate` for dates, `LocalTime` for times, and `LocalDateTime` for both. `ZonedDateTime` handles time zones. The classes are immutable, so they are thread-safe. I prefer this over the old `Date` and `Calendar` because it's much cleaner and less error-prone. It also provides methods like `plusDays()`, `minusMonths()`, and `isAfter()`."

Example:
LocalDate today = LocalDate.now();
LocalDate birthday = LocalDate.of(1990, Month.JANUARY, 1);
LocalDate future = today.plusDays(10);
Period period = Period.between(birthday, today);

Real-world use case: Any application that handles dates, like booking systems, logging, schedules.

Common mistakes:
- Still using `java.util.Date` and `Calendar` in new code.
- Not using `java.time.format.DateTimeFormatter`.
- Confusing `Instant` with `LocalDateTime`.
- Forgetting that `LocalDateTime` doesn't have timezone info.

Interview tip: Mention that `Instant` is useful for timestamps (e.g., for databases).

---

Q264: Explain the difference between `Stream.findFirst()` and `Stream.findAny()`.

Direct answer: `findFirst()` returns the first element of the stream, while `findAny()` returns any element. In sequential streams, they often return the same result, but in parallel streams, `findAny()` gives better performance.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of parallel streaming and performance.

Breaking it down:
- `findFirst()`: deterministic, returns first element in encounter order.
- `findAny()`: non-deterministic, returns any element (usually the first in sequential).
- In parallel streams, `findAny()` is optimized and may return different results.
- Use `findFirst()` when order matters; `findAny()` when you just need some element (e.g., for checking existence).

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The difference is determinism. `findFirst()` returns the first element in the stream's encounter order, which is predictable. `findAny()` can return any element, which is faster in parallel streams because it doesn't need to enforce order. I use `findFirst()` when I need the first result, and `findAny()` when I just need any element, like checking if any item matches a predicate."

Example:
Optional<Integer> first = list.stream().findFirst();   // first element
Optional<Integer> any = list.parallelStream().findAny(); // may be any

Real-world use case: Search operations where order doesn't matter.

Common mistakes:
- Using `findFirst()` in parallel streams when performance matters.
- Assuming `findAny()` always returns the same result.
- Not handling the `Optional` properly.
- Using `findAny()` on a sequential stream without understanding it's still deterministic (usually first).

Interview tip: Mention that `findAny()` is more efficient for parallel streams.

---

Q265: What is a `Collector` in Streams? Name a few common ones.

Direct answer: A `Collector` is a terminal operation that accumulates stream elements into a result container, such as a List, Set, Map, or a String. Common collectors are `toList()`, `toSet()`, `toMap()`, `joining()`, and `groupingBy()`.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of stream termination and aggregation.

Breaking it down:
- `Collectors` is a utility class with static methods for common collectors.
- `toList()`: collects into a List.
- `toSet()`: collects into a Set.
- `toMap()`: collects into a Map with key and value mapping.
- `joining()`: concatenates strings.
- `groupingBy()`: groups elements by a classifier function.
- `summarizingInt()`: computes summary statistics.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A collector is used to accumulate the elements of a stream into a container. I use `Collectors.toList()` to get a List, `Collectors.toSet()` for a Set, and `Collectors.toMap()` for a Map. For strings, `Collectors.joining()` is handy. `Collectors.groupingBy()` allows me to group elements by a key. There are also collectors for averages, sums, and summaries."

Example:
List<String> list = stream.collect(Collectors.toList());
String joined = stream.collect(Collectors.joining(", "));
Map<Integer, List<Person>> byAge = people.stream().collect(Collectors.groupingBy(Person::getAge));

Real-world use case: Converting streams to collections, reporting, and aggregation.

Common mistakes:
- Not importing `Collectors` statically for readability.
- Using `toList()` when you need a specific list type (use `toCollection`).
- Not handling duplicate keys in `toMap()` (provide a merge function).
- Using collectors inside parallel streams incorrectly.

Interview tip: Mention that `collect` is a terminal operation; you can create custom collectors.

---

Q266: What is the `Predicate` functional interface?

Direct answer: `Predicate<T>` is a functional interface that represents a boolean-valued function of one argument. It has a method `test(T t)` and also default methods like `and`, `or`, and `negate` for logical composition.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of the functional interfaces in `java.util.function`.

Breaking it down:
- Single abstract method: `boolean test(T t)`.
- Used primarily for filtering in streams.
- Default methods: `and(Predicate)`, `or(Predicate)`, `negate()`.
- Static method: `isEqual(Object targetRef)`.
- Commonly used with `filter()`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`Predicate` is a functional interface that takes one argument and returns a boolean. I use it in `Stream.filter()` to decide which elements to include. For example, `predicate = s -> s.length() > 5`. I can also chain predicates using `and`, `or`, and `negate` for complex conditions. It's a fundamental building block for functional programming."

Example:
Predicate<String> longString = s -> s.length() > 5;
Predicate<String> startsWithA = s -> s.startsWith("A");
Predicate<String> combined = longString.and(startsWithA);
list.stream().filter(combined).collect(Collectors.toList());

Real-world use case: Filtering collections, validation, and conditional logic.

Common mistakes:
- Not using `and`/`or` for readability.
- Creating predicates that throw exceptions.
- Using `Predicate` where a lambda would be simpler.
- Forgetting that `Predicate` is a functional interface.

Interview tip: Mention that you can also use `Predicate.not()` (Java 11) for negation.

---

Q267: What is the `Consumer` functional interface?

Direct answer: `Consumer<T>` is a functional interface that represents an operation that accepts a single input argument and returns no result. It has a method `accept(T t)` and a default method `andThen(Consumer)` for chaining.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know the other common functional interfaces.

Breaking it down:
- Single abstract method: `void accept(T t)`.
- Used for side-effect operations, like `forEach()`.
- Default method: `andThen(Consumer)` to chain two consumers.
- Also has a primitive variant `IntConsumer`, `LongConsumer`, etc.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`Consumer` takes an argument and does something with it, but returns nothing. I use it with `forEach` to perform an action on each element. For example, `list.forEach(System.out::println)` uses a `Consumer`. I can also chain consumers with `andThen` if I need to perform multiple actions."

Example:
Consumer<String> print = s -> System.out.println(s);
Consumer<String> log = s -> System.out.println("Log: " + s);
Consumer<String> combined = print.andThen(log);
list.forEach(combined);

Real-world use case: Logging, updating UI, saving to database.

Common mistakes:
- Not remembering that `Consumer` returns `void`.
- Using `Consumer` for operations that should return a value.
- Not handling exceptions in `accept`.
- Using `andThen` incorrectly (order matters).

Interview tip: Mention that `BiConsumer` exists for two arguments.

---

Q268: What are the different ways to create a thread in Java?

Direct answer: There are three ways: extending `Thread` class, implementing `Runnable` interface, and implementing `Callable` interface (used with `Future`/`ExecutorService`). The preferred way is to implement `Runnable` or `Callable` to avoid the limitations of multiple inheritance.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your basic concurrency knowledge.

Breaking it down:
- Extend `Thread`: `class MyThread extends Thread { public void run() { ... } }`
- Implement `Runnable`: `class MyRunnable implements Runnable { public void run() { ... } }`
- Implement `Callable`: `class MyCallable implements Callable<T> { public T call() throws Exception { ... } }`
- `Callable` can return a result and throw exceptions.
- `ExecutorService` is the modern way to manage threads.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"There are three ways. I can extend `Thread` and override `run()`. I can implement `Runnable` and pass it to a `Thread`. I can also implement `Callable` for tasks that return a result, used with an `ExecutorService`. I prefer implementing `Runnable` or `Callable` because I can still extend another class, and I use `ExecutorService` for better thread management."

Example:
// Extend Thread
class MyThread extends Thread { public void run() { ... } }
new MyThread().start();

// Implement Runnable
Runnable task = () -> System.out.println("Hello");
new Thread(task).start();

// Callable with Executor
ExecutorService es = Executors.newSingleThreadExecutor();
Future<String> future = es.submit(() -> "Hello");

Real-world use case: Background tasks, parallel processing.

Common mistakes:
- Starting a thread by calling `run()` instead of `start()`.
- Not using `ExecutorService` for larger applications.
- Not handling exceptions in `Callable`.
- Extending `Thread` when implementing `Runnable` is better.

Interview tip: Mention that using `CompletableFuture` is the modern alternative.

---

Q269: What is the difference between `Runnable` and `Callable`?

Direct answer: `Runnable` has a `run()` method that returns `void` and cannot throw checked exceptions. `Callable` has a `call()` method that returns a value and can throw checked exceptions.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of task interfaces.

Breaking it down:
- `Runnable`: no return value, no checked exceptions.
- `Callable`: returns a value, can throw checked exceptions.
- `Callable` is used with `ExecutorService.submit()` which returns a `Future`.
- `Runnable` can be submitted to `ExecutorService` but `Future.get()` returns null.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The main difference is that `Callable` returns a result and can throw checked exceptions, while `Runnable` returns nothing and cannot throw checked exceptions. I use `Callable` when I need to get a result back from a background task, like a computation. `Runnable` is for fire-and-forget tasks. I use `Callable` with `ExecutorService` and `Future`."

Example:
// Runnable
Runnable r = () -> System.out.println("Hello");

// Callable
Callable<Integer> c = () -> 42;
Future<Integer> future = executor.submit(c);
Integer result = future.get(); // blocks

Real-world use case: Asynchronous computations, batch processing.

Common mistakes:
- Using `Runnable` when you need a return value.
- Not handling `Future.get()` exceptions.
- Forgetting that `Callable` can throw checked exceptions.
- Not using `Callable` with an executor.

Interview tip: Mention that `CompletableFuture` is now preferred for chaining asynchronous tasks.

---

Q270: What is the lifecycle of a thread in Java?

Direct answer: The thread lifecycle has states: NEW, RUNNABLE, BLOCKED, WAITING, TIMED_WAITING, and TERMINATED.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of thread states.

Breaking it down:
- NEW: thread created but not started.
- RUNNABLE: thread is running or ready to run.
- BLOCKED: waiting for a monitor lock.
- WAITING: waiting indefinitely (e.g., `wait()`, `join()`).
- TIMED_WAITING: waiting with a timeout (e.g., `sleep()`).
- TERMINATED: thread has finished.
- State transitions occur via `start()`, `sleep()`, `wait()`, `notify()`, `join()`, etc.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A thread starts in NEW state. After `start()`, it moves to RUNNABLE. It can become BLOCKED if waiting for a lock, WAITING if `wait()` is called, or TIMED_WAITING if `sleep()` is called. Eventually, it finishes and becomes TERMINATED. I can check the state using `getState()`."

Example:
Thread t = new Thread(() -> {});
System.out.println(t.getState()); // NEW
t.start();
System.out.println(t.getState()); // RUNNABLE

Real-world use case: Debugging, monitoring thread pools.

Common mistakes:
- Not understanding that RUNNABLE includes both running and ready states.
- Confusing WAITING and BLOCKED.
- Not handling thread termination properly.
- Using `stop()` (deprecated) to terminate threads.

Interview tip: Mention that you use `Thread.join()` to wait for a thread to finish.

---

Q271: What is the `synchronized` keyword used for?

Direct answer: `synchronized` is used to create a critical section by locking a monitor, ensuring that only one thread can execute the block or method at a time, thus preventing data inconsistency.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of thread safety.

Breaking it down:
- Can be used on methods or blocks.
- On method: locks the instance (for non-static) or class (for static).
- On block: locks a specified object.
- It provides mutual exclusion (no two threads run simultaneously) and visibility (changes are flushed to main memory).
- It is reentrant: a thread can acquire the same lock multiple times.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`synchronized` is a keyword that makes a method or block thread-safe by locking a monitor. Only one thread can hold the lock at a time. I use it to protect shared mutable state, like a counter or a collection. For example, `synchronized (this) { ... }`. It ensures visibility and atomicity for the protected code. However, it can cause performance issues and deadlocks."

Example:
public synchronized void increment() { count++; }
// or
public void increment() {
    synchronized (this) { count++; }
}

Real-world use case: Shared data in multi-threaded applications.

Common mistakes:
- Synchronizing on a mutable object that can change.
- Having too large a critical section (performance).
- Not synchronizing on a consistent object (different objects for the same lock).
- Forgetting that `synchronized` blocks can be nested (leading to deadlock).

Interview tip: Mention that `java.util.concurrent` classes often provide better alternatives.

---

Q272: What is a `deadlock` and how can you prevent it?

Direct answer: A deadlock occurs when two or more threads are blocked forever, each waiting for a resource held by the other. It can be prevented by using a lock ordering, using timeouts, or using higher-level concurrency utilities.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of concurrency problems and solutions.

Breaking it down:
- Conditions: mutual exclusion, hold and wait, no preemption, circular wait.
- Prevention: avoid circular wait by ordering locks; use `tryLock()` with timeouts; use `ReentrantLock` instead of `synchronized`; use `ConcurrentHashMap` where possible.
- Detection: analyze thread dumps.
- Avoid nested locks where possible.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Deadlock happens when Thread A holds lock X and waits for lock Y, while Thread B holds lock Y and waits for lock X. They wait forever. To prevent it, I can ensure all threads acquire locks in the same order. I can also use `tryLock()` with a timeout to avoid indefinite waiting. I avoid nested locks when possible and use higher-level utilities like `ConcurrentHashMap`."

Example:
// Deadlock scenario
synchronized (lock1) {
    synchronized (lock2) { ... }
}
// Prevent by ensuring both threads lock lock1 then lock2.

Real-world use case: Database transactions, multi-resource systems.

Common mistakes:
- Not knowing the four conditions for deadlock.
- Using nested `synchronized` blocks without care.
- Not using timeouts.
- Not analyzing thread dumps to find deadlocks.

Interview tip: Mention that you can use `jstack` to detect deadlocks in a running JVM.

---

Q273: What is the difference between `wait()` and `sleep()`?

Direct answer: `wait()` is used for inter-thread communication and releases the lock; `sleep()` is used for pausing execution and does not release the lock. `wait()` must be called from a synchronized context; `sleep()` can be called anywhere.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of thread coordination.

Breaking it down:
- `wait()`: releases the monitor lock; must be in synchronized block; awakened by `notify()`/`notifyAll()`.
- `sleep()`: does not release any locks; can be used anywhere; awakens after timeout or interrupt.
- `wait()` is for coordinating between threads; `sleep()` is for delays.
- Both can be interrupted (thrown `InterruptedException`).

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`wait()` is used when a thread needs to wait for a condition and releases the lock so other threads can enter. It must be called within a synchronized block. `sleep()` simply pauses the thread for a specified time without releasing any locks. I use `wait()` with `notify()` for producer-consumer scenarios, and `sleep()` for delays, like polling."

Example:
// wait
synchronized (obj) {
    while (!condition) obj.wait();
}
// sleep
Thread.sleep(1000); // 1 second

Real-world use case: Producer-consumer, polling, and timeout handling.

Common mistakes:
- Calling `wait()` without `synchronized` (throws IllegalMonitorStateException).
- Using `sleep()` for synchronization.
- Not handling `InterruptedException`.
- Using `wait()` in a loop without checking condition (spurious wakeups).

Interview tip: Mention that `wait()` should be used in a loop to handle spurious wakeups.

---

Q274: What is `notify()` and `notifyAll()`?

Direct answer: `notify()` wakes up a single thread that is waiting on the object's monitor. `notifyAll()` wakes up all threads waiting on the object's monitor. They must be called from a synchronized context.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of thread notification.

Breaking it down:
- `notify()`: wakes one arbitrarily chosen thread.
- `notifyAll()`: wakes all waiting threads.
- `notifyAll()` is generally safer to avoid lost wake-ups.
- They do not release the lock immediately; the lock is released after the synchronized block ends.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`notify()` wakes up one waiting thread, while `notifyAll()` wakes up all waiting threads. I use `notifyAll()` more often because it's safer and prevents the risk of missing a notification. Both must be called from a synchronized block on the same object. After notification, the awakened threads will compete for the lock again."

Example:
synchronized (obj) {
    obj.notifyAll(); // wakes all threads
}

Real-world use case: Producer-consumer, blocking queues.

Common mistakes:
- Using `notify()` when multiple threads are waiting (may cause starvation).
- Forgetting to call `notify()` after changing condition.
- Calling `notify()` without holding the lock.
- Not using a loop to check condition after `wait()`.

Interview tip: Mention that `java.util.concurrent` classes like `BlockingQueue` handle this for you.

---

Q275: What is the `ExecutorService`?

Direct answer: `ExecutorService` is an interface that provides a thread pool and manages the lifecycle of threads, decoupling task submission from execution.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to see if you know the modern way to manage threads.

Breaking it down:
- Creates a pool of threads: `Executors.newFixedThreadPool()`, `newCachedThreadPool()`, `newSingleThreadExecutor()`.
- Submits tasks: `submit(Runnable)`, `submit(Callable)`.
- Returns `Future` for result retrieval.
- Shutdown: `shutdown()`, `shutdownNow()`.
- Prevents overhead of creating threads manually.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`ExecutorService` is a thread pool manager. I use it to execute tasks asynchronously without manually creating threads. For example, `Executors.newFixedThreadPool(10)` creates a pool of 10 threads. I submit `Runnable` or `Callable` tasks, and the executor handles the scheduling. I always shut it down when done to free resources. It's the preferred way to do concurrency in Java."

Example:
ExecutorService executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(5);
Future<String> future = executor.submit(() -> "Hello");
String result = future.get();
executor.shutdown();

Real-world use case: Web servers, batch processing, parallel computations.

Common mistakes:
- Not shutting down the executor (memory leak).
- Using `newCachedThreadPool()` for long-running tasks (unbounded growth).
- Not handling `Future.get()` exceptions.
- Using `Executors` without understanding thread pool size.

Interview tip: Mention that `ForkJoinPool` is used for divide-and-conquer tasks.

---

Q276: What is a `Future` in Java concurrency?

Direct answer: `Future` represents the result of an asynchronous computation. It provides methods to check if the computation is complete, wait for completion, and retrieve the result.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of async programming.

Breaking it down:
- Methods: `isDone()`, `cancel()`, `isCancelled()`, `get()` (blocking).
- `get()` throws `InterruptedException` and `ExecutionException`.
- Can be used with `ExecutorService.submit()`.
- `Future` does not support chaining; use `CompletableFuture` for that.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A `Future` is a placeholder for a result that hasn't been computed yet. When I submit a task to an `ExecutorService`, I get a `Future`. I can call `future.get()` to block until the result is ready. I can also check if it's done with `isDone()`, or cancel it. I use `Future` when I need a result from a background task, but for complex workflows, I prefer `CompletableFuture`."

Example:
Future<Integer> future = executor.submit(() -> compute());
Integer result = future.get(); // blocks

Real-world use case: Fetching data from multiple sources in parallel.

Common mistakes:
- Calling `get()` on a future that hasn't completed (blocking).
- Not handling `InterruptedException` and `ExecutionException`.
- Using `Future` when `CompletableFuture` is needed.
- Cancelling a future without handling the interrupt.

Interview tip: Mention that `CompletableFuture` is more powerful and supports chaining.

---

Q277: What is `CompletableFuture`?

Direct answer: `CompletableFuture` is an extension of `Future` that allows composition, chaining, and combining multiple asynchronous tasks using functional programming style. It supports non-blocking and callback-based operations.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of modern async programming.

Breaking it down:
- Implements `CompletionStage` and `Future`.
- Methods: `thenApply()`, `thenAccept()`, `thenRun()`, `thenCompose()`, `thenCombine()`.
- Can be manually completed with `complete()`.
- Supports exception handling: `exceptionally()`, `handle()`.
- Supports combining multiple futures: `allOf()`, `anyOf()`.
- Used with `CompletableFuture.supplyAsync()` and `runAsync()`.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`CompletableFuture` is a more powerful Future. It allows me to chain asynchronous operations without blocking. For example, I can call `supplyAsync` to run a task, then `thenApply` to process the result, and `thenAccept` to consume it. I can also combine multiple futures with `allOf` or `anyOf`. I use it for non-blocking APIs and reactive systems."

Example:
CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> compute())
    .thenApply(result -> result.toString())
    .thenAccept(System.out::println)
    .exceptionally(ex -> { System.err.println(ex); return null; });

Real-world use case: Microservices, reactive programming, parallel API calls.

Common mistakes:
- Not handling exceptions properly.
- Mixing blocking calls with `CompletableFuture`.
- Not using custom thread pools for heavy tasks.
- Forgetting that `CompletableFuture` is cancellable.

Interview tip: Mention that Spring uses `CompletableFuture` for `@Async` methods.

---

Q278: What is `ThreadLocal` and when would you use it?

Direct answer: `ThreadLocal` provides thread-local variables, where each thread has its own independent copy. It's used to keep state per thread, like user sessions, database connections, or transaction contexts.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of thread-specific data.

Breaking it down:
- Each thread gets its own value.
- Set: `threadLocal.set(value)`; Get: `threadLocal.get()`.
- Typically used with static final fields.
- Need to clean up (remove()) to avoid memory leaks.
- Used in frameworks for request-scoped data.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`ThreadLocal` allows me to store data that is local to a thread. Each thread accessing it gets its own copy. I use it to hold user session information in a web application, or to keep a database connection for the current transaction. However, I must remember to call `remove()` after the request to avoid memory leaks, especially in thread-pooled environments."

Example:
private static ThreadLocal<Connection> connectionHolder = new ThreadLocal<>();
connectionHolder.set(connection);
Connection conn = connectionHolder.get();
connectionHolder.remove(); // clean up

Real-world use case: Spring's transaction management, request-scoped beans.

Common mistakes:
- Not removing ThreadLocal values (memory leak).
- Using ThreadLocal for mutable shared data (defeats the purpose).
- Not using it with static final.
- Using ThreadLocal in long-lived threads without cleanup.

Interview tip: Mention that `InheritableThreadLocal` passes values to child threads.

---

Q279: What is a `Semaphore`?

Direct answer: A `Semaphore` is a concurrency utility that controls access to a shared resource using a counter. It maintains a set of permits, and threads must acquire a permit before accessing the resource.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of advanced concurrency controls.

Breaking it down:
- Counting semaphore: permits can be > 1.
- `acquire()`: tries to get a permit; blocks if none.
- `release()`: returns a permit.
- Used for resource pools (e.g., database connections).
- Can be fair or non-fair.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"A semaphore is a counter that limits concurrent access. If I have 5 permits, up to 5 threads can access the resource concurrently. I use it for connection pools or rate-limiting. A thread calls `acquire()` to get a permit and `release()` to return it. I can use `tryAcquire()` for non-blocking attempts."

Example:
Semaphore semaphore = new Semaphore(5);
try {
    semaphore.acquire();
    // access resource
} finally {
    semaphore.release();
}

Real-world use case: Connection pools, throttling, limited resources.

Common mistakes:
- Not releasing the permit (leads to exhaustion).
- Using semaphore when a lock is needed (different use case).
- Not handling `InterruptedException`.
- Using a semaphore with permits > 1 without considering fairness.

Interview tip: Mention that `Semaphore` is often used with `try-finally` to ensure release.

---

Q280: What is the difference between `ReentrantLock` and `synchronized`?

Direct answer: `ReentrantLock` provides more flexibility than `synchronized`, including try-lock, timed lock, interruptible lock, fairness, and multiple condition variables.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your advanced concurrency knowledge.

Breaking it down:
- `synchronized`: simple, JVM-managed, not flexible, no timeout.
- `ReentrantLock`: explicit lock/unlock, `tryLock()`, `lockInterruptibly()`, fairness option, multiple conditions.
- `synchronized` is easier; `ReentrantLock` is more powerful but requires careful management (unlock in finally).
- Performance: similar for low contention; `ReentrantLock` can be better for high contention.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`ReentrantLock` gives me more control. I can try to acquire the lock without blocking (`tryLock()`), wait with a timeout, or make it interruptible. It also supports fairness and multiple condition objects. `synchronized` is simpler and JVM-managed. I use `synchronized` for simple cases and `ReentrantLock` when I need these extra features."

Example:
ReentrantLock lock = new ReentrantLock();
if (lock.tryLock()) {
    try { /* critical section */ } finally { lock.unlock(); }
}

Real-world use case: Complex threading, timed operations.

Common mistakes:
- Forgetting to unlock in finally (deadlock).
- Using `tryLock()` without checking return.
- Using `ReentrantLock` when `synchronized` would suffice.
- Not handling exceptions properly.

Interview tip: Mention that `ReentrantLock` is also used with `Condition` for more sophisticated waiting.

---

Q281: What is `CountDownLatch`?

Direct answer: `CountDownLatch` is a synchronizer that allows one or more threads to wait until a set of operations being performed in other threads is complete. It uses a counter that is decremented, and threads can await until it reaches zero.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of coordination primitives.

Breaking it down:
- Initialized with a count.
- `countDown()` decrements the count.
- `await()` blocks until count reaches zero.
- Cannot be reset (use `CyclicBarrier` for resetting).
- Used for starting a service after its dependencies are ready.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`CountDownLatch` is a one-time synchronization helper. I initialize it with a count. Each time a task finishes, it calls `countDown()`. The main thread calls `await()` and waits until the count reaches zero. I use it to wait for multiple threads to finish before continuing, like starting a race only after all participants are ready."

Example:
CountDownLatch latch = new CountDownLatch(3);
for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
    new Thread(() -> { /* work */ latch.countDown(); }).start();
}
latch.await(); // wait for all three

Real-world use case: Starting services, parallel initialization.

Common mistakes:
- Not decrementing the latch (deadlock).
- Calling `await()` after `countDown()` is done (no effect).
- Using `CountDownLatch` when `CyclicBarrier` is needed.
- Not handling `InterruptedException`.

Interview tip: Mention that `CountDownLatch` can also be used with a timeout.

---

Q282: What is a `CyclicBarrier`?

Direct answer: `CyclicBarrier` is a synchronizer that allows a set of threads to wait for each other to reach a common point (barrier), and then all proceed together. It is reusable (cyclic) after a reset.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of coordination for parallel phases.

Breaking it down:
- Initialized with a number of threads and optionally a barrier action.
- `await()` blocks until all threads have called `await()`.
- Once the last thread arrives, all threads are released.
- Can be reset using `reset()`.
- Unlike `CountDownLatch`, it's reusable.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`CyclicBarrier` is used when I have a fixed number of threads that must wait for each other at a point before continuing. For example, in a parallel computation, all threads do phase 1, then wait at the barrier, then proceed to phase 2. It's reusable, so I can use it for multiple phases. I can also provide a barrier action that runs when all threads arrive."

Example:
CyclicBarrier barrier = new CyclicBarrier(3, () -> System.out.println("All ready"));
for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
    new Thread(() -> {
        // phase 1
        barrier.await();
        // phase 2
    }).start();
}

Real-world use case: Multithreaded algorithms (e.g., matrix multiplication), simulations.

Common mistakes:
- Not having the exact number of threads (deadlock).
- Not handling `BrokenBarrierException`.
- Using `CyclicBarrier` when `CountDownLatch` is simpler.
- Not resetting if some threads fail.

Interview tip: Mention that `Phaser` is a more flexible alternative.

---

Q283: What is a `ReentrantReadWriteLock`?

Direct answer: `ReentrantReadWriteLock` is a lock that allows multiple threads to read simultaneously, but write access is exclusive. It can improve performance in read-heavy scenarios.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of advanced locking.

Breaking it down:
- Two locks: read lock and write lock.
- Read lock: shared, multiple readers allowed.
- Write lock: exclusive, no readers or writers.
- Upgrading from read to write is not allowed to avoid deadlock.
- Useful for read-many, write-few scenarios.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`ReentrantReadWriteLock` separates read and write locks. Multiple threads can hold the read lock simultaneously, but the write lock is exclusive. I use this when I have a data structure that is read frequently and written rarely, like a cache. It improves throughput compared to a simple `synchronized` or `ReentrantLock`."

Example:
ReentrantReadWriteLock rwLock = new ReentrantReadWriteLock();
Lock readLock = rwLock.readLock();
Lock writeLock = rwLock.writeLock();

readLock.lock();
try { /* read */ } finally { readLock.unlock(); }

writeLock.lock();
try { /* write */ } finally { writeLock.unlock(); }

Real-world use case: Caches, configuration data, shared data structures.

Common mistakes:
- Upgrading from read lock to write lock (causes deadlock).
- Holding a read lock while performing write operations.
- Not using locks properly (always unlock in finally).
- Using it when there's no clear read/write ratio.

Interview tip: Mention that `StampedLock` is an alternative with optimistic reads.

---

Q284: What is the architecture of the Java Virtual Machine (JVM)?

Direct answer: The JVM architecture consists of the Class Loader subsystem, Runtime Data Areas (including heap, stack, method area, program counter, native method stacks), and the Execution Engine (interpreter, JIT compiler, garbage collector).

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of Java's runtime environment.

Breaking it down:
- Class Loader: loads `.class` files.
- Runtime Data Areas:
  - Heap: stores objects and arrays (shared).
  - Stack: each thread has its own stack, stores frames (local variables, operands).
  - Method Area: stores class metadata, constants, static variables.
  - PC Register: holds address of current executing instruction.
  - Native Method Stack: for native code.
- Execution Engine: interprets bytecode, JIT compiles hot methods, GC reclaims memory.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The JVM has three main components. The Class Loader loads the bytecode. The Runtime Data Areas include the heap (for objects), stacks (for threads), and the method area (for class metadata). The Execution Engine interprets bytecode, compiles it with JIT, and runs garbage collection. Understanding this helps me diagnose memory issues and performance bottlenecks."

Example:
// Not code, but conceptual

Real-world use case: Performance tuning, debugging memory leaks.

Common mistakes:
- Not knowing the difference between heap and stack.
- Confusing method area with heap.
- Not knowing the JIT compiler's role.
- Forgetting the PC register.

Interview tip: Mention that the heap is the main area for garbage collection.

---

Q285: Explain the Java Memory Model (JMM).

Direct answer: The Java Memory Model defines the rules for how threads interact with memory, specifying what values can be seen by threads and when, ensuring consistency across platforms.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your deep understanding of concurrency.

Breaking it down:
- Defines happens-before relationships.
- Guarantees visibility (e.g., `volatile`, `synchronized`).
- Defines atomicity for operations.
- Prevents certain compiler optimizations that could break thread safety.
- Ensures that Java programs run consistently on different hardware.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The JMM defines how threads see shared variables. It specifies that changes made by one thread are visible to others only if there is a happens-before relationship, which is established by `synchronized`, `volatile`, and `final`. It also defines which operations are atomic. This model ensures that my code behaves predictably across different hardware."

Example:
// Happens-before: write to volatile happens-before read of that volatile.

Real-world use case: Concurrency, safe publication, and designing thread-safe classes.

Common mistakes:
- Not understanding visibility issues.
- Assuming changes are immediately visible without synchronization.
- Using `volatile` incorrectly.
- Not using `synchronized` for compound operations.

Interview tip: Mention that the JMM is part of the Java Language Specification.

---

Q286: What is the JIT compiler and how does it work?

Direct answer: The JIT (Just-In-Time) compiler is part of the JVM that compiles frequently executed bytecode into native machine code for better performance, at runtime.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of Java performance optimization.

Breaking it down:
- Bytecode is interpreted initially.
- Hot methods (frequently called) are identified by the JVM.
- JIT compiles them to native code, which is faster.
- Uses profiling and optimization (e.g., inlining, loop unrolling).
- Two main compilers: C1 (client) for fast startup, C2 (server) for aggressive optimization.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The JIT compiler optimizes performance by converting bytecode to native machine code at runtime. It starts with interpretation, but it monitors which parts of the code are executed often (hot spots). Those are compiled to native code, which runs much faster. It also performs optimizations like method inlining. This is why Java can be as fast as C++ in many cases."

Example:
// Not code, but conceptual

Real-world use case: Improving performance of long-running applications.

Common mistakes:
- Not knowing about -XX flags to tune JIT.
- Assuming all code is JIT-compiled equally.
- Not understanding that startup time can be slower due to JIT.
- Not using warm-up time in benchmarks.

Interview tip: Mention that `-XX:+PrintCompilation` shows JIT activity.

---

Q287: What is the difference between Heap and Stack memory?

Direct answer: Heap is the memory area where objects are allocated, shared across threads. Stack is per-thread memory for method frames, storing local variables, method calls, and operand stacks.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of memory management.

Breaking it down:
- Heap: created when JVM starts; managed by GC; holds objects and arrays; larger.
- Stack: per-thread; stores frames (local variables, operands); limited size; LIFO.
- Heap is global; stack is thread-local.
- Stack overflow (recursion) vs. Heap overflow (too many objects).

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The heap is where all objects and arrays live, and it's shared across all threads. It's garbage-collected. The stack is per-thread and stores method frames, local variables, and return addresses. It's smaller and faster. I need to be mindful of stack depth (recursion) and heap usage (memory leaks)."

Example:
// Stack: primitive int i; reference to object.
int i = 10; // on stack
Person p = new Person(); // p on stack, Person object on heap

Real-world use case: Performance tuning, memory profiling.

Common mistakes:
- Thinking all variables are on the heap.
- Not knowing that primitives are on the stack.
- Confusing stack and heap sizes.
- Not understanding that references are on the stack, objects on the heap.

Interview tip: Mention that you can configure stack size with `-Xss` and heap size with `-Xmx`.

---

Q288: What is garbage collection in Java? How does it work?

Direct answer: Garbage collection (GC) is the automatic memory management process that finds and reclaims objects that are no longer referenced by the program, freeing memory.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of automatic memory management.

Breaking it down:
- GC runs on the heap.
- It identifies reachable objects (from GC roots: stack references, static variables, etc.).
- Unreachable objects are collected.
- GC algorithms: Mark-and-Sweep, Copying, Generational.
- Generational: young generation (minor GC) and old generation (major GC).
- It stops the world (pause) to collect, but with low-latency collectors, pauses are minimized.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"GC is automatic memory management. It periodically identifies which objects are no longer reachable and reclaims their memory. The heap is divided into generations: young and old. Most objects die young, so a minor GC is fast. Older objects are promoted. I can choose different GC algorithms based on my application's needs: throughput or low latency."

Example:
// Not code, but conceptual

Real-world use case: All Java applications rely on GC.

Common mistakes:
- Not tuning GC for the application.
- Creating too many objects (GC overhead).
- Using finalizers (deprecated).
- Not understanding that GC pauses can affect performance.

Interview tip: Mention that you can enable GC logging with `-Xlog:gc*`.

---

Q289: What are the different garbage collectors available in Java?

Direct answer: The main garbage collectors are Serial GC, Parallel GC, CMS (deprecated), G1 GC, ZGC, and Shenandoah. Each has different trade-offs in throughput, latency, and footprint.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of performance tuning options.

Breaking it down:
- Serial GC: single-threaded, for small heaps.
- Parallel GC: multi-threaded, high throughput, default for older versions.
- G1 GC: region-based, good balance of throughput and latency, default for many modern JVMs.
- ZGC: low latency (sub-millisecond pauses), scalable.
- Shenandoah: low latency, concurrent compaction.
- Choose based on application needs (low latency vs. high throughput).

How to structure your spoken answer:
"There are several GCs. Serial GC is single-threaded, good for small apps. Parallel GC is multi-threaded, good for throughput. G1 GC is the default, balances throughput and latency, uses regions. ZGC and Shenandoah are low-latency collectors with very small pause times. I choose based on my application's requirements—if I need low latency, I use ZGC; if I need high throughput, I use Parallel GC."

Example:
# JVM flags:
-XX:+UseSerialGC
-XX:+UseParallelGC
-XX:+UseG1GC
-XX:+UseZGC

Real-world use case: Web applications (low latency) vs. batch processing (throughput).

Common mistakes:
- Not tuning GC for the application.
- Using a collector that doesn't fit the workload.
- Not knowing the default GC in the JVM version.
- Ignoring GC logs.

Interview tip: Mention that G1 GC is the default in Java 9+.

---

Q290: What is the `Metaspace` and how is it different from `PermGen`?

Direct answer: Metaspace is the memory area for class metadata (like class definitions, method data) that was formerly PermGen. It is not part of the heap and grows dynamically (unless limited), unlike PermGen which was fixed-size and part of the heap.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of JVM memory areas and improvements.

Breaking it down:
- PermGen: fixed size (could cause OutOfMemoryError), part of heap.
- Metaspace: native memory (not Java heap), unlimited by default (can set MaxMetaspaceSize).
- Metaspace avoids PermGen full GC and OOM issues.
- Class unloading is more efficient in Metaspace.
- Introduced in Java 8.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"PermGen was the memory area for class metadata before Java 8. It had a fixed size, which often caused OutOfMemoryError when many classes were loaded. Metaspace replaced it; it's allocated from native memory and grows automatically (unless limited). This prevents many OOM issues and improves performance. I no longer need to tune PermGen size."

Example:
# JVM flags:
-XX:MaxMetaspaceSize=256m

Real-world use case: Applications that dynamically load classes (e.g., app servers, OSGi).

Common mistakes:
- Still trying to set PermGen flags in Java 8+.
- Not monitoring Metaspace usage.
- Not limiting Metaspace size when needed.
- Thinking Metaspace is part of the heap.

Interview tip: Mention that Metaspace can be garbage-collected when classes are unloaded.

---

Q291: How do you diagnose a memory leak in Java?

Direct answer: I diagnose memory leaks by using heap dumps, profilers (like JVisualVM, JProfiler), and analyzing GC logs. I look for objects that are not being freed, often due to static collections or listeners not being unregistered.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your troubleshooting skills.

Breaking it down:
- Signs: increasing heap usage, frequent GC, OutOfMemoryError.
- Tools: JVisualVM, Eclipse MAT, JProfiler, YourKit.
- Steps: capture heap dump, analyze dominators, find large objects, trace references to GC roots.
- Common causes: static collections, listeners, ThreadLocal without cleanup, database connections.
- Use `jmap` to get heap dumps, `jstat` for GC monitoring.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"I use tools like JVisualVM or Eclipse MAT to analyze heap dumps. I look for objects that should be unreachable but are still held by GC roots, often due to static maps or unregistered listeners. I also check GC logs to see if the heap keeps growing after full GC. Once I find the leak, I fix the code to release references."

Example:
# Take heap dump
jmap -dump:live,file=heap.hprof <pid>
# Analyze with Eclipse MAT

Real-world use case: Long-running server applications.

Common mistakes:
- Not taking a dump when the problem occurs.
- Not understanding that `live` dump only includes reachable objects.
- Ignoring GC logs.
- Not profiling with a tool.

Interview tip: Mention that you often use `jcmd` for diagnostics.

---

Q292: What are `soft`, `weak`, and `phantom` references?

Direct answer: They are reference types that allow the GC to treat objects differently. `SoftReference` is for memory-sensitive caches (GC may clear before OOM). `WeakReference` is for non-essential objects (cleared on next GC). `PhantomReference` is for finalization/pre-mortem cleanup.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your advanced knowledge of memory management.

Breaking it down:
- SoftReference: cleared only when memory is low; good for caches.
- WeakReference: cleared at the next GC; used for weak maps (WeakHashMap).
- PhantomReference: not cleared automatically; used to track object finalization.
- All extend `Reference` and are used to avoid memory leaks.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`SoftReference` is for caches – the GC will clear them only if memory is low. `WeakReference` is cleared at the next GC, used for `WeakHashMap` or listeners. `PhantomReference` is used to perform cleanup after the object is finalized, but before memory is reclaimed. I use them to avoid memory leaks in caches or event listeners."

Example:
WeakReference<Person> ref = new WeakReference<>(person);
// When person is no longer referenced, it can be GC'd.

Real-world use case: Caching, listeners, weak collections.

Common mistakes:
- Using `WeakReference` for strong references (defeats purpose).
- Not understanding that `PhantomReference` requires `ReferenceQueue`.
- Using `SoftReference` without a cache size limit.
- Not clearing references in `ReferenceQueue`.

Interview tip: Mention that `WeakHashMap` is a practical use of `WeakReference`.

---

Q293: What is a `ClassNotFoundException` and how is it different from `NoClassDefFoundError`?

Direct answer: `ClassNotFoundException` is a checked exception thrown when the class loader tries to load a class but cannot find it. `NoClassDefFoundError` is an error thrown when a class was present at compile time but not found at runtime.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of class loading issues.

Breaking it down:
- `ClassNotFoundException`: thrown when explicitly loading a class via `Class.forName()` or `ClassLoader.findClass()`.
- `NoClassDefFoundError`: thrown when the JVM tries to resolve a class that was present during compilation but is missing at runtime (classpath issue).
- First is checked, second is an Error.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`ClassNotFoundException` occurs when I try to load a class dynamically (like `Class.forName()`) and the class is not on the classpath. `NoClassDefFoundError` is an error that happens when a class that was present at compile-time is missing at runtime—for example, a dependency jar is missing. I handle the first by catching it; the second is usually a configuration issue."

Example:
// ClassNotFoundException
try { Class.forName("com.example.MissingClass"); } catch (ClassNotFoundException e) { }

// NoClassDefFoundError: runtime classpath issue.

Real-world use case: Application startup, classpath issues.

Common mistakes:
- Confusing the two.
- Not handling `ClassNotFoundException`.
- Not checking the classpath for `NoClassDefFoundError`.
- Using wrong classloader.

Interview tip: Mention that `NoClassDefFoundError` can also happen if static initializers fail.

---

Q294: What is the `-Xmx` and `-Xms` JVM parameters?

Direct answer: `-Xmx` sets the maximum heap size; `-Xms` sets the initial heap size. They are used to control the JVM's memory allocation.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of JVM tuning.

Breaking it down:
- `-Xmx`: maximum heap size (e.g., `-Xmx2g` for 2GB).
- `-Xms`: initial heap size (e.g., `-Xms512m`).
- Setting `-Xms` equal to `-Xmx` avoids resizing overhead.
- Too small `-Xmx` → OutOfMemoryError; too large → OS memory pressure.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`-Xmx` sets the maximum heap memory available to the JVM. `-Xms` sets the initial heap size. I set `-Xms` and `-Xmx` to the same value to avoid the overhead of heap resizing. I base these values on the application's memory needs and the available system memory."

Example:
# Set heap to 1GB initial and 2GB max
java -Xms1g -Xmx2g MyApp

Real-world use case: Application startup, server configuration.

Common mistakes:
- Setting `-Xmx` too low (OOM).
- Setting `-Xms` too high (wastes memory).
- Not considering system memory.
- Forgetting that other memory areas (Metaspace) also need tuning.

Interview tip: Mention that you can also use `-XX:MaxMetaspaceSize` for Metaspace.

---

Q295: What is Spring Framework?

Direct answer: Spring Framework is a comprehensive Java framework that provides infrastructure support for developing enterprise applications, with core features like Dependency Injection, Aspect-Oriented Programming, and a comprehensive web framework.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of the most popular Java framework.

Breaking it down:
- Core: IoC/DI, AOP, event handling.
- Modules: Spring MVC (web), Spring Data (data access), Spring Security (security), Spring Boot (auto-configuration).
- Reduces boilerplate code.
- Promotes loose coupling and testability.
- Supports both XML and annotation-based configuration.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Spring is a framework that makes Java enterprise development easier. Its core is Dependency Injection, which helps me create loosely coupled components. It also provides AOP for cross-cutting concerns. I use Spring Boot for auto-configuration, Spring MVC for web apps, and Spring Data for data access. It simplifies many common tasks."

Example:
@Service
public class UserService {
    @Autowired
    private UserRepository repo;
    // business logic
}

Real-world use case: Almost all Java enterprise applications use Spring.

Common mistakes:
- Not understanding that Spring manages object lifecycle.
- Using Spring without understanding DI.
- Overusing XML configuration (modern Spring uses annotations).
- Not leveraging Spring Boot for faster development.

Interview tip: Mention that Spring Boot is the most popular way to start a Spring project.

Q296: What is Dependency Injection (DI) and how does Spring implement it?

Direct answer: Dependency Injection is a design pattern where an object receives its dependencies from an external source (rather than creating them itself). Spring implements it via its IoC container, using constructor injection, setter injection, or field injection.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of Spring's core concept.

Breaking it down:
- DI: dependencies are injected, not hard-coded.
- Spring IoC container manages beans and their dependencies.
- Constructor injection (preferred), setter injection, field injection.
- `@Autowired` annotation for automatic wiring.
- Beans are defined via XML, annotations, or Java config.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"DI means that I don't create my dependencies; they are provided to me. Spring's IoC container manages this. I define beans, and Spring wires them together. I prefer constructor injection because it makes the class immutable and ensures all dependencies are set. I use `@Autowired` or constructor injection to inject dependencies."

Example:
@Service
public class UserService {
    private final UserRepository repo;
    @Autowired
    public UserService(UserRepository repo) {
        this.repo = repo;
    }
}

Real-world use case: All Spring applications.

Common mistakes:
- Using field injection (harder to test).
- Creating circular dependencies.
- Not using constructor injection for required dependencies.
- Not understanding that Spring creates beans at startup.

Interview tip: Mention that constructor injection is recommended by the Spring team.

---

Q297: What is the Spring IoC Container?

Direct answer: The Spring IoC Container is the core of the Spring Framework. It creates, configures, and manages the lifecycle of Spring beans (objects) and wires dependencies between them.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of Spring's architecture.

Breaking it down:
- Container: `ApplicationContext` (extension of `BeanFactory`).
- Reads configuration (XML, annotations, Java config).
- Instantiates beans, resolves dependencies, and manages scope.
- Provides advanced features like event propagation, internationalization, and resource loading.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The IoC container is the engine of Spring. It manages the beans – their creation, configuration, and lifecycle. I use `ApplicationContext` which is the main container. It reads my configuration, creates the beans, and injects dependencies. It also handles bean scopes (singleton, prototype)."

Example:
ApplicationContext context = new AnnotationConfigApplicationContext(AppConfig.class);
MyService service = context.getBean(MyService.class);

Real-world use case: Every Spring application uses the IoC container.

Common mistakes:
- Not knowing the difference between `BeanFactory` and `ApplicationContext`.
- Using `ApplicationContext` without understanding its initialization.
- Not closing the container (resources not released).
- Forgetting to register configuration classes.

Interview tip: Mention that `ApplicationContext` is preferred over `BeanFactory`.

---

Q298: What is `@Autowired` in Spring?

Direct answer: `@Autowired` is an annotation used to automatically inject dependencies into a Spring bean. It can be applied to constructors, setters, fields, and methods.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of dependency injection.

Breaking it down:
- Marks a dependency to be autowired by type.
- Can be used on constructors (preferred), setter methods, and fields.
- By default, the injection is required; can use `required=false`.
- Autowiring by name with `@Qualifier` if multiple beans of same type.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`@Autowired` tells Spring to inject a bean automatically. I use it on constructors to inject dependencies, on setter methods, or directly on fields. Spring looks for a matching bean by type. If there are multiple beans of the same type, I use `@Qualifier` to specify which one. Constructor injection is my preference for immutability."

Example:
@Service
public class UserService {
    private final UserRepository repo;
    @Autowired
    public UserService(UserRepository repo) {
        this.repo = repo;
    }
}

Real-world use case: All Spring services.

Common mistakes:
- Using `@Autowired` on fields (harder to test).
- Not using `@Qualifier` when there are multiple beans.
- Forgetting that `@Autowired` is required by default.
- Using `@Autowired` on a private field (still works but not recommended).

Interview tip: Mention that Spring recommends constructor injection and `@Autowired` can be omitted if you have a single constructor.

---

Q299: Explain the `@Component`, `@Service`, `@Repository`, and `@Controller` annotations.

Direct answer: These are stereotype annotations that indicate a class is a Spring bean and define its role. `@Component` is a generic bean. `@Service` is for service layer beans. `@Repository` is for data access layer beans (and provides exception translation). `@Controller` is for web controllers.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of Spring's layer separation.

Breaking it down:
- `@Component`: generic stereotype for any Spring-managed bean.
- `@Service`: indicates a service class (business logic).
- `@Repository`: indicates a DAO/repository class; enables persistence exception translation.
- `@Controller`: indicates a web controller, used with `@RequestMapping` and `@RestController`.
- All are specializations of `@Component` and enable component scanning.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"These annotations mark classes as Spring beans. `@Component` is the base. `@Service` is for business logic classes. `@Repository` is for data access and adds exception translation. `@Controller` is for web endpoints. I use `@Service` for my service layer, `@Repository` for JPA repositories, and `@Controller` for REST controllers. They help with component scanning and make the code more readable."

Example:
@Service
public class UserService { ... }

@Repository
public interface UserRepository extends JpaRepository<User, Long> { ... }

@RestController
public class UserController { ... }

Real-world use case: Layered architecture in Spring Boot.

Common mistakes:
- Using `@Component` everywhere instead of specific annotations.
- Forgetting that `@Repository` adds exception translation.
- Using `@Controller` without `@ResponseBody` (use `@RestController`).
- Not using any stereotype and manually creating beans.

Interview tip: Mention that Spring Boot auto-configuration relies on these annotations.

---

Q300: What is the difference between `@Bean` and `@Component`?

Direct answer: `@Component` is a class-level annotation that marks a class as a Spring bean, enabling component scanning. `@Bean` is a method-level annotation used in `@Configuration` classes to declare a bean manually.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of bean declaration methods.

Breaking it down:
- `@Component`: on class; automatic scanning; class must be in scanned package.
- `@Bean`: on method in configuration class; explicitly creates bean; allows custom instantiation logic.
- `@Bean` is used for third-party classes or when you need custom logic.
- Both produce Spring beans.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`@Component` is used on a class to tell Spring to create a bean from it automatically via component scanning. `@Bean` is used inside a `@Configuration` class to manually define a bean, often for third-party classes I can't annotate. I use `@Bean` when I need to customize the instantiation, like setting parameters."

Example:
@Component
public class MyService { ... }

@Configuration
public class AppConfig {
    @Bean
    public MyCustomService customService() {
        return new MyCustomService("param");
    }
}

Real-world use case: Configuring third-party libraries.

Common mistakes:
- Using `@Bean` on a class (doesn't work; must be method).
- Using `@Component` for classes not in scan path.
- Not knowing that `@Bean` can be used with `@Scope`.
- Forgetting that `@Bean` methods are proxied (singleton scope).

Interview tip: Mention that `@Bean` is often used with `@Configuration` and `@PropertySource`.

---

Q301: What is Spring Boot? How does it simplify Spring development?

Direct answer: Spring Boot is a framework that simplifies Spring development by providing auto-configuration, starter dependencies, and embedded servers, allowing you to create stand-alone production-ready applications with minimal configuration.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of the modern Spring ecosystem.

Breaking it down:
- Auto-configuration: configures beans based on classpath dependencies.
- Starters: opinionated sets of dependencies (e.g., `spring-boot-starter-web`).
- Embedded servers: Tomcat, Jetty, Undertow built-in.
- Production-ready: health checks, metrics, externalized configuration.
- Reduces boilerplate and XML.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Spring Boot takes the complexity out of Spring. It auto-configures beans based on the dependencies I add, so I don't need to write configuration. Starters bundle common dependencies. I can run my application as a JAR with an embedded server. It also includes production features like health endpoints. I use it for almost all new Spring projects."

Example:
@SpringBootApplication
public class MyApp {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        SpringApplication.run(MyApp.class, args);
    }
}

Real-world use case: All new enterprise Java applications.

Common mistakes:
- Not understanding the magic behind auto-configuration.
- Overusing `@SpringBootApplication` with custom configurations.
- Not using `application.properties` effectively.
- Not disabling auto-configuration when needed.

Interview tip: Mention that you can exclude auto-configuration with `exclude` attribute.

Q302: What is the purpose of `application.properties` or `application.yml` in Spring Boot?

Direct answer: They are configuration files used to externalize configuration for a Spring Boot application, allowing you to set properties like server port, database connections, and logging levels.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of configuration management.

Breaking it down:
- `application.properties`: key-value format.
- `application.yml`: hierarchical YAML format.
- Can be overridden by environment variables, command-line args, and profile-specific files.
- Supports property placeholders and type-safe configuration.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"I use `application.properties` or `application.yml` to configure my Spring Boot application. For example, I set `server.port=8081` to change the port. I use it for datasource URLs, logging, and other settings. It supports profiles, so I can have `application-dev.properties` and `application-prod.properties`. I prefer YAML for its readability."

Example:
# application.properties
server.port=8081
spring.datasource.url=jdbc:mysql://localhost/db

# application.yml
server:
  port: 8081
spring:
  datasource:
    url: jdbc:mysql://localhost/db

Real-world use case: Configuring environment-specific settings.

Common mistakes:
- Hardcoding values instead of using external config.
- Not using profiles for different environments.
- Using spaces in YAML incorrectly.
- Not knowing that environment variables override properties.

Interview tip: Mention that you can use `@Value` or `@ConfigurationProperties` to read properties.
