Q352: What is a `var` keyword introduced in Java 10?

Direct answer: `var` is a keyword for local variable type inference. The compiler infers the type from the initializer. It's used to reduce boilerplate, but can only be used for local variables (not fields, method parameters, or return types).

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of modern Java features.

Breaking it down:
- `var` infers type at compile-time (not runtime).
- Use when the type is obvious from the right side.
- Cannot be used without initializer.
- Cannot be used for lambda parameters (inferred by target).
- Improves readability when types are verbose.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`var` allows me to declare local variables without specifying the type explicitly. The compiler infers it. For example, `var list = new ArrayList<String>()`. I use it when the type is obvious, making the code cleaner. However, I avoid using it when the type is not clear."

Example:
var list = new ArrayList<String>(); // infers ArrayList<String>
var user = new User("John"); // infers User

Real-world use case: Reducing boilerplate in local variables.

Common mistakes:
- Using `var` for method return types (not allowed).
- Using `var` without initialization.
- Using `var` in public APIs.
- Overusing `var` where readability suffers.

Interview tip: Mention that `var` is not allowed for fields, method params, or return types.

---

Q353: What are Java `Records` (Java 14/16)?

Direct answer: Records are a special type of class (introduced in Java 14/16) designed to be transparent data carriers. They are immutable, with automatically generated constructor, `equals()`, `hashCode()`, and `toString()`.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of modern Java features.

Breaking it down:
- Records are for simple data classes (like DTOs).
- Defined with `record` keyword and components.
- Automatically have private final fields, public constructor, getters (`componentName()`), and `equals`/`hashCode`/`toString`.
- Can have custom constructors and methods.
- Great for reducing boilerplate.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Records are a new feature (Java 14/16) for immutable data classes. I define a record with components, and Java generates the constructor, getters, `equals`, `hashCode`, and `toString`. For example, `record Person(String name, int age) {}`. I use them for DTOs and value objects."

Example:
record Person(String name, int age) {}
Person p = new Person("John", 30);
System.out.println(p.name()); // getter

Real-world use case: DTOs, value objects, API responses.

Common mistakes:
- Using records for mutable objects.
- Adding too much logic to records.
- Forgetting that records are final (cannot be extended).
- Not using records when they are appropriate (reducing boilerplate).

Interview tip: Mention that records are often used with `@RequestBody` in Spring.

---

Q354: What is the `Optional` class and its common pitfalls?

Direct answer: `Optional` is a container for nullable values, used to avoid `NullPointerException` and make absence explicit. Pitfalls include using `Optional.get()` without checking, using `Optional` as a field or parameter, and overusing it for simple null checks.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of safe coding.

Breaking it down:
- `Optional.ofNullable()` and `Optional.of()` (non-null).
- Methods: `isPresent()`, `ifPresent()`, `orElse()`, `orElseGet()`, `orElseThrow()`.
- Do not use `Optional` as a field, method parameter, or in serialization.
- Avoid `Optional.get()`; use `orElseThrow` instead.
- Use `Optional` for return types only.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`Optional` is a container that may or may not hold a value. I use it to make the possibility of null explicit. For example, `Optional.ofNullable(user).orElseThrow(() -> new NotFoundException())`. I avoid using `Optional` as a field or parameter. The main pitfall is using `get()` without checking, which defeats the purpose."

Example:
Optional<User> opt = userService.find(id);
User user = opt.orElseThrow(() -> new UserNotFoundException());

Real-world use case: Repository find methods.

Common mistakes:
- Using `Optional.get()` without `isPresent()`.
- Using `Optional` as a field (not serializable).
- Using `Optional` as a method parameter.
- Not using `orElse` with lazy initialization (`orElseGet`).

Interview tip: Mention that `Optional` is for return types, not for collections or parameters.

---

Q355: What is Spring Cloud?

Direct answer: Spring Cloud is a framework for building cloud-native applications, providing tools for service discovery (Eureka), configuration management (Config Server), API Gateway (Gateway), and resilience (Circuit Breaker).

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of the Spring ecosystem for microservices.

Breaking it down:
- Provides patterns for distributed systems.
- Components: Eureka (service registry), Ribbon (client-side load balancer), Feign (declarative REST client), Zuul/Spring Cloud Gateway (API Gateway), Config Server (externalized config).
- Integrates with Spring Boot.
- Often used with Netflix OSS or Kubernetes.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Spring Cloud is a set of libraries for building distributed applications. I use it for service discovery (Eureka), centralized configuration (Config Server), and routing (Spring Cloud Gateway). It helps implement patterns like circuit breaker, retries, and distributed tracing."

Example:
@EnableDiscoveryClient
@SpringBootApplication
public class MyApp { ... }

Real-world use case: Microservices architectures.

Common mistakes:
- Overcomplicating with too many Spring Cloud components.
- Not handling fallback properly.
- Forgetting to configure service discovery.
- Not using configuration management.

Interview tip: Mention that Spring Cloud Gateway is preferred over Zuul.

Q356: What is a Circuit Breaker pattern and how is it implemented in Java?

Direct answer: Circuit Breaker prevents cascading failures by stopping calls to a failing service, and returning a fallback response. In Java, it can be implemented with Resilience4j, Hystrix (deprecated), or using Spring Cloud Circuit Breaker.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of resilience patterns.

Breaking it down:
- States: CLOSED (calls pass), OPEN (calls blocked), HALF-OPEN (test if service is back).
- Prevents timeouts and overloads.
- Resilience4j is the modern library.
- Spring Cloud Circuit Breaker abstracts it.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Circuit Breaker prevents a service from repeatedly calling a failing dependency, allowing it to fail fast and return fallback. I use Resilience4j with Spring Cloud Circuit Breaker. I configure thresholds for failure rate, and when exceeded, the circuit opens and calls are blocked for a period."

Example:
@CircuitBreaker(name = "userService", fallbackMethod = "fallback")
public String getUser(String id) { ... }

public String fallback(String id, Throwable t) { return "default"; }

Real-world use case: Microservices calling external APIs.

Common mistakes:
- Not providing a fallback.
- Setting thresholds too low (false positives).
- Not using timeouts correctly.
- Ignoring logging for circuit changes.

Interview tip: Mention that you use Resilience4j with Spring Boot.

---

Q357: Explain the Hibernate `Session` and how it differs from `EntityManager`.

Direct answer: `Session` is Hibernate's native API for interacting with the database (persistence context). `EntityManager` is the JPA standard API. `Session` has extra Hibernate-specific features. `EntityManager` can be unwrapped to get a `Session`.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of Hibernate vs JPA.

Breaking it down:
- `Session`: Hibernate-specific, includes methods like `save()`, `evict()`, `merge()`.
- `EntityManager`: JPA standard, uses `persist()`, `find()`, `merge()`.
- `EntityManager` is the JPA equivalent of `Session`.
- In Spring Data JPA, you use `EntityManager`; can use `unwrap` for Hibernate features.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`Session` is Hibernate's native interface, while `EntityManager` is the JPA standard. `Session` has additional methods like `save()` and `evict()`. In Spring, I use `EntityManager` for portability. If I need Hibernate-specific features, I can unwrap: `Session session = em.unwrap(Session.class)`."

Example:
EntityManager em = ...;
Session session = em.unwrap(Session.class);

Real-world use case: Using Hibernate-specific caching or criteria.

Common mistakes:
- Using `Session` and losing JPA portability.
- Not using `EntityManager` when possible.
- Forgetting that `EntityManager` is the standard.
- Using deprecated Hibernate methods.

Interview tip: Mention that Spring Data JPA uses `EntityManager` behind the scenes.

---

Q358: What is the N+1 query problem in Hibernate and how do you solve it?

Direct answer: The N+1 problem occurs when you fetch a list of entities and then lazily access each entity's association, resulting in 1 query for the list and N additional queries for each association. Solve with `@EntityGraph`, `JOIN FETCH`, or `@BatchSize`.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your performance tuning knowledge.

Breaking it down:
- N+1: 1 query to get the parent list, then N queries for each parent's child.
- Solution 1: `JOIN FETCH` in JPQL: `SELECT u FROM User u JOIN FETCH u.orders`.
- Solution 2: `@EntityGraph` to specify fetch graph.
- Solution 3: `@BatchSize` to fetch multiple at once.
- Use lazy loading but be aware of the pitfall.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"The N+1 problem happens when I load a list of entities and then access a lazy association for each, causing many extra queries. I solve it by using `JOIN FETCH` in JPQL to load the association in one query. I also use `@EntityGraph` to define fetch strategies. I avoid using EAGER fetching globally, as it can cause performance issues."

Example:
@Query("SELECT u FROM User u JOIN FETCH u.orders")
List<User> findAllWithOrders();

Real-world use case: Displaying lists with child data.

Common mistakes:
- Using EAGER fetching indiscriminately.
- Not using `JOIN FETCH` and causing N+1.
- Using `JOIN FETCH` with pagination (can cause issues).
- Not using `@BatchSize` as an alternative.

Interview tip: Mention that you can use `@NamedEntityGraph` for dynamic fetching.

---

Q359: What is the difference between `FetchType.LAZY` and `FetchType.EAGER` performance implications?

Direct answer: `LAZY` delays loading until accessed, saving unnecessary queries. `EAGER` loads immediately, possibly causing extra queries and memory overhead. `LAZY` is usually preferred to avoid loading unused data, but can cause N+1 if not managed.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your understanding of performance.

Breaking it down:
- `LAZY`: Loads association when first accessed. Default for `@OneToMany`, `@ManyToMany`.
- `EAGER`: Loads association immediately (via join or separate query). Default for `@ManyToOne`, `@OneToOne`.
- `EAGER` can lead to unnecessary joins and large result sets.
- `LAZY` can lead to LazyInitializationException if accessed outside transaction.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`LAZY` is more efficient because it only loads associated data when needed. `EAGER` loads immediately, which can cause unnecessary queries and memory usage. I prefer `LAZY` by default, and use `@EntityGraph` or `JOIN FETCH` to load data when needed. However, I must ensure the session is open when accessing lazy associations."

Example:
@OneToMany(fetch = FetchType.LAZY)
private List<Order> orders;

Real-world use case: Optimizing database queries.

Common mistakes:
- Using `EAGER` on large collections (performance issues).
- Using `LAZY` and not handling transactions.
- Forgetting that `EAGER` can cause multiple queries if not joined.
- Not using `JOIN FETCH` to optimize eager fetching.

Interview tip: Mention that you use `EntityGraph` to control fetch strategies.

---

Q360: How do you create a custom exception in Java?

Direct answer: Extend `Exception` (checked) or `RuntimeException` (unchecked) and provide constructors. Optionally include custom fields for more information.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your ability to handle domain-specific errors.

Breaking it down:
- `public class MyException extends Exception` for checked.
- `public class MyRuntimeException extends RuntimeException` for unchecked.
- Provide constructors: no-arg, message, cause, and possibly custom fields.
- Use custom exceptions to make error handling more descriptive.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"I create a custom exception by extending `Exception` or `RuntimeException`. I add constructors to pass a message and cause. I also add custom fields if I need additional data. For example, `UserNotFoundException` could store the user ID. This makes error handling more informative."

Example:
public class UserNotFoundException extends RuntimeException {
    private final Long userId;
    public UserNotFoundException(Long userId) {
        super("User not found: " + userId);
        this.userId = userId;
    }
}

Real-world use case: Validations, business rule violations.

Common mistakes:
- Not providing constructors with cause.
- Using checked exceptions when unchecked would be better.
- Creating too many exception classes.
- Not using `super` to chain exceptions.

Interview tip: Mention that in Spring, you can use `@ResponseStatus` to map exceptions to HTTP status codes.

---

Q361: What is logging and why is it important? Compare Log4j and SLF4J.

Direct answer: Logging is recording application events for debugging, monitoring, and auditing. It's important for troubleshooting and tracking issues. SLF4J is a facade, Log4j is an implementation; SLF4J allows switching implementations without code changes.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of operational best practices.

Breaking it down:
- Logging: output messages at different levels (DEBUG, INFO, WARN, ERROR).
- Important for diagnosing issues in production.
- SLF4J: abstraction layer.
- Log4j: implementation, with configurations (log4j2.xml).
- Logback is another implementation, often used with SLF4J.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"Logging is crucial for debugging and monitoring. I use SLF4J as a facade, which decouples my code from the logging implementation. I can switch between Logback and Log4j2 without changing code. Log levels help me control verbosity. I always log meaningful messages, including context like user IDs."

Example:
private static final Logger log = LoggerFactory.getLogger(MyClass.class);
log.info("User {} logged in", userId);

Real-world use case: All applications.

Common mistakes:
- Not using parameterized logging.
- Using `System.out.println()`.
- Logging sensitive information.
- Not having a log rotation policy.

Interview tip: Mention that Spring Boot uses SLF4J with Logback by default.

Q362: How do you profile a Java application for performance bottlenecks?

Direct answer: I use profiling tools like JVisualVM, YourKit, JProfiler, or async-profiler. I look for CPU hotspots, memory allocation, GC pauses, and thread contention.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your performance troubleshooting skills.

Breaking it down:
- CPU profiling: find methods using high CPU (sampling).
- Memory profiling: find memory allocations and potential leaks.
- GC profiling: monitor GC frequency and pauses.
- Thread profiling: detect deadlocks and contention.
- Use Java Flight Recorder (JFR) for lightweight profiling.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"I use JVisualVM or JProfiler to profile CPU and memory. I look at the hot methods to see where the time is spent. For memory, I check object allocation rates and GC logs. I also use Java Flight Recorder to get production data. I focus on the bottleneck and then optimize the code."

Example:
# Start with JFR
java -XX:StartFlightRecording -XX:FlightRecorderOptions=duration=60s ...

Real-world use case: Performance tuning.

Common mistakes:
- Not profiling before optimizing.
- Profiling in the wrong environment.
- Not focusing on the biggest bottleneck.
- Ignoring GC and memory.

Interview tip: Mention that you use JMC (Java Mission Control) with JFR.

Q363: What are `InputStream` and `OutputStream` in Java?

Direct answer: `InputStream` and `OutputStream` are abstract classes for byte I/O. `InputStream` reads bytes from a source; `OutputStream` writes bytes to a destination. They are the foundation of Java's I/O.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of I/O.

Breaking it down:
- `InputStream`: `read()` methods, used for reading files, network sockets, etc.
- `OutputStream`: `write()` methods.
- Subclasses: `FileInputStream`, `FileOutputStream`, `BufferedInputStream`, `BufferedOutputStream`.
- Used with `Reader`/`Writer` for character streams.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"`InputStream` and `OutputStream` are the base classes for byte-based I/O. I use `FileInputStream` to read files, and `FileOutputStream` to write. I often wrap them with `BufferedInputStream` for performance. For text, I use `Reader`/`Writer` which are character-based."

Example:
try (InputStream is = new FileInputStream("file.txt")) {
    int data = is.read();
}

Real-world use case: File I/O, network communication.

Common mistakes:
- Not closing streams (use try-with-resources).
- Not using buffered streams.
- Not handling encoding for text.
- Using byte streams for character data.

Interview tip: Mention that you always use try-with-resources for I/O.

---

Q364: What is the NIO (New I/O) package?

Direct answer: NIO (java.nio) is a non-blocking I/O API introduced in Java 1.4. It provides buffers, channels, and selectors for scalable I/O, supporting both blocking and non-blocking operations.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to test your knowledge of advanced I/O.

Breaking it down:
- Buffer: `ByteBuffer`, `CharBuffer`.
- Channel: `FileChannel`, `SocketChannel`, `ServerSocketChannel`.
- Selector: for multiplexing multiple channels (single thread handles many connections).
- Used in high-performance servers.
- `java.nio.file` (NIO.2) provides `Path`, `Files` for file operations.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"NIO is an alternative I/O API with buffers and channels. It supports non-blocking I/O, which is useful for scalable servers. I use `FileChannel` for file operations, and `SocketChannel` for network. The Selector allows one thread to manage multiple connections. For file operations, I also use `Files` and `Paths` from NIO.2."

Example:
try (FileChannel channel = FileChannel.open(Paths.get("file.txt"))) {
    ByteBuffer buf = ByteBuffer.allocate(1024);
    channel.read(buf);
}

Real-world use case: High-performance servers (Netty, Grizzly).

Common mistakes:
- Not using the newer `java.nio.file` API.
- Trying to use NIO without understanding buffers.
- Not handling buffer flipping correctly.
- Using NIO for simple I/O (overhead).

Interview tip: Mention that `Files.readAllLines` is convenient for small files.

Q365: Describe a challenging Java project you have worked on.

Direct answer: This is a behavioral question. I would describe a project with a clear challenge (e.g., high performance, complex data processing, microservices migration), my role, the solution, and the outcome, highlighting my technical skills and learning.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to assess your experience and problem-solving.

Breaking it down:
- Choose a project that showcases your Java skills.
- Describe: context, challenge, my specific contribution, technologies used, result.
- Emphasize learning and teamwork.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"I worked on a large microservices project where we had to migrate from a monolithic application. The challenge was to ensure zero downtime and data consistency across services. I designed the data partitioning and used Spring Cloud with Kafka for eventual consistency. The project was a success with minimal downtime and improved scalability. I learned a lot about distributed systems and event-driven architecture."

Example:
# Mention specific: Java 11, Spring Boot, JPA, Kafka, Docker, Kubernetes.

Real-world use case: Interviews.

Common mistakes:
- Being too vague.
- Not focusing on your role.
- Not mentioning the outcome.
- Not highlighting technical depth.

Interview tip: Use the STAR method (Situation, Task, Action, Result).

Q366: How do you prioritize between performance and code readability?

Direct answer: I prioritize readability first, then optimize for performance only when necessary, using profiling to identify bottlenecks. Clean code is easier to maintain and optimize later.

Why this is asked: The interviewer wants to assess your software engineering philosophy.

Breaking it down:
- Readability: maintainable, understandable, easier to debug.
- Performance: speed, memory, scalability.
- Premature optimization is the root of all evil.
- Profile first, then optimize the critical parts.
- Write clear code, use efficient algorithms when natural.

How to structure your spoken answer:
"I prioritize readability and maintainability first. I write clean, well-documented code that is easy to understand. If I suspect performance issues, I profile to confirm, then optimize specific bottlenecks. In most cases, readability doesn't significantly impact performance. If a very performance-critical part is needed, I may sacrifice some readability, but I add comments to explain."

Example:
# Write clean, then use profiler.

Real-world use case: All development.

Common mistakes:
- Premature optimization.
- Writing unreadable code for micro-optimizations.
- Not measuring before optimizing.
- Sacrificing maintainability for marginal gains.

Interview tip: Mention that you use proper data structures and algorithms from the start.

End of Part 8 (Q311–Q366)
